Access All Areas / By Mark Gidley
The Making of Oxygene
The full story of the ground-breaking Oxygene album and how it has been re-imagined for Dolby Atmos
01/Feature
Oxygene - The History
In late 1976, an album was released which re-wrote the rulebook of synthesizers and electronic music and captured the hearts and imagination of the entire world. Composed by the then unknown 28-year-old French artist Jean-Michel Jarre, the album Oxygène broke out of France to become a top 10 album in seven different countries, spending a remarkable 24 weeks in the UK album charts and selling now close to 20 million copies worldwide.
Uniquely, the album consisted of just six tracks, numbered Oxygène Parts 1 through 6, and was entirely instrumental with no acoustic instruments whatsoever, which was unheard of in 1976.
Jean Michel Jarre tells the story of the making of his ground-breaking album in his own words.
"Around 1969, I felt that the world of classical music, the world of rock and other forms of music were a bit narrow and living in their own worlds. I had the feeling it was the right time to explore other fields to perhaps see if a combination of different styles were possible.
“The main difference with electronic music is that you create music in terms of sound, not just notes. It’s something else entirely. When I was a teenager, I always liked processing my guitar or organ or whatever, playing the tape backwards and changing the speed. Then the drummer in my band said, ‘You should meet with these crazy guys at Groupe De Recherches Musicales [GRM] in Paris who are working in French public radio. They’re doing the same kinds of things as you, and they have labs.’
“When I first went there, I was immediately seduced by what they were doing. Electronic music owes a lot to public radio stations - the BBC Radiophonic Workshop, the German lab where Stockhausen was working and, of course, the one in Paris.
"Pierre Schaeffer was one of the most brilliant guys of the century in terms of what he brought to the music world. He created the notion of Musique concrète and influenced me a lot in this direction. Saying that, I felt that contemporary music had got a very elitist attitude - a bit too intellectual vis-à-vis the music and it created barriers between the audience and yourself. I wanted to be much more open to the audience.
"I stayed with Pierre Schaeffer [and Pierre Henry] for three years and I also went to Cologne to work with Stockhausen. He was totally crazy, but a real genius. He considered himself to be made of sound, biologically, so he had a kind of surrealistic approach. Crazy, but realistic at the same time. It was very interesting.
"When I left GRM and through to 1976, I did production work for some rock artists, earning enough to set up a studio in my kitchen. I didn’t have much equipment, though, just a few guitar pedals and my first synthesizer, the VCS3, which looked like a telephone exchange. I realised that using a Revox tape machine to delay the sound that came out of one loudspeaker created a huge sense of space.
“The British-made VCS3 from EMS [Electronic Music Studios, based in London] was created by a genius called Peter Zinovieff. It was a kind of poor European version of the Moog. In those days Europe was not as rich as the States, so the sound was drier. In a sense, it was a kind of punk synthesizer - there was something very harsh and rough about it. I loved it; I still take one on stage with me today.
“At the time, I was obsessed with this idea of creating a bridge between experimentation and pop melody. I liked this kind of challenge, playing around with sound design and integrating melodies. Oxygène was a new start for music with analogue synthesizers"
The album was recorded on a Scully recorder with just eight tracks, and all through a home-made mixer. On top of the VCS3, Jarre had managed to add some other classic equipment including EMS Synthi AKS and ARP 2600 synths, an Eminent String Ensemble, an RMI Harmonic Synthesizer, and Farfisa organ, while an RMI Keyboard Computer was used for some sequencing.
Jarre continues, “I really did it in a kind of home way using what, I think, was one of the first home studios. I had just three or four synthesizers and was using a Scully eight–track and a mixture of Ampex 256 and 3M tape. The whole album was done on just one eight–track and you can hear that in the piece — it’s quite minimalist and I think that contributes to its timelessness.
“Nothing is repeated the same way. Each sound, even if appears to be the same, has a slightly different attack and release, and, as the sequences are being made by hand, by definition they are not the same. Even the sounds of the drum machines were filtered in a very subtle way to give life inside the patterns.
”Michel Geiss was a maintenance technician, at a famous French record company at that time called Barclay and I met him because he was very interested in electronic instruments. He visited my home and we started to experiment. He was teaching me to use the first semi-modular synths I could put my hands on. And then step by step we worked together, Michel mainly as a kind of sound engineer for me.
“For Oxygene, he was working at his record company as a maintenance guy during the day, and he was coming to work with me at night. In those days I probably had the first home studio in France, when the serious music was done in serious recording studios with a window where God was the sound engineer behind the window, and we were just on the other side as musicians. For electronic music you need to match the mixing area with the main studio to record your own sounds.
“So, I had this home studio in a kitchen in the flat I had in Paris, and Michel joined me, using one of the first eight-track tape recorders to record my music. I only had two tapes. So, I had no choice if I was not happy with something to erase what I'd done the previous day, and this is the reason why I have no archive of material from Oxygène. It's such a shame.
“And that is also the reason why if you take the master tape of Oxygène, it is made up of two different tapes. Right in the middle of part one of Oxygène, I had one piece I really liked, where suddenly in the first part you have this kind of brassy, quite epic brassy moment. This is coming from another tape that I recorded another time.
“I thought to myself that it's really matching very well. So, I did a very strong edit, quite dramatic edit, by cutting the two tapes together, even the colours are different. One is pink, one is black. It's totally crazy. And whilst you can't hear the edit, it's quite challenging to cut an eight-track tape like this.
“With so little to work with, I had to be creative. My old Mellotron only had a few working keys, but I still managed to write Oxygene Part II on it. My primitive drum machine - a Korg Mini Pops was the sort of thing people played in pubs, but by using Sellotape I could make it play two preset rhythms at the same time, creating cool beats. Oxygène Part IV, which became iconic, was a mixture of [presets] Slow Rock and Rock, while Part VI combined Rumba and Bossa Nova.
“But when I finished the album, because Oxygène consisted of entirely instrumental music, none of the record companies thought it would work. The only people who took the risk were a small French company called Dreyfus Music. Even after it had become a hit in France, people said it couldn't possibly be a success anywhere else. Then eventually, after it got to the top of the charts in Britain and all over Europe, the critics explained that it was successful because of its originality and because of its use of melody. It's easy to be wise after the event!
“I made the first album back in the days of vinyl, when we were always thinking about sides one and two. The other characteristic of Oxygène is that it has sunny melodies but it’s also quite dark, like the cover artwork.”
Studio engineer and would-be long-time collaborator Michel Geiss, has fond memories of his first encounter with Jean Michel Jarre, recalling, "Oxygene marked the beginning of my collaboration with Jean-Michel. At the time, I was working during the day at a large studio complex in Paris called Studios Barclay. I was employed there as a technician. The recording studios were located on the upper floors, while I worked in the workshop downstairs. My body was downstairs, but my mind was upstairs! I felt strongly attracted to the world of music engineering, although I wasn't particularly interested in the equipment I had been assigned to work with.
"In fact, I found the right balance when I began working with Jean-Michel at his own studio around the same time. Every day, once my working day at Barclay had finished, I would join Jean-Michel in his studio, at his request, to work on his album. We would continue until I had to catch the last train home. I remember him calling me every afternoon to ask if I could come over.
"This was my first real opportunity to work in a studio as a sound and music collaborator, and I thoroughly enjoyed it. Jean-Michel would also occasionally ask for my musical opinion on various matters. I programmed some specific sounds on the ARP 2600, including the main theme sound in Oxygene IV and the sound of the waves in Part VI.
"It was a close working relationship, and I felt that I had finally found my rightful place. It suited me much better than simply being an electronics specialist, which was my background before I met him.
"My main role during the recording of Oxygene was to record Jean-Michel while he played his instruments, as he needed someone to handle the recording process.
"At the time, he was also keen to expand his relatively limited collection of instruments in order to explore new sounds. I suggested that we take a look at the RMI keyboards, which were sold in France by Piano Center in Montreuil. Jean-Michel immediately agreed, saying, 'OK, let's go!'
"When we arrived at the music shop, Jean-Michel discovered the two instruments I had suggested: the RMI Harmonic Synthesizer and the RMI Keyboard Computer. The latter used punched cardboard cards to store its sound information. The Harmonic Synthesizer had a truly distinctive sound and featured an arpeggiator. It would later prove to be a key instrument on the album, particularly on Oxygene IV."
The album’s iconic artwork came about when Jarre was visiting Marquet Gallery in Paris and a piece caught his eye that he was instantly drawn to. A painting of a skull hidden within the Earth. The painting was created by young artist Michel Granger, titled Oxygène. By chance, Granger happened to be in the gallery at the same time and the pair spoke and Jean-Michel bought the painting to use as the album cover and adopted its title.
Michel Granger recalls, “I was having my first major show in Paris. On display was a 30x40cm watercolour called Oxygène and Jean-Michel bought it, telling me he’d love to use it as an album cover. Oxygène was part of a series about the damage being done to our planet. It was quite a violent image for a record sleeve.
“The only change I made was to the background, making it square to fit the album shape. Jean-Michel and I chose the typography and that was that. We were both similar ages and he was very passionate about music and painting. We would have long conversations in the evenings, always at the same pizzeria, then he went home to compose all night.”
Jean Michel Jarre commented on the artwork,"What I like about this picture is that it isn’t necessarily pessimistic. It rather poses the question: where are we going?”
With its iconic title and cover set, and after the album was mixed at Studio Gang by Jean-Pierre Janiaud, an accomplished engineer, Jarre took the album to Francis Dreyfus, owner of the Disques Motors record label, who he had worked for during his songwriting days with Christophe. Dreyfus loved the album and was convinced it could be a success.
Dreyfus pressed 50,000 copies on his own label to sell in France and released the album in December 1976. Uniquely and a brainwave of Dreyfus and Jarre, they offered the album to nationwide hi-fi shops, given the album's innovative use of stereo. The idea being that high-end hi-fi buyers would buy a copy of the album to go with their hardware purchase. By April 1977, sales in France had reached 70,000 copies despite very little promotion. Legend has it that some people returned the LP believing the iconic white noise was a manufacturing defect.
Finding an international label to distribute was harder. Initially no record company was interested in the album, citing no singer, no guitars, no drummer, no proper track titles, no obvious singles, and to round it off, it was French.
Everything changed when Fred Haayen, the new head of Polydor Records in London discovered the record in a French record store and signed it to the label. He was instrumental in persuading BBC Radio 1 to play the album in full the week of release. The only other album to ever receive such an honour was Pink Floyd's Dark Side of the Moon.
The album was mostly slated by the UK's mainstream music press, with the NME calling it “another interminable sonic cruise and infuriatingly derivative.” but the public loved the album and on August 23rd 1977, Oxygène entered the UK album charts at number 19, climbing and remaining in the top 10 for the next 11 weeks. After moving to the United States to lead Polydor USA, Haayen also spearheaded the launch of the album in the US, to achieve official platinum status.
We’ll let Jarre conclude the story, “It’s now sold something like 18 million copies and, no matter what I do, I am defined by Oxygène. But that’s OK. It’s funny. At first, the album was played in hi-fi shops as an example of 'state-of-the-art sound'. I didn’t tell them I made it in my kitchen.
“When you’ve been lucky enough to have some kind of success with your first project, it will always be part of your DNA, no matter what you do afterwards. It’s like the first movie for Tarantino or Kubrick. For me, it’s the same with Oxygène. I love many other things that I’ve done and I’m very proud of them all, but Oxygène is something unique.”
When you’ve been lucky enough to have some kind of success with your first project, it will always be part of your DNA, no matter what you do afterwards. I love many other things that I’ve done and I’m very proud of them all, but Oxygène is something unique.
02/Feature
Oxygene 50th Anniversary
Jean-Michel Jarre will release a 50th anniversary edition of his breakthrough album, Oxygene, on November 20 2026.
Jarre originally released the album in France in 1976, ahead of its international release in 1977, Oxygene helped bring electronic music to a global audience and established Jarre as one of the figureheads of the genre.
At the heart of the anniversary edition is a new 2026 remaster of the original stereo mix, created from the original tapes to restore the full dynamics and character of the recording.
“When I created Oxygene, I was working directly with analogue instruments and tape, and the sound had a particular depth, movement and life of its own," he recalls. "For this anniversary edition, we returned to the original tapes with the aim of preserving that character rather than changing it”.
The release includes a 180g black-vinyl edition, half-speed mastered and cut by Miles Showell at Abbey Road Studios; CD; limited-edition coloured eco-vinyl; digital editions; and a deluxe Blu-ray mediabook featuring Oxygene in Dolby Atmos for the first time, alongside a new 5.1 mix, the 2026 stereo remaster and selected archival material.
03/Feature
Oxygene - Dolby Atmos release
Oxygene has been mixed in Dolby Atmos and 5.1 Surround by David Kosten, Steven Wilson and Jean-Michel Jarre, from the original multi-tracks, with sessions in Paris and London to perfect these immersive mixes.
Speaking about the process, Jean Michel Jarre observed, "It was quite challenging for David because I originally recorded Oxygene on eight tracks, and because you have a lot of things going on on the same track, you have lots of different elements. You can go from a bass to a sound effect to some pads. And David had to separate all this coming from the same original track and after that then the next challenge was to put the different audio objects into space."
David Kosten reflected, "Once the hard work of recreating the original mixes is done, I start to think about how to present the music in three dimensions. Any sound can be placed anywhere so there's a lot of choices to make across an entire album! With Atmos we have speakers in front, to the side, behind and above the listener. It's actually all too easy though to take a coherent and brilliantly mixed original track, and reduce it to a collection of disparate sounds, and lose the coherence and emotional impact of a piece. So the first thing to make sure of is to "do no harm"! But with care and lots of time, you find a way to present the music in a new way that holds together - and potentially even adds a wonderful extra layer of depth, excitement and emotion."
In addition to the Atmos and 5.1 mixes, the blu-ray also features a Miles Showell 2026 remaster of the original 1976 album made from a new transfer of the original stereo tapes (recently unearthed from Jean-Michel Jarre’s archive). This is unlimited audio presented at the highest possible resolution (192 kHz / 24 bit) – the original version has never sounded better, and possibly never will!
Audio streams on the SDE exclusive Oxygène blu-ray:
• Oxygène – 2026 Dolby Atmos Mix (48 kHz / 24 bit)
• Oxygène – 2026 5.1 Mix (48 kHz / 24 bit)
• Oxygène – 1976 Stereo Mix – Unlimited Miles Showell 2026 Remaster (192 kHz / 24 bit)
• Oxygène – Binaural Mix (headphones only) (48 kHz / 24 bit)
Additionally, the blu-ray includes Oxygène (Part IV) original music video, upgraded to HD, and an exclusive 30 minute video interview with Jean-Michel Jarre, David Kosten and Steven Wilson conducted at Abbey Road Studios earlier this year, during the remastering sessions.
The blu-ray is a hardcover deluxe presentation and comes with 48-page booklet featuring essays from music journalists Alexis Petridis (The Guardian, UK) and Alma Rota (Rolling Stone, France) and David Kosten’s notes about mixing in Dolby Atmos (all three in both French and English). It also includes rare images from the forthcoming book Machines: A History of Electronic Music by Jean-Michel Jarre (to be published by Thames & Hudson on 29 October).
The Oxygene blu-ray containing the Dolby Atmos mix is released on 4 December 2026.
04/Interview
David Kosten
Dolby Atmos Mix
How did you first become involved in music production and specifically what sparked your interest in immersive audio and Dolby Atmos?
After making my own music under the name Faultline, (mostly instrumental music, with occasional guest vocalists including Chris Martin, Michael Stipe and The Flaming Lips) I moved into producing and mixing other artists. I've worked with a really wide range of acts including Keane, Bat For Lashes, Everything Everything, Marina and the Diamonds, Enter Shikari and Steven Wilson.
It was a visit to Dolby HQ with Steven, where we had help mixing his The Future Bites album in Dolby Atmos, that sparked my interest in the format and immersive music in general. I was blown away by the fun sonic possibilities, both for my own music which I'd started to create again, and for the acts I was producing. I was also looking for some new avenues to explore and enjoy, after a long period of really intense work as a producer. Once I'd installed an Atmos speaker rig in my studio, I also started to consider the idea that maybe I could work with classic albums, re-imagining them in an immersive format.
My first commercial job doing this was re-mixing Tubular Bells in Atmos for its 50th anniversary re-issue, and since then I've taken on a few more projects - including Thompson Twins, Heaven 17, Propaganda, Paul Young, Fine Young Cannibals, Keane and Mike Oldfield's second album Hergest Ridge.
Were you aware of Jean Michel Jarre’s music and career ahead of working on Oxygene?
The first record I ever bought was the 7" of Oxygène Part 4. I must have been nine years old. I had a little record player with a built-in speaker, and played the single over and over again. The b-side was Oxygène Part 6 - my favourite track on the album. His music became part of my DNA - every synth sound I make is through that prism and early discovery of Jean-Michel, The Human League, John Foxx, Kraftwerk etc in the late 70's. Nine year old me didn't foresee working with JMJ on Oxygène immersive mixes almost 50 years later of course...
How did the opportunity to create a Dolby Atmos version of Oxygene come about?
Early in 2025 I started sending speculative emails to JMJ's (very patient) manager! I knew the 50th anniversary was coming around, and really wanted to be the person JMJ turned to if this dream project became a reality.
In spring 2026, we started having some serious discussions about getting a demo made, to show how this potentially might sound. And then as soon as JMJ heard the work in progress, it was all systems go...
When dealing with such an iconic album, how do you approach the challenge of respecting the original while bringing something completely new to the listener?
I'm a music fan much more than I am a technician - respecting the source material is vital for my own enjoyment of the end result. And in fact, this was a big reason why I got involved in making Atmos rebuilds of classics - I'd just heard too many average, bland or poor mixes! Either where the new version sounded nothing like the original and hadn't had the love and care needed to bring it back to life accurately, or where the immersive mix was barely a step up from stereo, or a mess, and ruined the original intent of the artist. Or all the above!
I spend hours and hours in the studio fine tuning my work to reflect the original release mix, in as much detail as my ears can detect. The balance between instruments, the tone, use of effects and where the sound sits in the stereo spectrum - I try very hard to bring this all back to life from the raw multitrack data, using my production and mixing experience. This part of the process is a real grind, but if you're lucky enough to be playing with iconic, precious cargo like Oxygène, it's not exactly a hardship!
Often I find that the technical limitations of music originally made primarily for vinyl mean that the lowest (and sometimes highest) frequencies on the master tapes never made it through to the listener, so it's fun to bring those back to life. There's some incredible low bass frequencies on the Oxygène multitracks that have probably never been heard before - with digital there are no limitations regarding low frequency reproduction, so that's often a wonderful and satisfying new thing to capture and reveal. The Atmos format is new too of course - hearing the music completely surround you is an astonishing experience for someone used only to stereo - so even this on its own is a wonderful new prize for a fan of the recording.
I am primarily interested in recreating the original mixes and intentions of the artist, so am not trying to make a 'David' version or 'remix' of it at all - each mix I do of a classic really should sound pretty much like the original - but in a brand new 'exploded three dimensional view'.
What original recordings and source material were available to you, and how did you go about reconstructing the album for an immersive environment? How important was it to preserve the original character and sonic identity of the album?
I was given a digital copy of the original 8 track tape multitrack, from 1976. This had all of JMJ's synth parts and drum machines, in isolation on separate tracks, and also included some of the effects he used too, like the echoes.
This is actually incredibly complex, time consuming and delicate work - many weeks of intense listening to try to recreate the spirit, tone and balance of original mixes, using only raw original multitrack materials, and a final stereo mix as a listening reference to recreate the new mixes from.
I have access to every individual sound and effect used on the tapes to make an album. In essence, I listen to a final stereo mix, and then recreate every nuance of that, using the raw data on the multitrack. I balance the individual sounds, add effects, equalisation and stereo positioning to rebuild an album like Oxygène from scratch, before I move onto the immersive / three dimensional part of the process.
Can you describe some of the creative and technical decisions you made when positioning the sounds around the listener? And the technology you are using?
So this is the really fun part of the process! Once the hard work of recreating the original mixes is done, I start to think about how to present the music in three dimensions. Any sound can be placed anywhere so there's a lot of choices to make across an entire album! With Atmos we have speakers in front, to the side, behind and above the listener. It's actually all too easy though to take a coherent and brilliantly mixed original track, and reduce it to a collection of disparate sounds, and lose the coherence and emotional impact of a piece. So the first thing to make sure of is to "do no harm"! But with care and lots of time, you find a way to present the music in a new way that holds together - and potentially even adds a wonderful extra layer of depth, excitement and emotion.
What was the biggest challenge you encountered, and were there any unexpected discoveries in the original recordings?
There were loads of technical challenges - things are never quite as straightforward as one might hope. I had a few missing sounds or parts that were in need of some sneakery to make work. The birdsong at the end of Part 3 for instance - I had to take that from the original stereo tape as it wasn't on the multitrack. But through that limitation came one of my favourite mix ideas - all the sounds at the end of Part 3 disappearing down the hole of the mono centre speaker - and then the birdsong reappearing overhead.
For a music and synth obsessive like me it's just wonderful hearing the individual elements that go to make up an iconic recording. Often things are much simpler than you realise. Or perhaps a part you've known most of your life is revealed as a combination of two or three different sounds. Also, it's a lot easier to hear the hand-played nature of the synths when you hear them in isolation. Loads of wonderful human timing discrepancies and character are there that kind of dissolve when mixed with other sounds.
How closely was Jean-Michel involved, and what was his reaction when he first heard the finished Atmos mix? Did he give you any specific instructions about how certain sounds or musical passages should be treated?
Neither Steven nor myself have ever had this level of input with a mix from the original artist before. It was just wonderful to have Jean-Michel's ideas and changes, and they made a huge positive impact on the end result. In our Paris sessions together we worked for many hours on detailed changes. How the synths moved in the 3D space, the positioning of the sounds, the tone and balance of the music etc. He was involved in every single second of this mix in one way or another. He would make tons of suggestions about how the sounds should sit together, or work in the immersive space. The idea of having the waves in Part 6 crash from front to back rather than side to side was his for instance. We'd play and keep adjusting detail, fine tuning it until we both were happy. He's an incredibly able listener and instinctive mixer. Do meet your heroes: this one was hilariously funny and kind too. He knew how nervous I must have been, especially in our first session, but he put me at ease and allowed me to work at my best.
There was an enormous amount of work to do before we got to a finished piece. But we shared a satisfied look, and were probably both exhausted at the effort it had taken! We were making changes right up until the final moment before we had to deliver all the files.
Did you ever find yourself having to step back and remind yourself that you were working on an album that millions of people already know and love?
No - it's constantly on my mind - it's a truly daunting responsibility! These are important, iconic pieces of our shared culture. There will be fans who've listened to this record every week for 50 years. They want to know that their beloved music is in safe hands, and I really try to make sure I give them as much respect as the music.
Were there any particular moments where Jean-Michel's reaction surprised you or where he asked you to change something?
After our first few minutes in the studio together I knew what level of attention to detail I was going to get from JMJ, and just how much of a unique experience I was in for. He's a fearless and brave creator, and being in the studio with him was of course an astonishing thrill for me as a fan. I loved every minute, and was kind of sad when we got the album over the line, as I'd enjoyed the project so much!
Having demonstrated the mix to audiences, what has been the most memorable reaction you've witnessed?
It's just enormously satisfying to see people so moved by this work. It can be quite overwhelming to hear your favourite music played in a completely new way - so it's a joy to hear about that afterwards. Once the blu ray and streaming versions come out and everyone can experience it, I hope the reaction remains as positive, and all the work and effort we put into Oxygène simply gives people enormous pleasure!
Do you think immersive audio reveals something about the original recording that simply wasn't possible to appreciate through conventional stereo?
Absolutely. It can and should, otherwise there's not a lot of point in all that effort haha! I guess the experience will be different for every listener, but aside from being able to hear more detail and dynamics, weight and space, the most obvious change would be in the power of the immersive experience. It's thrilling and moving in equal amounts.
If you could take another Jean-Michel Jarre album into Dolby Atmos, which one would it be and why?
Hmmm - isn't Équinoxe going to be 50 quite soon....?!
Additional quotations for this article are originally from Electronics & Music Maker (E&MM) June 1982; Prog Magazine – Rob Hughes - December 2016; Louder Magazine 2024; Guardian – Dave Simpson – 16 Oct 2018; Music Radar – Andy Jones - 25 May 2026; Sound-on-Sound – February 2008; Planet Jarre: The Podcast - Matt Berry – September 2018; Fairlight Jarre – Michel Geiss Interview - March 2014; Medium – George Palladev – 24 Jan 2018

Frostbite: A new CPU architecture discovered, designed and verified by AI
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
10:01:41 UTC
19:01:41 KST

Introducing Frostbite: A new CPU architecture discovered, designed, and verified end-to-end by AI
Abstract
In our previous post, Redwood, we showed that given an architecture, our AI systems could implement, verify, and deploy an inference accelerator in record time. However, similar to other scientific domains, we believe that AI systems, given the correct freedom and guardrails, can co-design software and hardware together, making optimizations across the stack and opening a design space so large it was previously inaccessible. We set out to answer the question: “Could our AI systems & tools lead to a novel hardware discovery that radically outperforms human-made designs?”
Today, we're introducing Frostbite, a general-purpose CPU with a new instruction set built from the ground up. Frostbite exploits the properties of interaction nets, namely that every step is local, every dependency is explicit, and every value has exactly one owner, to reach a step-function gain in CPU performance that has long stagnated. On a set of 25 initially chosen workloads, including 15 held out until the final evaluation, we achieved a 4.59x performance-per-watt multiple when compared to a modern, silicon-proven superscalar (dual-issue, in-order) CPU core.
Our AI systems autonomously iterated over the compiler, ISA, architecture, microarchitecture, RTL, verification, and final GDS. When we took a closer look at the discovered architectural primitives, we were surprised to find novel hardware implementations and runtime features that would have otherwise been difficult to architect. In spite of stagnation in CPU core improvement, we were able to take a workload concept all the way to a final GDS, and prove a step-function increase in perf/W and perf/area. We believe that as AI systems become more capable, every class of chip we know today, from datacenter-grade CPUs, to AI accelerators, to networking switches and NICs, will go through similar fundamental architectural shifts.
Introduction to interaction nets
Mainstream programming languages, when compiled for general-purpose CPUs, hide the dependencies between the parts of a program. Any piece of code may read or write any variable through a shared, mutable state, and so modern CPU hardware assumes the worst case and rediscovers, at runtime, which instructions actually depend on each other. Moreover, single-core CPU performance has largely stalled since the early 2000s, pushing people towards large multicore CPUs such as Intel Xeon, AMD Zen, and NVIDIA Vera. This forces CPUs to maintain the illusion that every core sees the same memory, and keeping that illusion consistent and performant requires deep cache hierarchies and coherence protocols that cost area and power, and scale poorly.
Interaction nets1 take a different approach. A program is a graph of nodes, and computation happens by rewriting pairs of nodes that are connected through their ports. Every rewrite only touches a small, local part of the graph. Because each node has exactly one principal port, no two of these active pairs ever overlap, so they can be rewritten in any order, or all at once, and the result is always the same. Interaction nets are also linear: every value is used exactly once, so there is no shared mutable state for the hardware to guard. Languages such as Vine2compile ordinary-looking programs directly to interaction nets. For an excellent introduction to interaction nets and why they suit hardware, we highly recommend Tendrils' “On Interaction Nets.” 3
Fig. 1 shows the difference on a small search problem, placing five queens on a five-by-five board. The sequential program visits one candidate at a time. The interaction net exposes all of its independent work: on average, about twelve interactions are ready to run at every step. Nothing about the program was rewritten to get this parallelism; the dependencies were simply never thrown away.
In theory, this should have two large advantages. The first is multithreading for free: rather than varying levels of coarse and fine-grained parallelism, parallelism comes straight from the program. No threads, locks, or race conditions, and none of the out-of-order machinery a conventional core uses to dig it back out. The second is that, since every value has one owner and every step is local, data can live in small local memories next to where it is used. That removes much of the area and power a conventional chip spends on large caching subsystems, and much of the system-level complexity of keeping those caches coherent.
Process
Frostbite was designed with zero architectural priors. We followed the process below, riding multiple agent swarms along the way.
Architecture exploration and selection
To begin architectural exploration, we had to establish the set of workloads Frostbite should do exceedingly well in. We decided to look at different ways a program could expose its work: many independent pieces at once, rounds of parallel work, long sequential chains, data-dependent branches, and recursion. Under each category, we created a search set and a held-out eval set to evaluate the final Frostbite on, ensuring the design generalized to programs it was never tuned on. The kernels for FFT, radixsort, and LU follow the implementation in the SPLASH-2 benchmark, although 64 times smaller, because at regular sizes they are not suitable for exploratory work.
Later, we added five more practical workloads to the held-out set: a Kalman filter, PCA, finite element analysis, an LU solve, and LZ4 decompression.
Agents synthesized research papers and existing benchmarks, and built Python models to trade off high-level architectural ideas against each other, before narrowing to a single architectural concept. The output of this exploration was a 141-page PDF covering the proposed programming model, the runtime, and the high-level blocks of the chip. This was nowhere near the final architecture, but rather a starting point where all the prior findings, high-level requirements, constraints, experiments, and runtime traits were described. Once the first architectural iteration completed (first RTL generation), this was deleted, since many of the numbers and estimates were well off what was physically possible.
Formal contracts and system modeling
Once the architecture candidate was selected, our system moved on to building the full software model of the chip. Building software models is essential when exploring an architectural space, as they allow fast iteration between the workloads, the ISA, and the abstracted view of the hardware. Whether we build a CPU, GPU, NPU, or networking switch, mastering this iteration loop allows the fastest exploration of the design space within a finite time. To do this repeatedly, we designed a new in-house specification language, ASL, and wrote Frostbite's system model “Arcmodel” in it. ASL supports machine-checkable translations and refinements between RTL, system models, UVM, SVA, and more, all against a formally backed specification. Its power and performance estimates are accurate to within 1–5%, it runs roughly 200x faster than RTL simulation, and it is formally verifiable through “contracts.”
In every silicon program to date, human architects have written many English PDFs before handing off to an implementation team and a verification team. However, natural language cannot capture every detail an implementation needs precisely and unambiguously, and it cannot guarantee that every agent hallucination is caught while the ASIC is being designed and verified. Natural language was never built for this, and in a world where AI writes the specification, we opt for a formal, machine-checkable specification language instead.
ASL can formally describe both high-level, protocol-like behavior, such as out-of-order handling, CSR programming, memory ordering, and coherency, and low-level details, such as pipelines, arbitration mechanisms, and compute transfer functions. This lets the model leave the specification open-ended where that is useful and constrain it exactly where a precise behavior is required. ASL also translates natively to Lean,4 which we use for all theorem proving when checking AI outputs across RTL, UVM, C, SVA, and more. We will discuss ASL in more detail in a future post.
Throughout the entire development of Frostbite, our AI iterates on the Frostbite ISA, Arcmodel, Design, and Verification tightly together, maintaining the ASL Contract as the source of truth. We've found that having a single, provable source of truth prevents a large class of bugs, silly mistakes, and reward-hacking instances when deploying large agent swarms. Ultimately, this common language allows the models to work across the stack nimbly, reflecting the changes propagated from the workload down to the final hardware. By the end, our Frostbite ASL lowered down to 800k+ lines of Lean.
RTL and alignment
Because ASL is a formally backed specification, designing and verifying the RTL derived from it is the fastest part of the entire Frostbite process. Arcmodel, however, is not physically aware, so its power and performance estimates at first diverge from the true numbers of the timing-converged, synthesis-clean RTL, in the worst case by almost 250%.
Before continuing the ASL autoresearch, one of our systems recalibrates Arcmodel against the RTL. This is a fully autonomous pipeline. It first identifies the power hotspots in the RTL and traces them down to key counters in the formal specification, such as SRAM reads and writes, operation counts, flits sent and received, credit returns, and frame misses. The model may then write any scalar Python function over those counters to predict power, and it repeats this process until the RTL and Arcmodel agree to within 5%.
We find this style of non-gradient learning extremely powerful. Once this first physical alignment has been applied, later iterations are much faster. These are, of course, still pre-silicon power numbers, and over multiple tapeout loops, ASL models can be correlated to true post-silicon, production power numbers.
By aligning the Arcmodel and RTL so closely, the continued swarms were able to explore and exploit much faster. The full Arcmodel chip simulations are much faster than the RTL (660x faster), which led to many cross-stack optimizations. For instance, our AI systems discovered very fine-grained clock gating tricks, and adjusted the software runtime accordingly to accommodate them in a matter of hours. Such a cross-stack optimization is near-impossible to execute on in a traditional ASIC flow.
Contracts, Arcmodel, RTL, and power, iterated together
Once Arcmodel and the RTL agreed, the original Python models and the architecture PDF were deleted, and the ASL contracts became the only specification of the chip. From then on, contracts, Arcmodel, RTL, and power moved forward together. Swarms of agents proposed architecture revisions in parallel worktrees; each change was checked against the contracts, modeled in Arcmodel, implemented in RTL, and measured for power, and then either kept or dropped. Across four searches and a final round of ablations, the swarm tried 58 ideas and kept 37 of them, from clock gates on a running tile to smaller L3 tables and one shared expander.
Architectural novelty
The architecture in full detail would warrant its own post. Instead, we choose to focus here on the features that we considered either novel or creative and highlight the capability of our systems to push the frontiers of hardware design.
Programs for Frostbite are written in Vine,2 which is a fully fledged programming language with syntax similar to Rust. A Vine program is compiled to Ivy, an intermediate representation (IR) that encodes the interaction net structure. The optimizer also tries to apply as many of the graph reduction rules as it can, so the final Ivy net, as it is called, has a (mostly) fixed shape, composed of three primitives: data, computation nodes, and control nodes. However, the runtime of this net is still very dynamic, because unlike the typical computation graph, where the parallelism is encoded in the structure and pipelined over time, the parallelism has to be derived at runtime from the state and pipelined spatially. The initial naïve implementations were slow, so our agents had to get creative.
The generated ISA follows from this set of constraints. A lot of it is expected: wide bundles, hardware loop counters, and streams. We found the dynamic features more interesting, particularly operations to take, peek, and copy values from other parts of the net that would be running in parallel. The tiles then come alive, communicating across space.
Emergent property: stealing work
Because the parallelism is spread over space, there is no central scheduler. Instead, we saw the agents design these elegant rules for propagating the work. A tile follows three rules: it runs its own work first, meaning work in its input ports. If those are empty, it takes work from a neighboring tile that has work to spare. If no such tile is found, it can just go inactive and save power.
The agents demonstrated remarkable sophistication in how they implemented this. Below, we have the hardware for a single Frostbite tile:
A lot of them should be familiar. We are interested in the RUNTIME domain that is responsible for the work-stealing behavior described above.
The compiler breaks code into short sections that run uninterrupted once their inputs are ready. These are run in the engine and are mostly organized like traditional computation through the front end. Whenever a loop or branch splits into parallel pieces whose sizes aren't known ahead of time, the running section parks whatever comes after into a small record in local memory, the frame manager. It then drops the new tasks onto a hardware queue called the work queue and picks up the newest one itself. Idle neighbors are then free to pull the remaining tasks over the network. As tasks finish anywhere on the chip in any order, their return packets tick down the parked record's counter and automatically wake the next section the instant the last piece arrives.
This is what it looks like in hardware:
We can think of each “task” as the current board, one level down, with one more queen. Tile 0 has to queue three tasks for all the possible queen configurations before it can finish the present board state. It's free, so it takes the newest task (from itself) and runs it. Meanwhile, its neighbors have gotten the other ones, except for Tile 5, which goes back to sleep.
This behavior was never specified by any human. We can directly trace it back to necessity on specific regressions and hill-climbing performance metrics.
Results
We synthesized Frostbite on a 7 nm process, reaching 800 MHz timing at synthesis and 500 MHz in the final GDS. Each Frostbite quad (a two-by-two grid of tiles) paired with four L3 nodes is 0.792 mm². We benchmark one Quad+L3 cluster against a single core of a modern superscalar (dual-issue, in-order) CPU, which we refer to as TCPU (“Traditional CPU”). This TCPU is 0.895 mm² at the same process.†
We benchmarked on a wide set of workloads, from dense, parallel kernels such as gemm and conv2d, to irregular, branching programs such as JSON parsing, Huffman coding, k-d trees, and recursive Fibonacci. A search set of workloads drove all architectural optimization, ISA design, RTL, and verification, and the chip was evaluated on both the 10 search-set workloads and the 15 held out, including the five practical workloads added later (Fig. 3).
For a fair comparison, we chose to first measure “single-core” performance by benchmarking one Frostbite quad against the TCPU, since they have similar area. Across all the workloads, we average an 8.77x performance-per-area, and across all 25 workloads, search set and held out together, Frostbite reaches a geometric mean of 4.59x the TCPU's performance-per-watt: 6.02x on the 10 search-set workloads, and 3.83x on the 15 held-out workloads it was never tuned on. As expected, the architecture does most strongly on highly parallelizable tasks, but it also outperforms the TCPU on branching, dynamic code.
The energy breakdown shows where the savings come from. Leaving out clock distribution (49% of TCPU energy, 41% of a Frostbite quad's), the TCPU spends 57% of its energy on memory and 27% on fetching, decoding, and issuing instructions. Frostbite cuts memory to 38% and fetch, decode, and issue to just 6%. In exchange, it spends 26% on its on-chip network and scheduler, the cost of sharing work and moving messages between tiles. What's more exciting, however, is that as we scale the chip to a four-by-four array (four quads), the power distribution is relatively the same. In a general-purpose multi-core CPU arrangement, scaling the number of cores requires adding heavy coherency mechanisms and large caches, whereas when running interaction nets, the same data locality rules always apply, and more cores do not increase system complexity. This is illustrated below. In the coming months, we'll be benchmarking Frostbite mesh-like configurations against multi-core “TCPU” arrangements with large caches and larger, wider, out-of-order CPUs, over a larger set of industry benchmarks such as SPEC and PARSEC.
What comes next
While interaction nets have been around for more than 35 years, their production value has never been fully clear. Historically, there has always been a risk of exploring out-of-distribution architectures, for the time, investment, and labor required to come to conviction. We believe AI fundamentally changes these economics. In the search for higher performance and lower power, extreme co-design of workloads, ISA, and hardware will continue to be demanded. With large agent swarms, sophisticated search-space techniques, precise guardrails, formal verification, and faster EDA tools, AI systems will be able to discover and build novel accelerators at a quality that exceeds human cognition. While there is much more to do, we feel it is the right time to share with the world what the future of autonomous chip design could look like. We plan to continue scaling development on Frostbite, including but not limited to:
- Deliver a software playground for anybody to run a program on Frostbite, hosted on FPGAs.
- Build a preliminary operating system capable of managing hardware resources, multiple programs, virtualization, and more.
- Scale swarms to bring Frostbite to higher performance-per-watt, and benchmark it on a much larger set of workloads against a broader set of CPUs.
- Release the RTL and ISA for Frostbite.
We are having a lot of fun!
Notes
†For legal reasons, we cannot disclose the exact CPU.
References
- 1.Lafont, Y. (1990). “Interaction Nets.” Proceedings of the 17th ACM SIGPLAN-SIGACT Symposium on Principles of Programming Languages (POPL ’90), 95–108. doi:10.1145/96709.96718
- 2.The Vine programming language. vine.dev
- 3.Tendrils (2026). “On Interaction Nets.” tendrils.co/blog/2026-04-26/on-inets
- 4.de Moura, L., and Ullrich, S. (2021). “The Lean 4 Theorem Prover and Programming Language.” Automated Deduction – CADE 28, LNCS 12699, 625–635. doi:10.1007/978-3-030-79876-5_37

If your edge depends on the silicon underneath it, whether you're an AI lab, a fabless chip company, an OEM, a defense prime, or ASIC design partner, we want to hear from you.

Across four searches and a final round of ablations, the swarm tried 58 ideas and kept 37 of them, from clock gates on a running tile to smaller L3 tables and one shared expander.
Architectural novelty
The architecture in full detail would warrant its own post. Instead, we choose to focus here on the features that we considered either novel or creative and highlight the capability of our systems to push the frontiers of hardware design.
Programs for Frostbite are written in Vine,2 which is a fully fledged programming language with syntax similar to Rust. A Vine program is compiled to Ivy, an intermediate representation (IR) that encodes the interaction net structure. The optimizer also tries to apply as many of the graph reduction rules as it can, so the final Ivy net, as it is called, has a (mostly) fixed shape, composed of three primitives: data, computation nodes, and control nodes. However, the runtime of this net is still very dynamic, because unlike the typical computation graph, where the parallelism is encoded in the structure and pipelined over time, the parallelism has to be derived at runtime from the state and pipelined spatially. The initial naïve implementations were slow, so our agents had to get creative.
The generated ISA follows from this set of constraints. A lot of it is expected: wide bundles, hardware loop counters, and streams. We found the dynamic features more interesting, particularly operations to take, peek, and copy values from other parts of the net that would be running in parallel. The tiles then come alive, communicating across space.
Emergent property: stealing work
Because the parallelism is spread over space, there is no central scheduler. Instead, we saw the agents design these elegant rules for propagating the work. A tile follows three rules: it runs its own work first, meaning work in its input ports. If those are empty, it takes work from a neighboring tile that has work to spare. If no such tile is found, it can just go inactive and save power.
The agents demonstrated remarkable sophistication in how they implemented this. Below, we have the hardware for a single Frostbite tile:
A lot of them should be familiar. We are interested in the RUNTIME domain that is responsible for the work-stealing behavior described above.
The compiler breaks code into short sections that run uninterrupted once their inputs are ready. These are run in the engine and are mostly organized like traditional computation through the front end. Whenever a loop or branch splits into parallel pieces whose sizes aren't known ahead of time, the running section parks whatever comes after into a small record in local memory, the frame manager. It then drops the new tasks onto a hardware queue called the work queue and picks up the newest one itself. Idle neighbors are then free to pull the remaining tasks over the network. As tasks finish anywhere on the chip in any order, their return packets tick down the parked record's counter and automatically wake the next section the instant the last piece arrives.
This is what it looks like in hardware:
We can think of each “task” as the current board, one level down, with one more queen. Tile 0 has to queue three tasks for all the possible queen configurations before it can finish the present board state. It's free, so it takes the newest task (from itself) and runs it. Meanwhile, its neighbors have gotten the other ones, except for Tile 5, which goes back to sleep.
This behavior was never specified by any human. We can directly trace it back to necessity on specific regressions and hill-climbing performance metrics.
Results
We synthesized Frostbite on a 7 nm process, reaching 800 MHz timing at synthesis and 500 MHz in the final GDS. Each Frostbite quad (a two-by-two grid of tiles) paired with four L3 nodes is 0.792 mm². We benchmark one Quad+L3 cluster against a single core of a modern superscalar (dual-issue, in-order) CPU, which we refer to as TCPU (“Traditional CPU”). This TCPU is 0.895 mm² at the same process.†
We benchmarked on a wide set of workloads, from dense, parallel kernels such as gemm and conv2d, to irregular, branching programs such as JSON parsing, Huffman coding, k-d trees, and recursive Fibonacci. A search set of workloads drove all architectural optimization, ISA design, RTL, and verification, and the chip was evaluated on both the 10 search-set workloads and the 15 held out, including the five practical workloads added later (Fig. 3).
For a fair comparison, we chose to first measure “single-core” performance by benchmarking one Frostbite quad against the TCPU, since they have similar area. Across all the workloads, we average an 8.77x performance-per-area, and across all 25 workloads, search set and held out together, Frostbite reaches a geometric mean of 4.59x the TCPU's performance-per-watt: 6.02x on the 10 search-set workloads, and 3.83x on the 15 held-out workloads it was never tuned on. As expected, the architecture does most strongly on highly parallelizable tasks, but it also outperforms the TCPU on branching, dynamic code.
The energy breakdown shows where the savings come from. Leaving out clock distribution (49% of TCPU energy, 41% of a Frostbite quad's), the TCPU spends 57% of its energy on memory and 27% on fetching, decoding, and issuing instructions. Frostbite cuts memory to 38% and fetch, decode, and issue to just 6%. In exchange, it spends 26% on its on-chip network and scheduler, the cost of sharing work and moving messages between tiles. What's more exciting, however, is that as we scale the chip to a four-by-four array (four quads), the power distribution is relatively the same. In a general-purpose multi-core CPU arrangement, scaling the number of cores requires adding heavy coherency mechanisms and large caches, whereas when running interaction nets, the same data locality rules always apply, and more cores do not increase system complexity. This is illustrated below. In the coming months, we'll be benchmarking Frostbite mesh-like configurations against multi-core “TCPU” arrangements with large caches and larger, wider, out-of-order CPUs, over a larger set of industry benchmarks such as SPEC and PARSEC.
What comes next
While interaction nets have been around for more than 35 years, their production value has never been fully clear. Historically, there has always been a risk of exploring out-of-distribution architectures, for the time, investment, and labor required to come to conviction. We believe AI fundamentally changes these economics. In the search for higher performance and lower power, extreme co-design of workloads, ISA, and hardware will continue to be demanded. With large agent swarms, sophisticated search-space techniques, precise guardrails, formal verification, and faster EDA tools, AI systems will be able to discover and build novel accelerators at a quality that exceeds human cognition. While there is much more to do, we feel it is the right time to share with the world what the future of autonomous chip design could look like. We plan to continue scaling development on Frostbite, including but not limited to:
- Deliver a software playground for anybody to run a program on Frostbite, hosted on FPGAs.
- Build a preliminary operating system capable of managing hardware resources, multiple programs, virtualization, and more.
- Scale swarms to bring Frostbite to higher performance-per-watt, and benchmark it on a much larger set of workloads against a broader set of CPUs.
- Release the RTL and ISA for Frostbite.
We are having a lot of fun!
Notes
†For legal reasons, we cannot disclose the exact CPU.
References
- 1.Lafont, Y. (1990). “Interaction Nets.” Proceedings of the 17th ACM SIGPLAN-SIGACT Symposium on Principles of Programming Languages (POPL ’90), 95–108. doi:10.1145/96709.96718
- 2.The Vine programming language. vine.dev
- 3.Tendrils (2026). “On Interaction Nets.” tendrils.co/blog/2026-04-26/on-inets
- 4.de Moura, L., and Ullrich, S. (2021). “The Lean 4 Theorem Prover and Programming Language.” Automated Deduction – CADE 28, LNCS 12699, 625–635. doi:10.1007/978-3-030-79876-5_37
Partner with us
If your edge depends on the silicon underneath it, whether you're an AI lab, a fabless chip company, an OEM, a defense prime, or ASIC design partner, we want to hear from you.

UK fridges secured from hackers in secret nationwide operation
United Kingdom
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
09:52:20 GMT
18:52:20 KST

Tens of thousands of UK fridges secured from hackers in secret nationwide operation
- Published
A behind the scenes cyber-security operation has taken place to secure tens of thousands of fridges and freezers at major UK supermarkets, warehouses, restaurants, petrol stations and stadiums from hackers, the BBC has learned.
Recent waves of cyber-attacks in Europe have seen tonnes of food spoiled at hundreds of stores, causing millions of pounds of damage.
France has blamed Iran for one of the waves of attacks that impacted its supermarkets, a claim Tehran denies.
It was thought the UK had largely escaped such hacks as the British refrigeration industry relies on different devices but there was in fact a potential vulnerability the sector has been rushing to fix.
In late October, security researchers discovered a major flaw that, if found by hackers, could have led to even worse damage to the UK.
Since then, researchers, IT teams and the National Cyber Security Centre have been working around the clock to warn dozens of companies.
The BBC has not reported on the large-scale, secretive operation until now to reduce the chances of hackers causing harm if they found the vulnerability.
Tech Life: The everyday tech at risk of hacks
As Europe sees a wave of cyber-attacks on commercial fridges, we investigate how everyday devices are vulnerable to basic attacks using easily-available tools.
Thousands of fridges taken offline
The security threat was discovered by staff at refrigeration consultants Omega Solutions - it was then verified and expanded upon by experts at Pen Test Partners who helped raise the alarm.
The National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC), the Department for Environment, Food & Rural Affairs (Defra) and Food Standards Agency (FSA) were then involved in the campaign to plug the security holes.
They held industry conference calls and tasked teams to contact firms at risk.
Thousands of devices controlling tens of thousands of refrigeration devices were taken off the open internet either remotely or, in some cases, physically - with staff ordered to remove internet cables and monitor temperatures manually.
BBC news has learnt most major supermarkets have been forced to divert IT teams to address the risks, with a worker at one large chain saying they took hundreds of devices offline every day over the course of a week.
"It did feel like a race against time as we were expecting another wave of these fridge hacks to come," said the person, who did not want to be publicly named.
"From a business perspective it could have been potentially devastating with business interruption and supply chain problems. Our warehouses have millions of pounds worth of food that could have been spoiled if any hackers found out about this method of attack," they added.
There have been three waves of hacks on refrigeration temperature controllers so far this year:
- In August, fridges at a small number of US military bases were impacted
- On 11 and 12 October hundreds of supermarkets and food processing sites in France were hit when Danfoss temperature control systems were targeted
- On 1 and 2 November dozens of supermarkets across seven European countries were hit, with Belgium and Luxembourg the worst affected. A small number of blood bank sites in France were also impacted
The waves of attacks prompted an expert at Omega Solutions to examine the security of the most popular refrigeration controllers in the UK, made by British firm Resource Data Management (RDM).
RDM disputes the critical nature of the vulnerabilities exposed but has been praised for its professional response to address the concerns, the BBC understands.
As well as supermarkets, multiple other areas of UK food and beverage industry were also warned to take action about the risks.
These include convenience stores, petrol stations, restaurants and fast food chains, sports arenas, hotels, food processing plants and cold storage sites.
"These popular controllers had a similar weak password problem to the Danfoss and other controllers that were attacked so successfully in previous hacks so we had to get the message out as soon as possible and keep it secret," said Ken Munro of Pen Test Partners.
"There is so much extra attention on these ubiquitous bits of kit now that it was likely a matter of days until criminals or nation state hackers figured it out," he added.
RDM, though, says comparisons with the units attacked in mainland Europe are not fair because hacking their fridges and freezers would have involved getting hold of a piece of hardware.
The ethical hackers say this would have been achievable as the hardware in question can be purchased cheaply online on sites like eBay.
"This was a vulnerability identified in a test environment," said Ged Evans, CEO of RDM.
He said the test "went beyond" what such exercises usually involved, as it "included dismantling our hardware".
"In any event we have acted at pace to address this issue and have already deployed first patch releases and have been in proactive contact with our customers to flag any issues and recommend mitigatory steps," he added.
RDM confirmed that there was no sign of any malicious activity on the controllers and that it continued to work with the NCSC and other authorities to monitor the situation.
Get in touch
What are your thoughts on this topic? Let us know

If you can't explain something in simple terms, you don't understand it (2017)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
09:43:19 UTC
18:43:19 KST

If you can’t explain something in simple terms, you don’t understand it
In the early 1960s, Richard Feynman gave a series of undergraduate lectures that were collected into a book called the Feynman Lectures on Physics. Absent from the book was a lecture Feynman gave on planetary motion, but a later finding of the notes enabled David Goodstein, a colleague of Feynman’s, to write a book about it: Feynman’s Lost Lecture. From an excerpt of the book published in a 1996 issue of Caltech’s Engineering & Science magazine:
Feynman was a truly great teacher. He prided himself on being able to devise ways to explain even the most profound ideas to beginning students. Once, I said to him, “Dick, explain to me, so that I can understand it, why spin one-half particles obey Fermi-Dirac statistics.” Sizing up his audience perfectly, Feynman said, “I’ll prepare a freshman lecture on it.” But he came back a few days later to say, “I couldn’t do it. I couldn’t reduce it to the freshman level. That means we don’t really understand it.”
John Gruber writes the simple explanations are the goal at Apple as well:
Engineers are expected to be able to explain a complex technology or product in simple, easily-understood terms not because the executive needs it explained simply to understand it, but as proof that the engineer understands it completely.
Feynman was well known for simple explanations of scientific concepts that result a in deeper understanding of the subject matter: e.g. see Feynman explaining how fire is stored sunshine, rubber bands, how trains go around curves, and magnets. Critically, he’s also not shy about admitting when he doesn’t understand something…or, alternately, when scientists as a group don’t understand something. There’s the spin anecdote above and of his explanation of magnets, he says:
I really can’t do a good job, any job, of explaining magnetic force in terms of something else you’re more familiar with, because I don’t understand it in terms of anything else you’re more familiar with.
Feynman was also quoted as saying:
I think I can safely say that nobody understands quantum mechanics.
Pretty interesting thing to hear from a guy who won a Nobel Prize for explaining quantum mechanics better than anyone ever had before. Even when he died in 1988 at the end of a long and fruitful careeer, a note at the top of his blackboard read:
What I cannot create, I do not understand.

If you can't explain something in simple terms, you don't understand it (2017)

2026-11-20

If you can’t explain something in simple terms, you don’t understand it

Signal Is Serious About Protecting Your Privacy
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
09:42:16 UTC
18:42:16 KST

Privacy is our mission; it’s why we exist, and we do everything we can to protect it.
This means that we are restricting the latest AI agents built into mobile operating systems from potentially capturing and storing your Signal data.
We’ve already had to do this once with Windows. Last year, we used screenshot blocking to prevent Microsoft’s Recall AI from taking and storing screenshots of your Signal messages on Windows 11 operating systems by default.1 We will now be taking similar measures to restrict agentic systems built into iOS 27 and on Android versions where we can detect an AI assistant attempting to read your Signal screen.2
The problem is similar to Microsoft Recall on Windows: Google and Apple are integrating agentic capabilities like Gemini Intelligence with Google Gemini and Onscreen Awareness with Siri AI deep into their operating systems. This is a significant move. Google (Android), Apple (iOS/macOS), and Microsoft (Windows) produce the three major consumer operating systems (OS), which comprise the environment that third-party developers – like Signal – build their apps to function and operate within. To put it differently, these three operating systems are the oceans in which Signal, and other applications, must swim; they dictate the conditions of life for the application ecosystem. Right now, these agentic AI integrations are changing this environment in a way that’s making it increasingly hostile to privacy. This is an existential threat to Signal, and our ability to responsibly uphold our mission.
In order to gain access to information about you, these OS AI agents “scan” the information that’s on your screen. In doing so, your Signal data is captured, then made available to these agentic systems in order to allow them to answer your questions and do your bidding.
The net result is that these AI capabilities in the operating system are undermining Signal’s ability to independently guarantee privacy. For over a decade, this guarantee has been backed by our tried and trusted open source code base and our formally verified encryption protocol. These have been scrutinized by some of the best security experts and hackers across the world, and shown to be robust. Now, in service of AI agentic capabilities, your sensitive data is being captured, stored, and made accessible to whatever local database or remote server an OS-level AI agent is using at that moment. All of this is outside of Signal’s control once the data has been captured, and imperils the privacy of all the participants in a conversation.
So, we are blocking such capture by preventing screenshots in the app on iOS 27 and when AI assistants attempt to capture screen data on Android.2
People who want the ability to take screenshots can disable this protection within the Signal app’s settings. However, doing so will potentially expose your (and your contacts’) Signal content to these AI agents, and break our privacy guarantees. Our remedy is also incomplete – while we are doing everything we can, restricting screenshots does not address other more sneaky ways AI agents could potentially access application data, such as retrieving content already present on a user’s device via the filesystem.
We want to be very clear: it is a serious problem that Signal – and application developers beyond – lack robust tools to prevent AI agents from undermining application-level privacy. Even where we are able to detect and block AI data access, Google, Apple, Microsoft, and others do not provide ways for developers to easily restrict such AI data access without making drastic compromises to the core user experience. This is also the reason we are currently unable to implement these protections on macOS.3
We are also struggling with a significant lack of documentation about how these AI agents actually work. Over the past few months, we’ve spent considerable developer time and energy doing forensic investigations across Android and iOS in an attempt to learn what is actually happening when these AI systems access application data. Without better documentation and clear technical specifications, it will be increasingly difficult for Signal to provide robust privacy and security to the many millions of people who rely on us.
We continue to call on the major consumer operating system companies – Apple, Google, and Microsoft – to provide simple technical tools and accurate documentation that give developers like Signal ways to prevent AI agents from accessing sensitive application data. In particular, we are calling on these companies to provide two common-sense remedies that can protect the privacy so many millions of people rely on:
- Developers should have an easy and well-documented way to opt their app out of AI data access and processing, including (but not limited to) screen reading, filesystem access, notifications, APIs, and other OS frameworks, all without undermining the core utility of an application.
- Operating system companies should create better documentation and technical specifications around how these AI agents function, particularly regarding data access and processing. Confidential computing approaches are not a complete solution, and do not address these core concerns. In particular, these approaches still leave the power to determine the extent of privacy guarantees (if any) in the hands of these providers.
We also see a growing need for tools that can prevent third-party agents, like Meta’s Muse, from accessing application data using the deep permissions that users are encouraged to grant them during setup. In many cases these agents are leveraging accessibility settings that were meant to empower actual people to get the most out of their apps and services, not to act as a skeleton key that provides access to AI agents.
In addition to taking these technical measures to block capture, we are engaging in conversations across the ecosystem to make the stakes clear. There is a lot of work to be done in the industry to improve the ways that AI agents operate in the operating system, and how and whether they capture and process private data. We are cautiously optimistic that some major OS providers, like Google and Apple, are engaging with us on how these systems should better interface with privacy-sensitive apps like Signal. We hope to have more to share on this topic soon, and that these companies recognize the danger to the ecosystem and work with us and others to create standardized approaches that meaningfully protect privacy across the industry.
Finally, we want to acknowledge that we are not the only ones recognizing these perils, and we are grateful to our allies for pushing for similar changes. We all need to recognize that the current integration of AI agents into the operating system, without these common-sense remedies, represents an existential threat to privacy at the application layer, and the ability of independent developers like Signal to protect your sensitive data. Signal is the canary in the coal mine, but this affects the entire ecosystem and should galvanize anyone who cares about these fundamental rights.
Signal Technology Foundation is the nonprofit home of the Signal app, which is trusted by millions around the world. We protect free expression and enable secure global communication through open source privacy technology. Signal Technology Foundation is a US 501(c)(3) tax-exempt organization.
- Microsoft Recall captures your screen every few seconds, extracts text from screenshots via optical character recognition, then populates a searchable on-device database with this personal data in order to allow the AI system to query it – potentially exposing and retaining your Signal data indefinitely, including disappearing messages. ↩
- There are multiple versions of Android beyond the Google-provided distributions on Pixel devices. For many vendors, we don’t have a reliable way to detect when a screenshot was attempted if screenshots are already disabled. Rather than choose a poor user experience (where screenshots simply stop working, confusing users), we decided to instead focus on detecting when an AI assistant attempts to access the screen. We automatically enable the “Screen security” setting in Signal Android when this type of access is detected. ↩ ↩2
- Careful readers will note that in addition to not being able to cover all Android devices, we are also not blocking Siri AI on macOS. This is because we cannot reliably block screenshots on macOS. Faced with a lack of clearly documented APIs that allow us to implement screenshot protection, we do not have the tools available that let us do the job. While protections against continuous screen capture exist on macOS, we cannot apply them to screenshots (which is how most agentic systems access data from applications like Signal). Trying to implement screenshot blocking on macOS has revealed to us a minefield of bewildering decisions that place user privacy at risk, including APIs that have been deprecated in favor of screen-protection solutions that only work for streaming media and other DRM-protected content. This is something we hope Apple remedies urgently. For now, the only recommendation we have for macOS 27 users is to add Signal to the Excluded Apps section in System Settings > Siri to protect message content from proactive access by Siri AI. ↩
