Jujutsu Primer
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
05:46:01 UTC
14:46:01 KST

Why try Jujutsu?
jj provides primitives for version control that are simple to learn, but elegantly compose into powerful workflows. Features like automatic rebase and native undo soften the sharp edges of Git. Eventually, you’ll organically discover new powerful workflows that would be too cumbersome to attempt with most other VCS tools.
If you work in a Git repository, you can try it today. Jujutsu and Git can be used side-by-side in the same repository, so you can try out jj on a git repo on your own, without fully ejecting from git.
Personally, I think jj considerably boosts my effectiveness, because it allows me to keep VCS bookkeeping out of my working memory. Automatic rebasing also makes stacked diff workflows quite easy, and reduces thrashing of my working memory. It’s also just a fun tool to use.
Installing jj
Official installation instructions are here, but here are a few common methods:
# macOS
# Arch Linux
# NixOS (ideally from nixpkgs-unstable)
# From source
# (Requires a recent Rust and ~/.cargo/bin on your PATH)
Setup and configuration
In an existing git repository, run:
From here on, jj and git repo state will be synchronized automatically.
To set your user details globally, run commands like these:
You can edit jj’s TOML configuration for your user in an editor with:
In addition to the --user scope, jj config also has --repo and --workspace scopes. View the merged config with:
Commits and changes
A commit, also called a revision, is essentially the same thing as a git commit. It represents a snapshot of repository state that descends from one or more parent repository snapshots. It is often visualized as a diff against a singular parent commit, or in the case of a merge commit, the diff against multiple parent commits once merged.
When using a Git repo backend, the jj commit ID is the same as the git commit ID: a hash formatted as a hex string. In the output of jj log below, the hex hashes 2bea5e3d and 849165bc on the right are commit IDs.
$ 
@  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:17:12 849165bc
│  (empty) (no description set)
◆  royxmykx ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:10:46 main 2bea5e3d
│  Adjust config
~
The IDs on the left hand side (royxmykx and kpqxywon) are change IDs, which are stable identifiers for logical changes as they physically evolve over time. We’ll discuss these more in the next section.
Commit IDs and change IDs can be referenced in shorthand with an unambiguous (unique) prefix. jj helpfully highlights the shortest unique prefix of most IDs to make it easy to type them manually. For example,
instead of copying and pasting kpqxywon, we can type in a prefix, down to the shortest unique prefix k.
We can inspect the commit 2bea5e3d with jj show, using its short prefix 2.
$ 
Commit ID: 2bea5e3d1992a60ba9ee52c6ce37c55adba9e8fb
Change ID: royxmykxtrkrqppotnrvutxlvrvqyxmy
Bookmarks: main main@git main@origin
Author   : Ethan Lowman <ethan@example.com> (2026-11-15 12:10:46)
Committer: Ethan Lowman <ethan@example.com> (2026-11-15 12:10:46)
    Adjust config
diff --git a/config.py b/config.py
index 7366a4e31d..174ffea06b 100644
--- a/config.py
+++ b/config.py
@@ -1,1 +1,1 @@
-DEFAULT_LIMIT = 10
+DEFAULT_LIMIT = 20
Many folks configure a custom diff formatter, like Delta. See configuration docs if you’re interested.
Creating & evolving changes
Let’s take another look at our commit graph:
$ 
@  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:17:12 849165bc
│  (empty) (no description set)
◆  royxmykx ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:10:46 main 2bea5e3d
│  Adjust config
~
On commit 849165bc, you’ll see the symbol @ on the graph node. This represents a snapshot of our working copy, or the code we have actually checked out in our local directory. We can inspect this special commit pointer in a variety of ways:
jj show shows commit details and a diff, which is empty here.
$ 
Commit ID: 849165bca19639dbd6ccb53e8c1340e78c36d59a
Change ID: kpqxywonksrltlrkyqukqqsxpltznztk
Author   : Ethan Lowman <ethan@example.com> (2026-11-15 12:17:12)
Committer: Ethan Lowman <ethan@example.com> (2026-11-15 12:17:12)
    (no description set)
jj status shows repo state with diff stats.
$ 
The working copy has no changes.
Working copy  (@) : kpqxywon 849165bc (empty) (no description set)
Parent commit (@-): royxmykx 2bea5e3d main | Adjust config
jj diff works much like git diff. Its output is empty here, since there are no changes in the current working copy relative to the parent commit.
$ 
This output isn’t very interesting, since @ has no changes. Let’s make an example change to see what happens. If we edit main.py and rerun jj show, we see this:
$ 
Commit ID: 66b59f20c534705cfb753c722222290e9185b22c
Change ID: kpqxywonksrltlrkyqukqqsxpltznztk
Author   : Ethan Lowman <ethan@example.com> (2026-11-15 12:26:43)
Committer: Ethan Lowman <ethan@example.com> (2026-11-15 12:26:43)
    (no description set)
diff --git a/main.py b/main.py
new file mode 100644
index 0000000000..c74cd2e0e0
--- /dev/null
+++ b/main.py
@@ -0,0 +1,2 @@ def my_new_feature():
+def my_new_feature():
+  pass
$ 
@  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:26:43 66b59f20
│  (no description set)
◆  royxmykx ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:10:46 main 2bea5e3d
│  Adjust config
~
You’ll notice several things have changed. The working copy has been automatically committed, and @ is no longer marked (empty). The new commit ID for @ is 66b59f20, since we’ve added changes, but the change ID for 66b59f20 is still kpqxywon. jj has no staging area like in git, so we don’t have to manually add files. When we run a jj command to inspect or modify repo state after editing files, jj automatically snapshots the working copy as a new physical commit for the relevant logical change.
Let’s give this change a description (also called a commit message). We can either run jj desc, which opens an editor, or provide it directly with -m.
$ 
Working copy  (@) now at: kpqxywon 6b89ffb2 Implement the feature
Parent commit (@-)      : royxmykx 2bea5e3d main | Adjust config
Notice again that the commit ID has changed, since the hash covers the commit message, but the change ID has remained stable.
Now, let’s say we’re ready to use this new feature, and we want to organize that change in a separate commit. We can create a new commit with jj new:
$ 
Working copy  (@) now at: nlrtlrxv 6cca2add (empty) (no description set)
Parent commit (@-)      : kpqxywon 6b89ffb2 Implement the feature
$ 
@  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:35:49 6cca2add
│  (empty) (no description set)
○  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:33:23 6b89ffb2
│  Implement the feature
◆  royxmykx ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:10:46 main 2bea5e3d
│  Adjust config
~
We’ve created an empty change nlrtlrxv on top of kpqxywon. jj status reflects that we are editing this new revision which does not yet differ from its parent.
$ 
The working copy has no changes.
Working copy  (@) : nlrtlrxv 6cca2add (empty) (no description set)
Parent commit (@-): kpqxywon 6b89ffb2 Implement the feature
jj commit is a shorthand for this common jj desc and jj new combo. Let’s make another change to use our new feature, and commit it.
$ 
Working copy  (@) now at: qmkrwlvp 91067ad0 (empty) (no description set)
Parent commit (@-)      : nlrtlrxv e1b06678 Use the feature
$ 
@  qmkrwlvp ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:41:30 91067ad0
│  (empty) (no description set)
○  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:41:30 e1b06678
│  Use the feature
○  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:33:23 6b89ffb2
│  Implement the feature
◆  royxmykx ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:10:46 main 2bea5e3d
│  Adjust config
~
To inspect the commit we just created (n), we can no longer use the bare jj show, which defaults to showing @. We have to pass the revision we want to inspect:
$ 
Commit ID: e1b066782aa1a565c1e5d38ef27a308aa58e7e4c
Change ID: nlrtlrxvuuskxprvtuznquqzqzzwvnzk
Author   : Ethan Lowman <ethan@example.com> (2026-11-15 12:41:30)
Committer: Ethan Lowman <ethan@example.com> (2026-11-15 12:41:30)
    Use the feature
diff --git a/main.py b/main.py
index c74cd2e0e0..0714ec450b 100644
--- a/main.py
+++ b/main.py
@@ -1,2 +1,4 @@ def my_new_feature():
 def my_new_feature():
   pass
+
+do_something(my_new_feature())
We can describe empty commits to record intent before starting work. Let’s create a new commit on top of k to remind ourselves that we need to document the feature.
$ 
Working copy  (@) now at: qwyusntz 46a2ffda (empty) Document the feature
Parent commit (@-)      : kpqxywon 6b89ffb2 Implement the feature
Added 0 files, modified 1 files, removed 0 files
A separate jj desc would also work just fine instead of the -m flag on jj new. Here’s the current commit graph:
$ 
@  qwyusntz ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:46:35 46a2ffda
│  (empty) Document the feature
│ ○  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:41:30 e1b06678
├─┘  Use the feature
○  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:33:23 6b89ffb2
│  Implement the feature
◆  royxmykx ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:10:46 main 2bea5e3d
│  Adjust config
~
Note how we’ve created branching commits without creating a named branch. This highlights a major difference between jj’s branchless model and git’s branch-centric model of organizing work streams. Instead of juggling your work-in-progress on branches, you work on a graph of commits. Once ready to share, you push branches to a Git forge from your commit graph (more on that later).
Revsets
A revset, an idea borrowed from Mercurial, is an expression that selects a set of revisions from the commit graph. Most jj commands accept revsets in their arguments. When we ran jj new k, the argument k was actually a trivial revset that resolved to the single change with ID kpqxywon. The jj log command has an -r <REVSET> flag that we can use to preview a revset. Some demonstrative examples follow.
Parent of @:
$ 
○  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:33:23 6b89ffb2
│  Implement the feature
~
Grandchildren of r:
$ 
@  qwyusntz ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:51:45 5b55579a
│  Document the feature
~
○  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:41:30 e1b06678
│  Use the feature
~
Commits between r and @, inclusive:
$ 
@  qwyusntz ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:51:45 5b55579a
│  Document the feature
○  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:33:23 6b89ffb2
│  Implement the feature
◆  royxmykx ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:10:46 main 2bea5e3d
│  Adjust config
~
Commit k and all descendants:
$ 
@  qwyusntz ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:51:45 5b55579a
│  Document the feature
│ ○  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:41:30 e1b06678
├─┘  Use the feature
○  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:33:23 6b89ffb2
│  Implement the feature
~
Commits authored by ethan@example.com that also have ID k or q:
$ 
@  qwyusntz ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:51:45 5b55579a
│  Document the feature
○  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:33:23 6b89ffb2
│  Implement the feature
~
I’ll defer to the official documentation for exhaustive detail. The Revsets language specification is easily my most referenced jj documentation page, so I would recommend skimming it to see what’s possible.
Navigating the commit graph
Let’s say in the course of documenting our feature in qwyusntz we realized we made a mistake in the implementation in parent change kpqxywon. We don’t need to manually commit or stash changes to switch tasks. We simply switch our working copy to kpqxywon with jj edit:
$ 
Working copy  (@) now at: kpqxywon 6b89ffb2 Implement the feature
Parent commit (@-)      : royxmykx 2bea5e3d main | Adjust config
Added 0 files, modified 0 files, removed 1 files
Our working copy once again shows the changes from kpqxywon.
$ 
Commit ID: 6b89ffb2a250f622a8638145d65fe8c9bb308dbf
Change ID: kpqxywonksrltlrkyqukqqsxpltznztk
Author   : Ethan Lowman <ethan@example.com> (2026-11-15 12:26:43)
Committer: Ethan Lowman <ethan@example.com> (2026-11-15 12:33:23)
    Implement the feature
diff --git a/main.py b/main.py
new file mode 100644
index 0000000000..c74cd2e0e0
--- /dev/null
+++ b/main.py
@@ -0,0 +1,2 @@ def my_new_feature():
+def my_new_feature():
+  pass
jj hasn’t lost track of the changes above our working copy, including our most recent edits to q:
$ 
○  qwyusntz ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:51:45 5b55579a
│  Document the feature
│ ○  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:41:30 e1b06678
├─┘  Use the feature
@  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:33:23 6b89ffb2
│  Implement the feature
◆  royxmykx ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:10:46 main 2bea5e3d
│  Adjust config
~
Instead of switching commits with jj edit k, we could have run jj edit @-. The revset @- is evaluated before switching the working copy, so this moves to the parent commit. The built-in commands jj next [--edit] and jj prev [--edit] provide another way to move up and down a stack of commits.
Automatic rebasing
If we edit files in the working copy, the descendant commits will be automatically rebased upon the next jj invocation. For example, if we edit my_new_feature’s implementation in main.py and then run jj log, we’ll see that changes k, n, and q all get new commit IDs. jj also tells us that it performed a rebase.
$ 
Rebased 2 descendant commits onto updated working copy.
○  qwyusntz ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:03:41 7d293f19
│  Document the feature
│ ○  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:03:41 41a43a84
├─┘  Use the feature
@  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:03:41 d9f35831
│  Implement the feature
◆  royxmykx ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:10:46 main 2bea5e3d
│  Adjust config
~
Handling conflicts
If the automatic rebase happened to cause merge conflicts, jj doesn’t require us to fix them immediately, like git rebase would. Instead, jj records that the commit is conflicted and lets you carry on with your current task. This is massively helpful to reduce mental context switching, as your stacked commits grow more complex.
$ 
Rebased 2 descendant commits onto updated working copy.
○  qwyusntz ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:05:35 b96f8df4
│  Document the feature
│ ×  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:05:35 e562bff4 (conflict)
├─┘  Use the feature
@  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:05:35 c501c0bd
│  Implement the feature
◆  royxmykx ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:10:46 main 2bea5e3d
│  Adjust config
~
To view the conflicts, we can edit the conflicted commit…
$ 
Working copy  (@) now at: nlrtlrxv e562bff4 (conflict) Use the feature
Parent commit (@-)      : kpqxywon c501c0bd Implement the feature
Added 0 files, modified 1 files, removed 0 files
Warning: There are unresolved conflicts at these paths:
main.py    2-sided conflict
And inspect main.py.
def my_new_feature():
  print("Hello from the new feature!")
<<<<<<< conflict 1 of 1
%%%%%%% diff from: kpqxywon d9f35831 "Implement the feature" (parents of rebased revision)
\\\\\\\        to: kpqxywon c501c0bd "Implement the feature" (rebase destination)
-  pass
+  return 42
+++++++ nlrtlrxv 41a43a84 "Use the feature" (rebased revision)
  pass
do_something(my_new_feature())
>>>>>>> conflict 1 of 1 ends
In a text editor, we can resolve these conflicts, and the next time we run any jj command, we’ll see that jj has marked the commit as unconflicted:
$ 
Working copy changes:
M main.py
Working copy  (@) : nlrtlrxv 34f2e2a1 Use the feature
Parent commit (@-): kpqxywon c501c0bd Implement the feature
Undo and the op log
Let’s say we made a mistake in that conflict resolution, and we want to undo it. In git, undoing an action, if it’s even possible, is often complicated. In jj, we can always run jj undo:
$ 
Undid operation: 35beb0741f17 (2026-11-15 13:08:39) snapshot working copy
Restored to operation: f134ca987b59 (2026-11-15 13:07:18) edit commit e562bff4876f3ea76248c3f730e9e74d3e28e8a0
Working copy  (@) now at: nlrtlrxv e562bff4 (conflict) Use the feature
Parent commit (@-)      : kpqxywon c501c0bd Implement the feature
Added 0 files, modified 1 files, removed 0 files
Warning: There are unresolved conflicts at these paths:
main.py    2-sided conflict
New conflicts appeared in 1 commits:
  nlrtlrxv e562bff4 (conflict) Use the feature
Hint: To resolve the conflicts, start by creating a commit on top of
the conflicted commit:
  jj new nlrtlrxv
Then use `jj resolve`, or edit the conflict markers in the file directly.
Once the conflicts are resolved, you can inspect the result with `jj diff`.
Then run `jj squash` to move the resolution into the conflicted commit.
More generally, jj maintains a history of all actions in jj op log. We can inspect this log and jj op restore to a point in that history, or jj op revert an individual operation. If the jj undo itself was a mistake, we can jj redo.
The Hint in the output above is a bit of a spoiler for our next topic, the squash workflow.
Squash workflow
Editing commits that have descendants can sometimes feel sketchy. If you’re iterating on a large diff that has left your working memory, it would be nice to separate your new changes from the existing diff. For example, we might like to inspect the diff that resolves a rebase conflict in isolation.
Let’s follow the instructions from the above output. We first create a new commit on top of the conflicted commit:
$ 
Working copy  (@) now at: wqxolloz b878878a (conflict) (empty) (no description set)
Parent commit (@-)      : nlrtlrxv e562bff4 (conflict) Use the feature
Warning: There are unresolved conflicts at these paths:
main.py    2-sided conflict
$ 
@  wqxolloz ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:11:44 b878878a (conflict)
│  (empty) (no description set)
×  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:05:35 e562bff4 (conflict)
│  Use the feature
~
Now we can resolve the conflicts in main.py in our editor, and rerun jj log:
$ 
@  wqxolloz ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:14:12 91181aec
│  (no description set)
×  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:05:35 e562bff4 (conflict)
│  Use the feature
~
We can inspect the diff in @ if we like, and when we’re happy with the conflict resolution, we can squash it into the parent commit:
$ 
Working copy  (@) now at: vkywoywq f6b548b3 (empty) (no description set)
Parent commit (@-)      : nlrtlrxv 7208deb7 Use the feature
Existing conflicts were resolved or abandoned from 1 commits.
$ 
@  vkywoywq ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:15:39 f6b548b3
│  (empty) (no description set)
○  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:15:39 7208deb7
│  Use the feature
~
The conflict is now resolved, and we can continue our work.
More commit graph operations
One of the powerful qualities of jj is that it makes diffs malleable like clay. With automatic rebase and the confidence inspired by jj undo, we can move, combine, drop, and split diffs with ease.
Let’s try a few operations on our current commit graph. Here’s the starting point.
$ 
@  vkywoywq ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:15:39 f6b548b3
│  (empty) (no description set)
○  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:15:39 7208deb7
│  Use the feature
│ ○  qwyusntz ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:05:35 b96f8df4
├─┘  Document the feature
○  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:05:35 c501c0bd
│  Implement the feature
◆  royxmykx ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:10:46 main 2bea5e3d
│  Adjust config
~
Rebase
Let’s use jj rebase to coerce our branching commit graph into a linear history. Let’s say the documentation commit (q) should go between the implementation (k) and the usage (n). We could accomplish this by moving nlrtlrxv (and its empty descendant) on top of qwyusntz:
$ 
Rebased 2 commits to destination.
Working copy  (@) now at: vkywoywq 0405560c (empty) (no description set)
Parent commit (@-)      : nlrtlrxv 9920235b Use the feature
Added 1 files, modified 0 files, removed 0 files
$ 
@  vkywoywq ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:20:18 0405560c
│  (empty) (no description set)
○  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:20:18 9920235b
│  Use the feature
○  qwyusntz ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:05:35 b96f8df4
│  Document the feature
○  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:05:35 c501c0bd
│  Implement the feature
◆  royxmykx ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:10:46 main 2bea5e3d
│  Adjust config
~
Another way to accomplish the same effect would be to use jj rebase -r q --before n. Many jj commands have multiple ways to specify revsets. jj rebase --help documents these well.
Since arguments to jj rebase flags are all revsets, we can write a revset that selects a sequence of commits or commit subgraph, and move it elsewhere in the graph.
If we wanted to insert a commit below k, the most direct way is jj new --before k, but for demonstration purposes, we could alternatively create a new commit on top of r and rebase k:: on top:
$ 
Working copy  (@) now at: wxzmtyol be5ca29b (empty) Prepare for the feature
Parent commit (@-)      : royxmykx 2bea5e3d main | Adjust config
Added 0 files, modified 0 files, removed 2 files
$ 
@  wxzmtyol ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:23:29 be5ca29b
│  (empty) Prepare for the feature
│ ○  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:20:18 9920235b
│ │  Use the feature
│ ○  qwyusntz ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:05:35 b96f8df4
│ │  Document the feature
│ ○  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:05:35 c501c0bd
├─┘  Implement the feature
◆  royxmykx ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:10:46 main 2bea5e3d
│  Adjust config
~
$ 
Rebased 3 commits to destination.
$ 
○  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:28:02 cda0de88
│  Use the feature
○  qwyusntz ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:28:02 ec9de16f
│  Document the feature
○  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:28:02 722e7268
│  Implement the feature
@  wxzmtyol ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:23:29 be5ca29b
│  (empty) Prepare for the feature
◆  royxmykx ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:10:46 main 2bea5e3d
│  Adjust config
~
If you enjoy using TUIs, jj arrange provides a commit graph editor similar to git rebase -i for reordering commits.
Squash
If we decided documentation should be in the same commit as implementation, we can squash:
$ 
Rebased 1 descendant commits.
jj will open an editor and give us a chance to write a combined commit message.
$ 
○  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:31:30 cb468825
│  Use the feature
○  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:31:30 b93e4ef7
│  Implement and document the feature
@  wxzmtyol ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:23:29 be5ca29b
│  (empty) Prepare for the feature
◆  royxmykx ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:10:46 main 2bea5e3d
│  Adjust config
~
Abandon
To drop a commit from the graph, we can use jj abandon. Let’s drop the preparation commit:
$ 
Abandoned 1 commits:
  wxzmtyol be5ca29b (empty) Prepare for the feature
Rebased 2 descendant commits onto parents of abandoned commits.
Working copy  (@) now at: xzwrrwyv d460fbd4 (empty) (no description set)
Parent commit (@-)      : royxmykx 2bea5e3d main | Adjust config
$ 
@  xzwrrwyv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:33:49 d460fbd4
│  (empty) (no description set)
│ ○  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:33:49 b1284f61
│ │  Use the feature
│ ○  kpqxywon ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:33:49 df93f0a6
├─┘  Implement and document the feature
◆  royxmykx ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 12:10:46 main 2bea5e3d
│  Adjust config
~
To abandon parts of a commit (hunks or changes to whole files), we can use the jj diffedit TUI (see docs).
Split
The jj split TUI allows us to take a commit and split it into two consecutive commits by selecting which files or hunks should go in the first. Let’s use it to split up implementation and documentation again with jj split -r k:
The jj-hunk project provides a means to automate commit splitting. If you are so inclined, agents are quite good at using this tool to split up commits into a sequence of smaller changes for easier review.
Bookmarks and Git branches
jj does not have a native concept of a branch, but it does have a primitive called bookmarks that map to Git branches. When we need to manipulate Git branches, we do it by way of bookmarks.
You can go pretty far using Git without realizing this, but a Git branch is just a pointer to a commit. jj explicitly models bookmarks this way. jj bookmarks are essentially a named label attached to a commit that we can move around as we modify our commit graph, and push to/pull from a remote.
We’ll discuss a few basic workflows around Git branches below, but you can find a more complete guide in the jj docs on Working with GitHub.
Pushing a new branch
Assuming we initialized jj in a git repository that has remotes, we should have some output for jj git remote list.
Since jj is branchless, the commits we’ve created locally don’t have a branch name. Luckily, jj doesn’t require us to pick one, and generates one for us. Generated branch names free you from thinking of a unique branch name so you can instead focus on writing a good commit message (usually the more durable descriptor of a change).
To create a generated bookmark for change ID nlrtlrxv and push up a corresponding Git branch, we can run a command like the following:
  
)
)
) | 
)))
)
Now jj log shows our change with a correspondingly named bookmark push-nlrtlrxvuusk associated:
$ 
○  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:33:49 push-nlrtlrxvuusk b1284f61
│  Use the feature
~
We’re free to create a PR from this branch on GitHub now. You can customize the bookmark naming scheme with the git_push_bookmark template config documented here. It’s a common pattern to format branch names like <author name>/<change ID>.
Since jj git push -c creates a branch name that corresponds to a change ID, it seems to encourage one evolving change per PR. I think it’s usually best to lean into these ergonomics, and have one change per PR.
Updating a branch
To update a branch, the general process is to either squash commits into a change that is already bookmarked, or create new commits and move the bookmark forward.
Bookmark squash flow
If you choose to squash commits into an existing bookmark (recommended to keep one change per PR), the flow is as follows.
First, create a new commit on top of our branch.
$ 
Working copy  (@) now at: stzvpxow f14a0cf7 (empty) (no description set)
Parent commit (@-)      : nlrtlrxv b1284f61 push-nlrtlrxvuusk | Use the feature
Added 2 files, modified 0 files, removed 0 files
Then make our edits.
$ 
Working copy changes:
M README.md
Working copy  (@) : stzvpxow 6bdd43dc (no description set)
Parent commit (@-): nlrtlrxv b1284f61 push-nlrtlrxvuusk | Use the feature
Squash them into the bookmark branch.
$ 
Working copy  (@) now at: xryuuprx 04d65a03 (empty) (no description set)
Parent commit (@-)      : nlrtlrxv aeb869ae push-nlrtlrxvuusk* | Use the feature
We now see push-nlrtlrxvuusk has an asterisk next to it, indicating it has changes that haven’t been pushed to the remote. So let’s push them.
$ 
Changes to push to origin:
  bookmark: push-nlrtlrxvuusk [move sideways from b1284f61c417 to aeb869aef99b]
The remote branch now reflects the new changes. The above output says that we moved the branch sideways from one commit to another commit, which reflects that we did a force-push. jj git push runs the equivalent of a git push --force-with-lease, so if you’re ever unclear about what a push operation would do, you can use the jj git push --dry-run flag. jj protects you against most dangerous force pushes refusing to rewrite a configurable revset of immutable commits. The default immutable revset is sensible, but you can make it more or less conservative by configuring your own immutable heads.
Bookmark advance flow
In this flow, we add new commits on top of our bookmark and then move the bookmark to point to our new commit.
Let’s first prepare the new commit.
$ 
Working copy  (@) now at: pptrztmx 6e49ff1f (empty) (no description set)
Parent commit (@-)      : nlrtlrxv aeb869ae push-nlrtlrxvuusk | Use the feature
Then make our edits.
$ 
Working copy changes:
M README.md
Working copy  (@) : pptrztmx 89027165 (no description set)
Parent commit (@-): nlrtlrxv aeb869ae push-nlrtlrxvuusk | Use the feature
$ 
Working copy  (@) now at: wunttkrp 044f2d98 (empty) (no description set)
Parent commit (@-)      : pptrztmx 2341ee4a Add a usage example
The bookmark does not automatically move to our new commit. This is the biggest difference between bookmarks and Git branches. For now push-nlrtlrxvuusk still points at the commit below:
$ 
@  wunttkrp ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:51:57 044f2d98
│  (empty) (no description set)
○  pptrztmx ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:51:57 2341ee4a
│  Add a usage example
○  nlrtlrxv ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 13:43:16 push-nlrtlrxvuusk aeb869ae
│  Use the feature
~
There are several ways to move the bookmark to the new commit. The easiest is probably jj bookmark advance, which moves the closest bookmark to the given commit:
$ 
Advanced 1 bookmarks to pptrztmx 2341ee4a push-nlrtlrxvuusk* | Add a usage example
Now we’re ready to push the moved bookmark.
$ 
Changes to push to origin:
  bookmark: push-nlrtlrxvuusk [move forward from aeb869aef99b to 2341ee4aa554]
Checking out a remote branch
Since jj is branchless, strictly speaking, we don’t “check out” a branch, but we can create a commit on top of a remote branch. First do the equivalent of a git fetch, importing remote bookmarks:
And create a new empty commit on top of the remote branch:
Our working copy now contains all the commits on the remote branch. However, if we were to drop back to git, we see that from git’s perspective, we’re in a detached head state, not on any branch.
$ 
Not currently on any branch.
nothing to commit, working tree clean
This is because jj is editing a commit above a bookmark, and does not have an equivalent to git’s “current branch” state.
Pulling in changes from the trunk
Syncing with the trunk (e.g. main) is just a specific example of this flow. We would jj git fetch to update our main to the upstream main, and then rebase our commits on top. For example, the following would rebase my-branch on top of a new main:
jj rebase -b my-branch -o main
Staying oriented in the graph
jj commit graph manipulation is easiest when you have a live commit graph on screen. There are different ways you can accomplish this.
jjui is a TUI for jj that allows you to edit the visual graph directly. I have not personally used this, but I know people who like it.
I prefer to have an always-live jj log in the corner of my screen by way of viddy (a nicer version of watch that supports color):
I recommend filtering down this log to a revset that represents your current work. Here’s a starting point:
visible() & (trunk() | (tracked_remote_bookmarks() & ~::trunk()) | (mutable() & mine())::)
You might also consider trying my experimental project jj-stamp, which is a local code review GUI for the squash workflow. Even if you don’t use the squash review workflow, it makes a pretty good diff viewer with a live jj log on the side and a terminal for running jj operations.
Stacked PRs, in brief
One of the biggest productivity gains from jj comes in its ability to support stacked PR workflows. This merits its own post, so I’ll keep it short.
Large changes are easier to understand when they’re broken up into a series of logical steps. Breaking up a large change into smaller sequential atoms of review makes the whole change easier to understand. Stacked PRs attempt to leverage this fact to optimize merge throughput, while simultaneously removing pipeline stalls in the review process.
Let’s say we’re building a new feature. We can decide we want to split up implementation into three stages:
- Refactor to make the feature easier to implement
- Implement the feature
- Use the feature in another system
jj’s ability to work on stacked changes with automatic rebase is a natural fit for implementing these three stages. Each of the three stages maps to a single commit, each with an associated bookmark/branch/PR:
$ 
○  sxummoow ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 14:10:48 ethan/sxummoow b5f0af21
│  Use the feature
○  vqpzktsp ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 14:06:27 ethan/vqpzktsp fe52c25f
│  Implement the feature
○  oomyzqmz ethan@example.com 2026-11-15 14:01:19 ethan/oomyzqmz 07f69717
│  Refactor for the feature
~
After you implement the first change, you can put up a PR for review and immediately start working on the next change. Your work stream becomes a long queue where you pop off the bottom as PRs are ready to merge and push to the top, with no pipeline stalls.
It’s pretty easy to manually set up small PR stacks with jj and GitHub, but once you get hooked on the technique (making stacks 5–10 PRs deep), you’ll want a tool. jj-pancake is my entry into the ring of stacked PR tools. More on that soon.
Resources
- Official Jujutsu docs. I most often refer to the revset spec.
- Steve Klabnik’s jj tutorial was my first introduction to jj. I don’t think it’s maintained as a durable resource (and I don’t promise mine will be!), but it’s still a good read.
- I follow the jj releases RSS feed. Lately the releases have been packed with new features. Overall jj is quite stable, but the occasional bug is fixed promptly.
- The Jujutsu discord is active and friendly!
- The blog of East River Source Control, a company that is building a new forge and code review platform intended to support Jujutsu well. The creator of Jujutsu, Martin von Zweigbergk, has joined as the CTO, and they have lots of other smart people as well. Perhaps a successor to GitHub one day? I’m rooting for them.
- My personal jj config. You might find some useful aliases or tricks here, but I haven’t attempted to make it readable.
- The jj config of Austin Seipp, a jj maintainer. Cool tricks lie within.

FFmpeg 9.1 'Gauss'
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
04:02:39 UTC
13:02:39 KST

If you find FFmpeg useful, you are welcome to contribute by donating. More downloading options
Get packages & executable files
FFmpeg only provides source code. Below are some links that provide it already compiled and ready to go.
Linux Packages
Linux Static Builds
Windows EXE Files
Get the Sources
You can retrieve the source code through Git by using the command:
git clone https://git.ffmpeg.org/ffmpeg.git ffmpeg
FFmpeg has always been a very experimental and developer-driven project. It is a key component in many multimedia projects and has new features added constantly. Development branch snapshots work really well 99% of the time so people are not afraid to use them.
Git Repositories
Since FFmpeg is developed with Git, multiple repositories from developers and groups of developers are available.
Release Verification
All FFmpeg releases are cryptographically signed with our public PGP key and should be verified for authenticity.
pub   rsa2048 2011-04-26 [SC]
    FCF986EA15E6E293A5644F10B4322F04D67658D8
uid           [  full  ] FFmpeg release signing key <ffmpeg-devel@ffmpeg.org>
sub   rsa2048 2011-04-26 [E]
  To verify a release:
- Import our public key into your local keyring:
        $ curl https://ffmpeg.org/ffmpeg-devel.asc | gpg --import
- Download a release tarball and its corresponding signature.
- 
        Verify the signature:
        $ gpg --verify ffmpeg-4.3.2.tar.xz.asc ffmpeg-4.3.2.tar.xz gpg: Signature made Sun 21 Feb 2021 06:35:15 AEST gpg: using RSA key FCF986EA15E6E293A5644F10B4322F04D67658D8 gpg: issuer "ffmpeg-devel@ffmpeg.org" gpg: Good signature from "FFmpeg release signing key <ffmpeg-devel@ffmpeg.org>" [full]
Optionally, you can verify that git and tarball match, the only differences should be the absence of .git* files in the tarball and a VERSION file in the tarball containing the version. The git tags should be signed with EDDSA key DD1EC9E8DE085C629B3E1846B18E8928B3948D64
    A release that also has a .pq.asc file carries post-quantum
    signatures (RFC 9980)
    made with both of these keys:
  
ML-DSA-87+Ed448  99669C49693683D51F174FE0D9E84915F5C2A536F5C0DA70FB8DCBD8AA8C4B3D
SLH-DSA-256s     547721479F80449E77748C0235116A1F8A73D9ECA98673B90E4E39347D229989
GnuPG cannot verify these yet; use sq 1.4 or later built with a cryptographic backend that supports these algorithms (OpenSSL 3.5 or RustCrypto), both of these must succeed:
$ sq verify --signature-file ffmpeg-9.0.2.tar.xz.pq.asc --signer-file ffmpeg-mldsa87-ed448.cert.pgp ffmpeg-9.0.2.tar.xz
$ sq verify --signature-file ffmpeg-9.0.2.tar.xz.pq.asc --signer-file ffmpeg-slhdsa256s.cert.pgp ffmpeg-9.0.2.tar.xz
Releases
Approximately every 6 months the FFmpeg project makes a new major release. Between major releases point releases will appear that add important bug fixes but no new features. Note that these releases are intended for distributors and system integrators. Users that wish to compile from source themselves are strongly encouraged to consider using the development branch (see above), this is the only version on which FFmpeg developers actively work. The release branches only cherry pick selected changes from the development branch, which therefore receives much more and much faster bug fixes such as additional features and security patches.

Get packages & executable files
FFmpeg only provides source code. Below are some links that provide it already compiled and ready to go.
Linux Packages
Linux Static Builds
Windows EXE Files
Get the Sources
You can retrieve the source code through Git by using the command:
git clone https://git.ffmpeg.org/ffmpeg.git ffmpeg
FFmpeg has always been a very experimental and developer-driven project. It is a key component in many multimedia projects and has new features added constantly. Development branch snapshots work really well 99% of the time so people are not afraid to use them.
Git Repositories
Since FFmpeg is developed with Git, multiple repositories from developers and groups of developers are available.
Release Verification
All FFmpeg releases are cryptographically signed with our public PGP key and should be verified for authenticity.
pub   rsa2048 2011-04-26 [SC]
    FCF986EA15E6E293A5644F10B4322F04D67658D8
uid           [  full  ] FFmpeg release signing key <ffmpeg-devel@ffmpeg.org>
sub   rsa2048 2011-04-26 [E]
  To verify a release:
- Import our public key into your local keyring:
        $ curl https://ffmpeg.org/ffmpeg-devel.asc | gpg --import
- Download a release tarball and its corresponding signature.
- 
        Verify the signature:
        $ gpg --verify ffmpeg-4.3.2.tar.xz.asc ffmpeg-4.3.2.tar.xz gpg: Signature made Sun 21 Feb 2021 06:35:15 AEST gpg: using RSA key FCF986EA15E6E293A5644F10B4322F04D67658D8 gpg: issuer "ffmpeg-devel@ffmpeg.org" gpg: Good signature from "FFmpeg release signing key <ffmpeg-devel@ffmpeg.org>" [full]
Optionally, you can verify that git and tarball match, the only differences should be the absence of .git* files in the tarball and a VERSION file in the tarball containing the version. The git tags should be signed with EDDSA key DD1EC9E8DE085C629B3E1846B18E8928B3948D64
    A release that also has a .pq.asc file carries post-quantum
    signatures (RFC 9980)
    made with both of these keys:
  
ML-DSA-87+Ed448  99669C49693683D51F174FE0D9E84915F5C2A536F5C0DA70FB8DCBD8AA8C4B3D
SLH-DSA-256s     547721479F80449E77748C0235116A1F8A73D9ECA98673B90E4E39347D229989
GnuPG cannot verify these yet; use sq 1.4 or later built with a cryptographic backend that supports these algorithms (OpenSSL 3.5 or RustCrypto), both of these must succeed:
$ sq verify --signature-file ffmpeg-9.0.2.tar.xz.pq.asc --signer-file ffmpeg-mldsa87-ed448.cert.pgp ffmpeg-9.0.2.tar.xz
$ sq verify --signature-file ffmpeg-9.0.2.tar.xz.pq.asc --signer-file ffmpeg-slhdsa256s.cert.pgp ffmpeg-9.0.2.tar.xz

GIMP now has UI tests
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
04:00:24 UTC
13:00:24 KST

GIMP has a lot of intertwined menus, dialogs and tools. When we change a part of the interface, another may stop working and sometimes, the problem can be missed until a user reports it.
To find these problems early, a new GUI testing platform was implemented. A typical GUI test starts GIMP, clicks menus and buttons, and then checks the result. These tests run automatically each time we merge contributor changes.
Why we need them
GIMP already has unit tests. A unit test checks one small part of the code, for example how GIMP reads an XCF file. But our unit tests were not opening a real window. So it coult not tell us if a dialog opens, or if a menu item did its job.
Note that GIMP already had tests of GUI code, usually interacting directly with the core code, and sometimes even simulating input devices’ events (pointer movements, clicks, drags…). But nothing can beat interacting with the real interface. Furthermore such legacy tests directly hijacking core code come with their own problems, such as breaking all too often (and not because they detected a bug), all while not being able to detect whole categories of bugs.
UI tests check the program as a whole, in the same way as you use it.
What the tests do
Each test starts a real GIMP and does one small task. Then it reads the result from GIMP. If the result is not correct, the test fails and tells us what went wrong.
We have 13 tests at this time. They check that:
- GIMP starts and shows its main window.
- The Welcome dialog opens on the correct tab. It also stays hidden when you turn off “Show on Start”.
- File > New makes an image with the size you set.
- File > Export As writes a correct PNG file.
- Tools > Swap Colors swaps the foreground and background colors.
- You can type a new foreground color in the Colors dialog.
- You can add a layer and delete a layer.
- Undo and Redo work.
- Zoom In changes the zoom level.
- A click on a tool button in the toolbox selects that tool. The test then uses the Bucket Fill tool on the canvas.
- A change in Preferences is saved when you click OK.
- A menu action, Image > Flatten Image, does its job.
How they work
The tests do not need a screen. Each test starts a small, hidden desktop, runs GIMP in it, and removes it at the end. Because of this, the tests can run on our CI servers, where no screen is available.
To find a button or a menu, the tests do not look at the pixels on the screen. They use the accessibility information that GIMP gives to tools such as screen readers. This has one more good result: when we make GIMP easier to test, we also make it easier to use with a screen reader, hence improve the software accessibility.
The tests use dogtail, a Python tool for UI tests. To run GIMP on a server with no screen, dogtail needed a new feature. We contributed this feature upstream, to dogtail itself, so other projects can use it too.
At this time, the tests run only on Linux (both X11 and Wayland) since
dogtail does not support Windows or macOs.
Adding even these few simple tests considerably increases confidence that the GIMP build has worked, and also shows the test infrastructure is working and useful.
Credits
- Harsh Verma wrote the UI tests and the new dogtail feature.
- Jehan reviewed the UI tests.
- Michal Odehnal reviewed the new dogtail feature.
You can read the full change in merge request !2909.
How you can help
13 tests is a good start, but GIMP has many more dialogs and tools. If you want to help, you can write a test for the part of GIMP that you use the most.
We wrote a guide that shows how to write a new test and how to find the cause when a test fails: UI tests on developer.gimp.org.
We hope this work will help us find bugs earlier and keep GIMP working well for everyone.

PostgreSQL 19
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
03:45:45 UTC
12:45:45 KST

Release date: 2026-11-19
PostgreSQL 19 contains many new features and enhancements, including:
New REPACK command that reclaims disk space and reorganizes table contents, which can improve query performance. REPACK (CONCURRENTLY) allows repacking without blocking reads and writes to the table.
pg_plan_advice extension for controlling query planner decisions and pg_stash_advice extension that applies plan advice automatically based on the query.
Better performance in many areas, including automatic scaling of the number of I/O worker processes, quicker foreign-key checks, and further planning and execution optimizations.
Logical replication can now be enabled without a server restart when wal_level is set to replica.
Logical replication of sequence values.
Support for “read-your-writes” consistency on standbys via the new WAIT command.
Parallel autovacuum of table indexes and autovacuum prioritization through a new scoring system.
The above items and other new features of PostgreSQL 19 are explained in more detail in the sections below.
A dump/restore using pg_dumpall or use of pg_upgrade or logical replication is required for those wishing to migrate data from any previous release. See Section 18.6 for general information on migrating to new major releases.
Version 19 contains a number of changes that may affect compatibility with previous releases. Observe the following incompatibilities:
Add server variable password_expiration_warning_threshold to warn about password expiration (Gilles Darold, Nathan Bossart) §
The default warning period is seven days.
Issue a warning after successful MD5 password authentication (Nathan Bossart) §
The warning can be disabled via server variable md5_password_warnings. MD5 passwords were marked as deprecated in PostgreSQL 18.
Remove RADIUS support (Thomas Munro) §
PostgreSQL only supported RADIUS over UDP, which is unfixably insecure.
Force standard_conforming_strings to always be on in the database server (Tom Lane) §
Dumps created using pre-PostgreSQL 19 versions of pg_dump or pg_dumpall, and using standard_conforming_strings = off, will not properly load into PostgreSQL 19 and later servers. Users should create dumps using PostgreSQL 19 or later versions of these applications, or use standard_conforming_strings = on.
Client applications still support operations with servers having standard_conforming_strings = off, for compatibility with old servers. The server variable escape_string_warning has been removed as unnecessary.
Disallow carriage returns and line feeds in database, role, and tablespace names (Mahendra Singh Thalor) §
pg_upgrade will also disallow upgrading of clusters that use such names. This was changed to avoid security problems.
Change the default index opclasses for inet and cidr data types from btree_gist to GiST (Tom Lane) § §
The btree_gist inet/cidr opclasses are broken because they can exclude rows that should be returned. pg_upgrade will disallow upgrading of clusters with btree_gist inet/cidr indexes.
Disallow system columns from being used in COPY FROM ... WHERE (Tom Lane) §
The values of such columns were not well-defined.
Change a json_array() call which returns no rows to return an empty JSON array (Richard Guo) §
This previously returned NULL.
Cause transactions to pass their READ ONLY and DEFERRABLE status to postgres_fdw sessions (Etsuro Fujita) §
This means READ ONLY transactions can no longer modify rows processed by postgres_fdw sessions.
Change default of max_locks_per_transaction from 64 to 128 (Heikki Linnakangas) §
Lock size allocation has changed, so effectively settings must now be doubled to match their capacity in previous releases.
Remove server variable io_workers (Thomas Munro) §
This has been replaced by four new variables; see Section E.1.3.1.2.
Change JIT to be disabled by default (Jelte Fennema-Nio) §
Previously JIT was enabled by default, and activated based on optimizer costs, but this costing has been determined to be unreliable. This change requires sites that are doing many large analytical queries to manually enable JIT.
Change parallel vacuum leaders and workers to share the cost-based vacuum delay limit (Bharath Rupireddy) §
Previously each worker process slept based only on the cost it incurred itself, so a parallel vacuum could consume several times the budget set by vacuum_cost_limit. With this change, VACUUM with parallel workers and cost-based delay enabled might run slower than in previous releases.
Rename column sync_error_count to sync_table_error_count in system view pg_stat_subscription_stats (Vignesh C) §
This is necessary since sequence errors are now tracked separately.
Rename wait event type BUFFERPIN to BUFFER and rename wait event BufferPin to BufferCleanup (Andres Freund) §
Remove wait event BufferContent and add more precise wait events BufferShared, BufferShareExclusive, and BufferExclusive (Andres Freund) §
Change index access method handlers to use a static IndexAmRoutines structure, rather than dynamically allocated ones (Matthias van de Meent) §
Remove optimizer hook get_relation_info_hook and add better-placed hook build_simple_rel_hook (Robert Haas) §
Change psql's \crosstabview command to honor the \pset null setting (Chao Li) §
Change full text search and pg_trgm to use the database's locale provider for character classification when parsing text (Jeff Davis) §
Previously, characters were classified using libc, regardless of the locale provider. In databases with a locale provider other than libc (e.g., ICU, builtin) and where the locale configured through that locale provider behaves differently from the LC_CTYPE setting configured for the database, particularly a LC_CTYPE of C, the output of some full text search and pg_trgm functions could change. When upgrading such databases using pg_upgrade, it is recommended to reindex all indexes which use full text search and pg_trgm after the upgrade, and to recompute any stored tsvector values.
Change how fuzzystrmatch functions handle non-ASCII characters (Peter Eisentraut, Jeff Davis) § §
dmetaphone() and dmetaphone_alt() now upper-case their input using the database's default collation rather than always using libc, and soundex(), metaphone(), and difference() now treat only ASCII letters as letters. When upgrading with pg_upgrade, it is recommended to reindex any indexes that use these functions after the upgrade.
Remove MULE_INTERNAL encoding (Thomas Munro) §
This encoding was complex and rarely used. Databases using it will need to be dumped and restored with a different encoding.
Below you will find a detailed account of the changes between PostgreSQL 19 and the previous major release.
Allow NOT IN clauses to be converted to more efficient ANTI JOINs when NULLs are not present (Richard Guo) §
Allow more LEFT JOINs to be converted to ANTI JOINs (Tender Wang, Richard Guo) §
Allow use of Memoize for ANTI JOINs with unique inner sides (Richard Guo) §
Allow some aggregate processing to be performed before joins (Richard Guo, Antonin Houska) § § §
This can reduce the number of rows needed to be processed.
Improve hash join's handling of tuples with NULL join keys (Tom Lane) §
Improve the planning of semijoins (Richard Guo) §
Allow Append and MergeAppend to consider explicit incremental sorts (Richard Guo) §
Convert IS [NOT] DISTINCT FROM NULL to IS [NOT] NULL during constant folding (Richard Guo) §
The latter form is more easily optimized.
Simplify IS [NOT] DISTINCT FROM to equality/inequality operators when inputs are proven non-nullable (Richard Guo) §
Perform earlier constant folding of var IS [NOT] NULL in the optimizer (Richard Guo) §
This allows for later optimizations.
Simplify COALESCE() and ROW(...) IS [NOT] NULL to avoid evaluating unnecessary arguments (Richard Guo) § §
Simplify IS [NOT] TRUE/FALSE/UNKNOWN to plain boolean expressions when the input is proven non-nullable (Richard Guo) §
Speed up join selectivity computations for large optimizer statistics targets (Ilia Evdokimov, David Geier) §
Add proper optimizer statistics for functions returning boolean values (Tom Lane) §

This information is used by the optimizer in planning memory usage.

The following individuals (in alphabetical order) have contributed to this release as patch authors, committers, reviewers, testers, or reporters of issues.

====================================================================================================

PostgreSQL 19
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
03:45:45 UTC
12:45:45 KST

Release date: 2026-11-19
PostgreSQL 19 contains many new features and enhancements, including:
New REPACK command that reclaims disk space and reorganizes table contents, which can improve query performance. REPACK (CONCURRENTLY) allows repacking without blocking reads and writes to the table.
pg_plan_advice extension for controlling query planner decisions and pg_stash_advice extension that applies plan advice automatically based on the query.
Better performance in many areas, including automatic scaling of the number of I/O worker processes, quicker foreign-key checks, and further planning and execution optimizations.
Logical replication can now be enabled without a server restart when wal_level is set to replica.
Logical replication of sequence values.
Support for “read-your-writes” consistency on standbys via the new WAIT command.
Parallel autovacuum of table indexes and autovacuum prioritization through a new scoring system.
The above items and other new features of PostgreSQL 19 are explained in more detail in the sections below.
A dump/restore using pg_dumpall or use of pg_upgrade or logical replication is required for those wishing to migrate data from any previous release. See Section 18.6 for general information on migrating to new major releases.
Version 19 contains a number of changes that may affect compatibility with previous releases. Observe the following incompatibilities:
Add server variable password_expiration_warning_threshold to warn about password expiration (Gilles Darold, Nathan Bossart) §
The default warning period is seven days.
Issue a warning after successful MD5 password authentication (Nathan Bossart) §
The warning can be disabled via server variable md5_password_warnings. MD5 passwords were marked as deprecated in PostgreSQL 18.
Remove RADIUS support (Thomas Munro) §
PostgreSQL only supported RADIUS over UDP, which is unfixably insecure.
Force standard_conforming_strings to always be on in the database server (Tom Lane) §
Dumps created using pre-PostgreSQL 19 versions of pg_dump or pg_dumpall, and using standard_conforming_strings = off, will not properly load into PostgreSQL 19 and later servers. Users should create dumps using PostgreSQL 19 or later versions of these applications, or use standard_conforming_strings = on.
Client applications still support operations with servers having standard_conforming_strings = off, for compatibility with old servers. The server variable escape_string_warning has been removed as unnecessary.
Disallow carriage returns and line feeds in database, role, and tablespace names (Mahendra Singh Thalor) §
pg_upgrade will also disallow upgrading of clusters that use such names. This was changed to avoid security problems.
Change the default index opclasses for inet and cidr data types from btree_gist to GiST (Tom Lane) § §
The btree_gist inet/cidr opclasses are broken because they can exclude rows that should be returned. pg_upgrade will disallow upgrading of clusters with btree_gist inet/cidr indexes.
Disallow system columns from being used in COPY FROM ... WHERE (Tom Lane) §
The values of such columns were not well-defined.
Change a json_array() call which returns no rows to return an empty JSON array (Richard Guo) §
This previously returned NULL.
Cause transactions to pass their READ ONLY and DEFERRABLE status to postgres_fdw sessions (Etsuro Fujita) §
This means READ ONLY transactions can no longer modify rows processed by postgres_fdw sessions.
Change default of max_locks_per_transaction from 64 to 128 (Heikki Linnakangas) §
Lock size allocation has changed, so effectively settings must now be doubled to match their capacity in previous releases.
Remove server variable io_workers (Thomas Munro) §
This has been replaced by four new variables; see Section E.1.3.1.2.
Change JIT to be disabled by default (Jelte Fennema-Nio) §
Previously JIT was enabled by default, and activated based on optimizer costs, but this costing has been determined to be unreliable. This change requires sites that are doing many large analytical queries to manually enable JIT.
Change parallel vacuum leaders and workers to share the cost-based vacuum delay limit (Bharath Rupireddy) §
Previously each worker process slept based only on the cost it incurred itself, so a parallel vacuum could consume several times the budget set by vacuum_cost_limit. With this change, VACUUM with parallel workers and cost-based delay enabled might run slower than in previous releases.
Rename column sync_error_count to sync_table_error_count in system view pg_stat_subscription_stats (Vignesh C) §
This is necessary since sequence errors are now tracked separately.
Rename wait event type BUFFERPIN to BUFFER and rename wait event BufferPin to BufferCleanup (Andres Freund) §
Remove wait event BufferContent and add more precise wait events BufferShared, BufferShareExclusive, and BufferExclusive (Andres Freund) §
Change index access method handlers to use a static IndexAmRoutines structure, rather than dynamically allocated ones (Matthias van de Meent) §
Remove optimizer hook get_relation_info_hook and add better-placed hook build_simple_rel_hook (Robert Haas) §
Change psql's \crosstabview command to honor the \pset null setting (Chao Li) §
Change full text search and pg_trgm to use the database's locale provider for character classification when parsing text (Jeff Davis) §
Previously, characters were classified using libc, regardless of the locale provider. In databases with a locale provider other than libc (e.g., ICU, builtin) and where the locale configured through that locale provider behaves differently from the LC_CTYPE setting configured for the database, particularly a LC_CTYPE of C, the output of some full text search and pg_trgm functions could change. When upgrading such databases using pg_upgrade, it is recommended to reindex all indexes which use full text search and pg_trgm after the upgrade, and to recompute any stored tsvector values.
Change how fuzzystrmatch functions handle non-ASCII characters (Peter Eisentraut, Jeff Davis) § §
dmetaphone() and dmetaphone_alt() now upper-case their input using the database's default collation rather than always using libc, and soundex(), metaphone(), and difference() now treat only ASCII letters as letters. When upgrading with pg_upgrade, it is recommended to reindex any indexes that use these functions after the upgrade.
Remove MULE_INTERNAL encoding (Thomas Munro) §
This encoding was complex and rarely used. Databases using it will need to be dumped and restored with a different encoding.
Below you will find a detailed account of the changes between PostgreSQL 19 and the previous major release.
Allow NOT IN clauses to be converted to more efficient ANTI JOINs when NULLs are not present (Richard Guo) §
Allow more LEFT JOINs to be converted to ANTI JOINs (Tender Wang, Richard Guo) §
Allow use of Memoize for ANTI JOINs with unique inner sides (Richard Guo) §
Allow some aggregate processing to be performed before joins (Richard Guo, Antonin Houska) § § §
This can reduce the number of rows needed to be processed.
Improve hash join's handling of tuples with NULL join keys (Tom Lane) §
Improve the planning of semijoins (Richard Guo) §
Allow Append and MergeAppend to consider explicit incremental sorts (Richard Guo) §
Convert IS [NOT] DISTINCT FROM NULL to IS [NOT] NULL during constant folding (Richard Guo) §
The latter form is more easily optimized.
Simplify IS [NOT] DISTINCT FROM to equality/inequality operators when inputs are proven non-nullable (Richard Guo) §
Perform earlier constant folding of var IS [NOT] NULL in the optimizer (Richard Guo) §
This allows for later optimizations.
Simplify COALESCE() and ROW(...) IS [NOT] NULL to avoid evaluating unnecessary arguments (Richard Guo) § §
Simplify IS [NOT] TRUE/FALSE/UNKNOWN to plain boolean expressions when the input is proven non-nullable (Richard Guo) §
Speed up join selectivity computations for large optimizer statistics targets (Ilia Evdokimov, David Geier) §
Add proper optimizer statistics for functions returning boolean values (Tom Lane) §
Allow extended statistics on virtual generated columns (Yugo Nagata) §
Allow function pg_restore_extended_stats() to restore optimizer extended statistics (Corey Huinker, Michael Paquier, Chao Li) § § § §
Add function pg_clear_extended_stats() to remove extended statistics (Corey Huinker, Michael Paquier) §
Adjust the optimizer to consider startup costs of partial paths (Robert Haas, Tomas Vondra) §
Allow negative values of pg_aggregate.aggtransspace to indicate unbounded memory usage (Richard Guo) §
This information is used by the optimizer in planning memory usage.
Improve performance of foreign key constraint checks (Junwang Zhao, Amit Langote, Chao Li) § §
Improve asynchronous I/O read-ahead scheduling for large requests (Andres Freund) § § §
Allow io_method method worker to automatically control needed background workers (Thomas Munro) §
The new server variables are io_min_workers, io_max_workers, io_worker_idle_timeout, and io_worker_launch_interval.
Allow query table scans to mark pages as all-visible in the visibility map (Melanie Plageman) §
Previously only VACUUM and COPY ... FREEZE could do this.
Allow autovacuum to use parallel autovacuum workers (Daniil Davydov) § §
The maximum number of workers is controlled by server variable autovacuum_max_parallel_workers and per-table storage parameter autovacuum_parallel_workers.
Allow TID Range Scans to be parallelized (Cary Huang, David Rowley) §
Improve COPY FROM performance for text and CSV input using SIMD CPU instructions (Nazir Bilal Yavuz, Shinya Kato) §
Improve NOTIFY to only wake up backends that are listening to specified notifications (Joel Jacobson) §
Previously most backends were woken by NOTIFY.
Change wal_compression equals on to use the best WAL compression method available (wenhui qiu) §
Previously this always used pglz.
Change the default TOAST compression method from pglz to the more efficient lz4 (Euler Taveira) §
This is done by changing the default for server variable default_toast_compression.
Improve performance of internal row deformation (David Rowley) §
Improve performance of repeated UTF-8 case-folding operations (Andreas Karlsson) §
Improve performance of hash index bulk-deletion and GIN index vacuuming using streaming reads (Xuneng Zhou) § §
Improve sort performance using radix sort (John Naylor) §
Improve timing performance measurements (Lukas Fittl, Andres Freund, David Geier) § §
This benefits EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, TIMING) and pg_test_timing, and is controlled via server variable timing_clock_source.
Optimize plpgsql syntax SELECT simple-expression INTO var (Tom Lane) §
Add system view pg_stat_lock and function pg_stat_get_lock() to report per-lock-type statistics (Bertrand Drouvot) §
Add system view pg_stat_recovery to report recovery status (Xuneng Zhou, Shinya Kato) § §

Add vacuum initiation details to system view pg_stat_progress_vacuum (Shinya Kato) §
The new started_by column reports the initiator of the vacuum, and mode indicates its aggressiveness.
Add analyze initiation details to system view pg_stat_progress_analyze (Shinya Kato) §
The new started_by column reports the initiator of the analyze.

Add mem_exceeded_count column to system view pg_stat_replication_slots (Bertrand Drouvot) §
This reports the number of times that logical_decoding_work_mem was exceeded.

This occurs during ALTER SUBSCRIPTION ... [ADD|SET|REFRESH|DROP] PUBLICATION. Function pg_get_sequence_data() allows inspection of sequence synchronization.

Amazon Fights the Delivery Protection Act With Homepage Banner, In-App Pop-Up
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
03:30:00 UTC
12:30:00 KST

Amazon Fights the Delivery Protection Act With Homepage Banner, In-App Pop-Up (arstechnica.com) 82
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from Ars Technica: In an unprecedented move, Amazon is using its app and website to try to convince shoppers to not support a New York City bill. When I log into Amazon today, I'm met with a vertical banner that reads: "ATTENTION NYC Council could add up to $5 per order" with a button that says "Act now." The message is delivered as a pop-up for users of the Amazon app, Bloomberg reported. Amazon hasn't specified whether the messaging targets NYC residents based on addresses saved in their Amazon account or uses other techniques, such as device location services, but I only see the banner if I'm logged into Amazon.
Amazon is referring to Introduction 0518-2026 (518), also known as the Delivery Protection Act. If passed, it would require Amazon to directly employ the third-party delivery contractors that it uses. Clicking on "Act now" on Amazon's homepage or in the app brings up a page that gives shoppers a way to contact their NYC Council member and further states: "Right now, Amazon works with a network of small delivery businesses that are deeply embedded in your communities. If this legislation passes, we'd no longer be able to work with them, and we'd be forced to consider moving our operations outside of the city. That would likely mean slower and more expensive delivery for millions of customers in New York City, while also threatening small businesses and their thousands of employees." The move marks an unusual expansion of Amazon's political advocacy, reports Ars, and raises concerns about the company's ability to influence shoppers and present a one-sided view of legislation.
The bill, which also affects companies like FedEx and DoorDash, remains stalled in a City Council committee, with no vote scheduled.
