What Comes After AGI?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
02:44:13 UTC
11:44:13 KST

What comes after AGI?
As multiple technological routes toward artificial general superintelligence take shape, the regime beyond AGI is shifting from philosophical speculation to empirical research and governance questions.
For decades, AI research has been focused on a monumental milestone: building artificial general intelligence, or AGI - machines that match humans across nearly all cognitive tasks. It was always clear that, in principle, intelligence could progress beyond human level, but the prospect of AGI was far enough off, and surrounded by ample uncertainties of its own, that looking past it felt premature.
Now that AGI is coming within reach, we can no longer put off seriously considering what comes next. While far from certain, AI could continue to advance at its current pace - and possibly even accelerate - towards artificial general superintelligence, or ASI. It could have a profound impact on science, the economy, and society at a scale and speed much greater than AGI itself. Preparing for that potential shift requires mapping the possible roads from AGI to ASI and building rigorous, reliable ways to track progress.
The continuum of general intelligence
To examine how the development of advanced AI might unfold, it helps to think of general intelligence, whether biological or artificial, as existing on a continuum. Human-level AGI is just one point along that spectrum.
ASI sits further along as an advanced system that consistently outperforms large groups of human experts on tasks that could take them years to complete, using fewer resources and less time. Examples would be making the same scientific progress as an entire research field in a fraction of the time, or achieving technological developments, such as in chip design, on par with what large companies can do in a year. ASI would need to meet this bar across virtually all tasks of human interest, not just some in narrow domains (which is already the case with some AI systems today that can beat any human in Chess and Go, predict the structure of proteins from their amino acid sequences, or prove theorems that resisted human mathematicians for decades).
Interestingly, we have a good mathematical understanding of the very upper end of the intelligence continuum - the theoretical limits of general intelligence. We also know about hard physical, logical, and complexity-theoretic limits to what AI can achieve, such as predicting how real-world complex systems - from climate to immune systems to human societies - play out with arbitrary precision.
Yet knowing those upper limits doesn’t tell us what comes before them. Even in theory, it’s hard to predict how capable a ‘good enough’ approximation of general intelligence can be. For example, solving chess perfectly requires far more computing power than exists on Earth, yet a thumbnail-sized chip can play the game well enough to defeat any human.
What we do know is that fundamental limits lie far beyond both today’s AI systems and humans. But the vast space between human-level AI and theoretical limits to intelligence, which encompasses expert AGI and a whole regime of more and more advanced superintelligent systems, is largely uncharted.
Cognitive industrialization
Even if you are pessimistic that progress at the frontier of AI capabilities will continue, consider what happens if AI hardware and software continue to become more efficient.
Over the last ten years, AI progress was fueled by steady growth in effective compute, driven by three compounding factors:
- Hardware manufacturing improvements: Under Moore's law and related trends, compute per dollar has increased by roughly 1.5x per year for six decades - meaning the same investment 18 months from now buys twice the compute.
- Algorithmic efficiency: The amount of training compute (FLOPs) required to reach a fixed AI performance level has fallen at roughly 3–6x per year over the last decade, while algorithmic efficiency at inference improved by roughly 3x per year. Doubling efficiency is practically equivalent to doubling the stock of physical compute.
- Compute hardware investment: Spending on AI hardware has grown by roughly 2.5x per year over the past decade. For example, the training compute of the largest frontier training runs grows by more than 4x per year.
Multiplying all three factors gives an overall growth rate of global effective compute stock of at least 10x per year - a factor of 10 billion over ten years. Imagine that this trend holds. A decade from now, for each single AI agent you could run today, you would hypothetically be able to run more agents of the same quality than there are people in the world.
At the same time, continued hardware and software (inference) efficiency improvements alone would reduce the compute demand to run an AI workload by about 4x per year. A cognitive workload that can be performed by today’s AI systems for $1 billion in compute tokens would cost just $1000 in ten years. Looking only at the last few years gives even steeper estimates: a recent Epoch study finds the consumer price for achieving a fixed score on a set of benchmarks has fallen by more than 13x per year since 2023.
Extrapolating both trends into the next decade raises the prospect of a dramatically larger global effective compute stock combined with equally dramatic cost reductions for cognitive work performed by AI. Increasingly, many cognitive tasks may therefore soon become commoditised. Much like mechanical industrialization turned routine physical labor into a commodity, this shift will lead to cognitive industrialization characterized by rapidly increasing standardization and automation of cognitive work across sectors, industrial-scale infrastructure build-out, and an abundance of (certain) cognitive outputs and services.
Is such a dramatic proliferation of intelligence realistic? It’s certainly not a given, but practical ceilings on compute build-out, and more speculative ceilings on algorithmic efficiency, together might allow the effective population of today’s AI agents to scale up by more than a thousand-fold by 2030, and more than a billion-fold within ten years from now.
Would cognitive industrialization alone be enough to achieve ASI? If we froze other dimensions of AI progress and only scaled up today’s AIs in speed and number, the result might not even count as full AGI: there may still be cognitive tasks that AI cannot do without human help. Nonetheless, sheer scale can have a quality of its own. Suppose that we do achieve broadly human-level AI. Then there is clearly a major difference between human-level AIs that are slow and expensive to run, and a world where billions of agents act much faster than humans. That quantitative leap in speed and scale might itself be enough to bridge the qualitative gap from AGI to (weak) ASI.
Unlike humans, digital intelligence directly benefits from more and faster compute in several fundamental ways:
- Processing speed: AI systems can reason, ingest data, and generate outputs at increasingly high rates.
- Working memory and memorization: Today’s models can literally memorize large parts of the internet and their working memory capacity far exceeds human limits.
- Substrate independence: The same AI system can migrate from one computer to another, upgrade to faster or more energy-efficient hardware, or run across heterogeneous, spatially distributed fleets.
- Lossless replication: Digital intelligence can be copied nearly instantly and without loss - not only its source code (akin to DNA), but also its memory state (our lifetime experience). Specialist systems can be trained once, then spawned in millions of copies when needed.
- High-bandwidth sharing of experiences: Humans must strongly compress experience and knowledge in order to teach others; AIs can replay stored high-bandwidth digital input-output streams across an entire population, and homogeneous AI groups could share raw learning signals directly.
Four pathways from AGI to ASI
Dramatically more efficient AI and larger compute stocks can scale up the speed and number of AI agents at a given capability level. But some missing capabilities may be hard - or impossible - to patch over with sheer volume. While Einstein did figure out general relativity, a conference of one hundred thousand Ptolemaic astronomers probably wouldn’t have.
AI could follow four potential technological pathways to unlock previously unavailable cognitive capabilities. (For an in-depth discussion, see our From AGI to ASI report.)
Scaling models and data
So far, qualitative capabilities have improved with remarkable consistency alongside model and data scale. This trend is captured by scaling laws that relate model performance to training compute, training data, and more recently, test-time compute (giving a model extra computing power to reason through a problem before answering).
These scaling “laws,” however, are extrapolations of observed trends that hold only within familiar boundaries, such as a particular model architecture, type of training data, and family of evaluations. They can’t confidently predict the scale required for AGI or ASI, but we can identify potential bottlenecks to continued scaling of models and data:
- Data exhaustion: The need for larger amounts of high-quality training data is outpacing humanity’s ability to produce more of it. ‘Synthetic’ data could help, but not all data produced by AI models is useful for training. To overcome this ‘data wall,’ researchers could rely on high-fidelity simulations, generate data within interactive (RL) environments, and use test-time compute to create better training data for future AI models.
- Diminishing returns on model scale: Each linear step up in model capability typically requires multiplying, not just adding, training compute and model size - but bigger models are slower, thus bottlenecking test-time scaling methods such as chain-of-thought reasoning.
- Physical and economic resource limits: Sustaining multi-order-of-magnitude increases in training compute runs up against real-world limits in building power plants, electrical grids, chip fabrication plants, and low-latency hardware interconnects. It remains unclear whether hardware and software efficiency gains - the main trends underlying cognitive industrialization - can compensate for this.
Technological paradigm shifts
Today’s dominant AI paradigm is to train large transformers to predict the next symbol in a long sequence across vast datasets, then refine them to learn to reason and follow instructions, and give them extra test-time compute. Recent research suggests this paradigm can, in principle, produce models that can run complex algorithms, including powerful learning algorithms, via their prompts - that is, without requiring retraining. This is a key ingredient for building AI systems at the theoretical upper limit of intelligence.
But today’s models fall short of humans on a few fundamental dimensions, including continual learning, certain forms of world modeling, decision-making, and sample efficiency - meaning that AI models need to be initially trained on much more data than humans to reach roughly the same intelligence level. They may also be missing the ability to invent genuinely new concepts and abstractions rather than recombining existing ones. Researchers are divided on whether these problems can be solved with simple extensions and evolutions of today’s paradigm, or whether reaching ASI will require truly deep technological paradigm shifts.
Improving agent coordination
Human corporations, markets, and research communities solve problems that no individual could tackle alone. Similarly, coordinating thousands or millions of AGI instances into "group agents" or virtual agent economies could produce collective superintelligence. What is unclear today is how AI group intelligence could scale as we add more agents and compute, depending on how agents are organized. That is, we need multi-agent scaling laws. Early research finds clear intelligence gains in some tasks, but coordination inefficiencies still lead to diminishing returns as the population size grows.
Less obviously, steering large agent collectives will require robust institutional design and organizational mechanisms, including defenses against accidental or intentional misinformation spread, manipulation, and collusion. Experiments with agent swarms are already demonstrating how quickly these dynamics can emerge, with individual agents learning to cheat and others acting as whistleblowers to expose them.
Recursive self-improvement (RSI)
Finally, AI systems could begin automating improvements to future generations of AI - a process known as recursive self-improvement (RSI) that was first discussed by I. J. Good and Ray Solomonoff.
Unlike typical technological development, improvements to AI could directly speed up subsequent gains, leading to an accelerating rate of progress. However, further improvements in research tend to be “harder to find” - more and more effort is required to maintain the same rate of improvement across technologies. Whether RSI leads to ‘runaway’ progress is largely an empirical question.
RSI is not a single process. It can operate via improvements to code and designs - from model architectures and training algorithms to chip layouts. It can also operate through data, by spending compute at deployment to produce improved results and outputs that can be reused to train future models. AlphaZero self-improved purely in this data driven way to reach superhuman performance.
Finally, RSI can play out at the group level by dividing labor and specializing roles in an agent collective. A group of coordinated specialists can achieve more than the sum of what they could achieve as isolated generalists, in turn becoming more effective at finding further improvements. Instead of just scaling up a team with fixed roles, larger and more efficient collectives enable new types of specialization that were previously unviable - much like how a multi-cellular organism can support specialized organs like a heart or a brain, as opposed to a single-celled organism that perform all survival functions on its own.
These three RSI mechanisms mirror evolutionary processes that drove the growth of human cognitive capabilities: genetic (code and design), cultural (data), and social evolution (group specialization through cooperation).
Importantly, RSI loops can easily stall or degenerate if the feedback guiding them is poor. Pursuing ASI via recursive self-improvement therefore requires evaluation methods that can be rapidly scaled into the superhuman regime, where even large groups of humans can't easily assess AI outputs. Accordingly, RSI should advance fastest in domains that allow for automated verification without humans in the loop, such as mathematical proofs, while other domains might lag behind or taper off sooner.
The many roads to ASI
These pathways to ASI are not mutually exclusive - in fact, they compound on one another. If progress on one stalls, economic and geopolitical competition will likely shift effort toward the others. Even if RSI doesn’t result in runaway gains, continued scaling in training data, speed, efficiency, and coordination could still drive rapid progress well beyond human-level AGI
Because there are multiple routes to superintelligence, trying to slow down AI development by restricting just one area - like the computing power for training an AI model - won't be enough to stop overall progress. Equally, suppressing a single dimension risks creating a capability “overhang”: if multiple inputs need to be combined to make progress, holding back one dimension while the others move forward would lead to sudden, steep jumps in progress when the suppressed dimension is relaxed (or overcome otherwise).
Taking all these arguments together, continued fast AI progress over the next decade is a serious possibility. It could take us relatively smoothly from today, to Expert AGI, and then into the superhuman AI regime, accelerating science and enabling breakthroughs in fields like medicine, materials, and clean energy. Even in the absence of dramatic frontier AI progress, cognitive industrialization alone could reshape knowledge work and the broader economy. Regardless of which path AI progress takes, we must anticipate far-reaching opportunities along with equally transformative pressures to many aspects of human society.
None of these predictions are certain, but the pathways and scenarios are also far from unfounded hype or fiction. So what can be done? Our best course of action may sound familiar: model, measure, forecast, and repeat. While those steps aren’t surprising, the complexity and scale of carrying them out on the road to ASI is entirely unprecedented. It requires large-scale and highly-interdisciplinary efforts across both frontier labs and national and global institutions. Many of the key metrics for tracking progress on these pathways require frontier-model access and sizable amounts of evaluation compute, alongside new benchmarking and modelling methodologies that could spawn entirely new research fields.
Human-level AGI has long been the horizon for AI research, but it is far from the upper limit of intelligence. Understanding what could come next - and its implications across science, industry, and society - is the grand challenge ahead for researchers, developers, and policymakers.
Acknowledgements
This essay is based on the technical report From AGI to ASI, co-authored with Matija Franklin, Alexander Lerchner, Laurent Orseau, Samuel Albanie, Adam Bales, Cole Wyeth, Stephanie Chan, Iason Gabriel, Joel Z. Leibo, Allan Dafoe, Marcus Hutter, Thore Graepel, and Shane Legg, as well as subsequent discussions within the DeepMind Institute team. Thanks also to Nenad Tomašev, Seb Krier, Davide Paglieri, Sasha Vezhnevets, Alison Snyder, and Fin Moorhouse for helpful discussions and feedback.

====================================================================================================

What Comes After AGI?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
02:44:13 UTC
11:44:13 KST

What comes after AGI?
As multiple technological routes toward artificial general superintelligence take shape, the regime beyond AGI is shifting from philosophical speculation to empirical research and governance questions.
For decades, AI research has been focused on a monumental milestone: building artificial general intelligence, or AGI - machines that match humans across nearly all cognitive tasks. It was always clear that, in principle, intelligence could progress beyond human level, but the prospect of AGI was far enough off, and surrounded by ample uncertainties of its own, that looking past it felt premature.
Now that AGI is coming within reach, we can no longer put off seriously considering what comes next. While far from certain, AI could continue to advance at its current pace - and possibly even accelerate - towards artificial general superintelligence, or ASI. It could have a profound impact on science, the economy, and society at a scale and speed much greater than AGI itself. Preparing for that potential shift requires mapping the possible roads from AGI to ASI and building rigorous, reliable ways to track progress.
The continuum of general intelligence
To examine how the development of advanced AI might unfold, it helps to think of general intelligence, whether biological or artificial, as existing on a continuum. Human-level AGI is just one point along that spectrum.
ASI sits further along as an advanced system that consistently outperforms large groups of human experts on tasks that could take them years to complete, using fewer resources and less time. Examples would be making the same scientific progress as an entire research field in a fraction of the time, or achieving technological developments, such as in chip design, on par with what large companies can do in a year. ASI would need to meet this bar across virtually all tasks of human interest, not just some in narrow domains (which is already the case with some AI systems today that can beat any human in Chess and Go, predict the structure of proteins from their amino acid sequences, or prove theorems that resisted human mathematicians for decades).
Interestingly, we have a good mathematical understanding of the very upper end of the intelligence continuum - the theoretical limits of general intelligence. We also know about hard physical, logical, and complexity-theoretic limits to what AI can achieve, such as predicting how real-world complex systems - from climate to immune systems to human societies - play out with arbitrary precision.
Yet knowing those upper limits doesn’t tell us what comes before them. Even in theory, it’s hard to predict how capable a ‘good enough’ approximation of general intelligence can be. For example, solving chess perfectly requires far more computing power than exists on Earth, yet a thumbnail-sized chip can play the game well enough to defeat any human.
What we do know is that fundamental limits lie far beyond both today’s AI systems and humans. But the vast space between human-level AI and theoretical limits to intelligence, which encompasses expert AGI and a whole regime of more and more advanced superintelligent systems, is largely uncharted.
Cognitive industrialization
Even if you are pessimistic that progress at the frontier of AI capabilities will continue, consider what happens if AI hardware and software continue to become more efficient.
Over the last ten years, AI progress was fueled by steady growth in effective compute, driven by three compounding factors:
- Hardware manufacturing improvements: Under Moore's law and related trends, compute per dollar has increased by roughly 1.5x per year for six decades - meaning the same investment 18 months from now buys twice the compute.
- Algorithmic efficiency: The amount of training compute (FLOPs) required to reach a fixed AI performance level has fallen at roughly 3–6x per year over the last decade, while algorithmic efficiency at inference improved by roughly 3x per year. Doubling efficiency is practically equivalent to doubling the stock of physical compute.
- Compute hardware investment: Spending on AI hardware has grown by roughly 2.5x per year over the past decade. For example, the training compute of the largest frontier training runs grows by more than 4x per year.

At the same time, continued hardware and software (inference) efficiency improvements alone would reduce the compute demand to run an AI workload by about 4x per year. Increasingly, many cognitive tasks may therefore soon become commoditised. This fixes the buffer overflow in `EVP_EncodeBlock()` (CVE-2026-121481)
by returning `-1` for invalid inputs. It also provides a similar guard
for `EVP_DecodeBlock()`, and fixes the incorrect length check in the
`base64encode()` function of `http_client.c`. Also updates the
documentation and adds new tests.

This fixes the buffer overflow in `EVP_EncodeBlock()` (CVE-2026-121481)
by calling `OPENSSL_assert()` for inputs that lead to undefined
behavior. The same fix is applied to `EVP_DecodeBlock()`, and the
incorrect length check in `base64encode()` of `http_client.c`
is fixed. Also updates the documentation and adds new tests to document
the existing behavior for zero-length inputs.

by calling `OPENSSL_assert()` for inputs that lead to undefined
behavior. The same fix is applied to `EVP_DecodeBlock()`, and the
incorrect length check in `base64encode()` of `http_client.c`

      OpenSSL 4.x AVX2 encoder (openssl/openssl#33652 area) vs scalar via the
public API, toggling OPENSSL_ia32cap_P[2] bit 5; decode round trip as
second oracle. Mismatch raises SIGABRT (shim swallows abort()).
Vendor default 4.1.0, stale-tree guard, version in build stamp, asm
libcrypto variant, apps/include workaround for 4.1.0 Configure.
Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5.5 <noreply@anthropic.com>
Claude-Session: https://claude.ai/code/session_01XPHAxcmCVco7xEZjip8Axk
    
  atomchild411 
      added a commit
        to atomchild411/pkgsrc
      that referenced
      this pull request
    
      Nov 20, 2026 
    
    
      
  
    
      Backport of the guards in openssl/openssl#33652
(head ad8169a9bb, not merged yet; advisory pending) to 3.6.5:
- EVP_EncodeBlock(): stop on a negative length or one whose base64
  encoding does not fit in an int (dlen > (INT_MAX / 4) * 3), where the
  output length overflowed; an empty input writes only the NUL.
- EVP_DecodeBlock(): 0 for an empty input, -1 for a negative length.
- http_client.c base64encode(): reject lengths whose encoding would not
  fit in an int, not only len > INT_MAX (reachable through a long
  s_client -proxy_pass).
Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5.5 <noreply@anthropic.com>

Vint Cerf on why TCP/IP was never patented
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
01:06:12 UTC
10:06:12 KST

EXTRA, extra! Today I’m launching Future Stories, my own podcast about technology, science and the way we live.
The first guest is Vint Cerf. He co-designed TCP/IP with Bob Kahn in the 1970s, the rules that let the world’s networks talk to each other. Interviewing him was on my bucket list for years. When I was at Cloudflare I thought about asking him many times, but I was a bit shy. He’s Vint Cerf, after all. This year I finally asked, and he said yes.
We talked over Zoom (not using Zoom again) on October 14. He’s 83, he left Google this year after more than twenty years, and he’s helping start a company, Tolero, that is building software for an Internet between planets. His words: “the speed of light is too slow.”
“Who will our allies be 25 years from now? And we didn’t know. So we thought the only way we can be assured that the allies will have access to this is to simply open it up.”
Some peculiar stories from the conversation
First, a bug related one (ring a bell? those never stopped over the years…). Vint once spent two weeks on a program that failed at random, so he couldn’t reproduce it. He finally asked his best friend, Steve Crocker, to step through it with him. At one point Crocker said “that’s the problem”, and he was right. Thirty seconds, after two weeks. Vint’s takeaway is that this is why collaboration matters.
Then a bottle of glitter. In late 1989 three commercial Internet services started in the United States, and one of them was called CERFnet, in San Diego. They first wanted to call it SURFnet, because surfing the Internet, but a network in the Netherlands had a name too close to it. So they asked Vint if they could use his.
He had a moment of “what if they screw it up?”, then decided that people name kids after other people and nobody blames the person they were named after. At the launch, he and the network’s director smashed a plastic bottle of glitter over a Cisco router.
He also tried to connect Minitel, the French online service, to MCI Mail, the email service he built in the 1980s. MCI Mail was designed to link to everything. It reached the telegraph system, which he calls 19th-century stuff, and fax. It even reached the post office: type a postal address into the email and they printed it, put it in an envelope and mailed it. Minitel refused to connect, and he says he still doesn’t understand why.
One more, from outside the interview: In 2020, someone on the Internet-history mailing list (I’m on it) asked if anyone had ever seen Vint without a three-piece suit. One reply: “I have seen Vint in a t-shirt!” Vint answered with just a photo, “IP on Everything”, from a 1992 engineers’ meeting where, in his words, he “felt compelled” to do an “IP on Everything” striptease. The thread is public.
We didn’t go very deep into AI. Time was short, and it’s not his main field these days. But even there, he has a clear way of thinking about it. And near the end he shows a piece of the original ARPANET on camera, and talks about his worry that the next century won’t be able to read what we leave behind.
“I’m worried that the 22nd century won’t know what happened in the 21st because all of our records will have evaporated or will be uninterpretable.”
The full conversation is 56 minutes, with chapters:
You can also read the transcript at ourfuturestories.eu/vint-cerf, or listen in your favourite podcast app: ourfuturestories.eu/subscribe. A Portuguese article about it is on SAPO. Short clips are coming in the next days.
This is a passion project. The idea is to tell good stories, the kind that hold up over time. If you like it, subscribe, share it with a friend, and tell me who I should talk to next, you can do it here: ourfuturestories.eu/#contact.
Stay tuned, geek out!
———
Fun fact: in 2024 I did a segment on the 50 years of the TCP paper on my previous podcast, This Week in NET at Cloudflare. My guests were three then-colleagues: Mark Nottingham (standards), Marwan Fayed (research) and Lucas Pardue (web protocols, co-chair of the IETF’s QUIC working group). You can listen here (or below).
The paper is “A Protocol for Packet Network Intercommunication”, by Vint Cerf and Bob Kahn, published in May 1974 in IEEE Transactions on Communications. It described TCP, a way to join many packet networks into a network of networks. TCP was later split into TCP and IP, and in 1983 they became the core of the Internet that DARPA launched.

====================================================================================================

Iowans Brace For Lead Dust Cloud As Army Plans to Detonate 5 Tons of Bomb-Making Chemicals
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
01:00:00 UTC
10:00:00 KST

Iowans Brace For Lead Dust Cloud As Army Plans to Detonate 5 Tons of Bomb-Making Chemicals (pbs.org) 96
			
		 	
				The U.S. Army plans to detonate roughly five tons of unstable lead azide explosives at the Iowa Army Ammunition Plant on Friday. "The detonation will result in an air plume with some lead-containing particles which will disperse in the air and deposit on the ground," an advisory states. "Lead is toxic to humans and animals. The goal of this advisory is to provide the public with actionable information out of an abundance of caution to minimize lead exposure." PBS reports:  Officials have released maps showing the potential drift north, and the Army will continue to update the maps based on weather. Some schools in the region have already canceled classes. Nearly 400 people live in Middletown, but thousands more could be affected. The vibrations from the two blasts will be quick but could break old windows in town. The resulting dust is expected to linger for no more than one hour, depending on wind speed, officials said. While the Army recommends cleaning cars, windowsills and outdoor surfaces, residents have taken to social media to voice concern and question whether that will be enough.
[...] Middletown residents learned in March that the Army needed to dispose of several dozen barrels containing lead azide, after the ammunition plant paused operations as part of a review and the degraded material was discovered. "The safety of our people and our neighbors is non-negotiable," Lt. Col. Daniel Nosse, commander of the plant, said at the time, acknowledging the volatility of the chemical compound used in munitions production. The Army shared more details during a public meeting in Middletown in October, advising that a detonation was planned for an unspecified date in November.

Apple may be developing its own AI agent that runs your Mac
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
00:56:01 UTC
09:56:01 KST

Apple appears to be working on its own AI agent to conduct computer work on behalf of users — including using apps, accessing websites, and coding, among other things.
The news was originally reported by Bloomberg, which spotted a job listing the iPhone maker recently posted for a senior engineering manager to oversee the creation of an “AI agent that can use a computer.”
“Imagine an AI agent that can use a computer the way a capable person does,” the posting says. “It browses the web, works across apps, runs commands, writes and debugs code, and calls services, and it chooses the right tool for each step. It does all this privately and with the polish people expect from Apple.”
The posting additionally notes that the agents should be able to move “fluidly between tools” and “carry out multi-step tasks across apps, browsers, command lines, code, and APIs.”
TechCrunch reached out to Apple for more information but did not receive a response.
Agents capable of computer work have become one of the hottest commodities in the tech industry over the past year. Major labs like Anthropic and OpenAI have launched such services, while other major tech brands — like Meta, which recently launched its Muse agent line — have also moved into the space. It makes sense that Apple — which is trying to expand its AI offerings in the new John Ternus era — would want to follow suit.
Bloomberg additionally notes that, at the same time that Apple is working on its own digital assistant, the company is making it significantly harder for third-party agents to gain total control over its computers.
The outlet notes that the version of its upcoming macOS 27.3 will deploy warnings discouraging users from granting full access to third-party agents. In October, Apple issued a notification to developers stating that the company planned to “introduce additional controls to ensure that users who genuinely wish to grant an app this extraordinary level of access can only do so with very explicit user action.”
Bloomberg writes that while the iPhone maker “has eyed introducing the service as soon as the end of next year,” progress on its in-house agent “remains in the early stages.”

====================================================================================================

Anthropic’s 7 founders cashed out nearly $2B in record-breaking IPO
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
00:43:50 UTC
09:43:50 KST

Anthropic became a public company on Thursday, raising a total of $81.3 billion, including $1.95 billion for its seven founders. As we previously reported, each of them ponied up about 3.4 million shares apiece to sell in the IPO.
At the upgraded IPO price of $83 per share, that means they each pocketed about $280 million before underwriting fees and taxes. It looked like they had calculated the number of shares they planned to sell to give them each around $250 million if shares hit $74. That was the midpoint price from Anthropic’s original price range. So that’s a cool $30-ish million more for each of them than perhaps they were gunning for.
Anthropic implied in its IPO filings that the founders aren’t entirely in this for the money though. The company said that each of the founders has pledged to dedicate 80% of their personal equity in the company to philanthropy.
However, if any of them struggle to live on their quarter of a billion, they can scale back their charitable commitments. The company points out that these commitments are “voluntary, revocable expressions of each co-founder’s philanthropic intent and do not constitute legally binding obligations.”
With the IPO in the record books, there were a few other interesting numbers worth highlighting.
$104.8 billion. That’s really the amount Anthropic raised for its coffers (with a possibility of $117 billion, if the bankers buy all of their allotment of shares). In addition to selling its Class A common stock, it also sold a previously announced $26 billion worth of a special non-voting Class N stock to some of the biggest tech names in the industry.
Nvidia bought $10 billion of these Class N shares, Google bought $10 billion, and AMD, Broadcom, Micron, Microsoft, and TSMC divvied up the rest.
Just for comparison: When Facebook had its then record-breaking IPO in 2012, it raised $16 billion on IPO day.
28% stake to Amazon and Google combined. All told, at the higher IPO share price, Google wound up owning 13.4% of the company. Amazon also now has a big stake in Anthropic because when it previously invested in the AI lab as a startup, it was granted promissory notes that could be converted to equity. It converted a small percentage of them as part of the IPO, leaving it with a 14.6% stake. Had it converted all of them, Amazon could have owned nearly 24%.
The two large cloud providers now own 28% of Anthropic, the AI lab says.
More than $1 trillion. As mind-blowing as it is to raise nearly $105 billion, giving Anthropic nearly $230 billion of working capital available (along with capital it already had on hand), the company’s need for future capital is still far greater.
Anthropic needs about $70.7 billion just to cover its compute and infrastructure spending commitments in the next 12 months as of the quarter ended September 30, 2026. It needs $451.6 billion to cover these commitments before the end of 2030. And it has racked up hundreds of billions of other commitments that are either conditional (and may not happen) or haven’t been priced yet.
All told, the company has disclosed a ballpark of $1.05 trillion to $1.08 trillion in infrastructure commitments in its foreseeable future. This was a huge IPO. But it is a pricey business to run.

====================================================================================================

Postgres 19 is out, and it's available on Neon and Databricks Lakebase
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
00:37:31 UTC
09:37:31 KST

Try the new features: REPACK, query plan advice, WAIT FOR LSN, and more
PostgreSQL 19 reached GA today and it's already available on Lakebase. New major versions often take weeks to reach other managed PostgreSQL services. Instead, we wanted to give access to it to our customers the same day.
Several engineers on the Lakebase team are PostgreSQL committers and contributors, and they worked on this release directly, from query plan advice to shared memory, locale handling, and release management. To try it, create a new Lakebase project and select PostgreSQL 19 as the version.
PostgreSQL 19 is a practical release. There is no single headline feature that changes what PostgreSQL is. Instead, version 19 goes after operational problems that database teams know well: bloated tables, unstable query plans, replica lag, incomplete statistics, and maintenance work that is hard to run safely in production.
The common thread is control. PostgreSQL 19 gives you more ways to step in when the defaults are not enough, without giving up the project's bias toward reliability and predictable behavior.
The full list of changes is in the PostgreSQL 19 release notes.
PostgreSQL uses multiversion concurrency control, so an UPDATE creates a new row version and a DELETE leaves a dead tuple behind. Routine vacuuming makes that space reusable, but it doesn't necessarily return space to the operating system or restore a useful physical row order. Over time, heavily updated tables can become bloated and poorly clustered.
The usual fixes have been VACUUM FULL, CLUSTER, or external tools such as pg_repack. The built-in commands work, but they take an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock for the whole operation, blocking normal access to the table.
PostgreSQL 19 adds REPACK, which reclaims space while normal reads and writes continue during most of the operation. For example, a chat application can rebuild its messages table while clustering rows by conversation and send time using an existing index:
REPACK reclaims space like VACUUM FULL; with USING INDEX, it also physically orders rows like CLUSTER. With CONCURRENTLY, PostgreSQL rebuilds the table while normal reads and writes continue, capturing ongoing changes through logical decoding. It then takes an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock to apply any remaining captured changes and swap the new table and index files into place. This phase is typically brief but can take longer on a busy table.
There are operational constraints. Concurrent repacking creates an additional replication slot for the duration of the operation, so it needs free capacity under max_repack_replication_slots and a table with either a non-deferrable primary key or a suitable index-based replica identity. It also needs temporary disk space for a copy of the table and all its indexes, potentially plus a sort file up to the size of the table. Tables with heavy update and delete traffic face an additional limit: the current upstream PostgreSQL 19 implementation fails if more than about 104 million tuples in the target table are updated or deleted while REPACK (CONCURRENTLY) is running. This counts changes made during the repack, not rows already in the table.
Not every relation type is eligible. Long-running transactions can delay the final lock. Concurrent repacking is also not MVCC-safe: after it commits, transactions using snapshots taken before the repack began can see the table as empty. Physical clustering also degrades as later writes arrive.
REPACK CONCURRENTLY still needs planning, but it brings into core a job that previously required more downtime or an extension.
The PostgreSQL cost-based optimizer uses table statistics and cost estimates to choose an execution plan. Most of the time, that works well.
Statistics describe individual tables well, but real datasets often contain relationships that are harder to capture. Imagine that enterprise customers make up 0.1% of customers but generate 30% of orders. Per-table statistics cannot capture that cross-table correlation.
With an index on orders.customer_id, the planner may estimate that each enterprise customer has only a few orders and choose a nested loop that repeatedly probes the index. If those customers actually account for 30% of orders, each lookup returns far more rows than expected, and a hash join may be more appropriate.
PostgreSQL 19 adds pg_plan_advice and pg_stash_advice for these situations:
pg_plan_advice describes planner decisions as advice, applies supplied advice, and reports whether it was followed.pg_stash_advice stores advice by query ID and applies it automatically when a matching query is planned
These components do not add cross-table statistics or replace the planner. Instead, they let you intervene when an inaccurate estimate produces a poor plan.
Consider a query that counts orders from enterprise customers:
Here, c is the alias for the filtered customers table. The following advice asks PostgreSQL to place those customers on the inner side of a hash join:
When PostgreSQL can apply the advice, EXPLAIN reports feedback similar to:
matched means the resulting plan complies with the advice. It does not mean the new plan is necessarily better; you still need to evaluate it against the actual workload.
Unlike inline optimizer hints, plan advice lives outside the application’s query text. You can inspect the current plan, test focused advice, and then stash it by query ID.
pg_stash_advice is the component that applies stashed advice automatically. Create the extension in each database before using its stash-management functions:
Create a stash and associate the tested advice with the query ID reported by EXPLAIN (VERBOSE). Replace <query_id> with the identifier from your plan:
Configure the database to use the stash for new connections:
Replace appdb with the name of your database.
Treat plan advice as a stabilizer once you've diagnosed the problem. Data distributions change, cached prepared statements may keep using an older plan for a while, and the optimizer is still right more often than it is wrong.
When the planner unexpectedly switches a critical query to a worse plan, PostgreSQL 19 gives you a supported way to stabilize it.
Autovacuum can now use parallel workers when vacuuming indexes, controlled by autovacuum_max_parallel_workers.  A new scoring system prioritizes eligible tables based on how urgently they need vacuuming or analysis.
New statistics views expose more of what the server is doing:
pg_stat_lock reports statistics by lock type.pg_stat_recovery shows recovery status.pg_stat_autovacuum_scores shows how autovacuum prioritizes tables.
Query-level diagnostics improve as well. EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, IO) reports asynchronous I/O activity, and auto_explain.log_io can include the same information in logged plans.
PostgreSQL 19 also reports bytes written for full-page images in pg_stat_wal, EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, WAL), and vacuum and analyze logs.
Together, these make performance investigations less dependent on inference. You can see why maintenance work was prioritized, where asynchronous I/O happened, and how many WAL bytes came from full-page images.
Logical replication has become core infrastructure for change data capture, migrations, real-time serving, and ETL pipelines. PostgreSQL 19 closes several long-standing gaps:
wal_level is replica. Creating the first valid logical replication slot raises effective_wal_level to logical, showing the WAL level currently in effect.FOR ALL SEQUENCES.CREATE PUBLICATION ... FOR ALL TABLES EXCEPT (...) publishes all tables except a named set.postgres_fdw server with CREATE SUBSCRIPTION ... SERVER.retain_dead_tuples subscription option preserves enough information to detect update_deleted conflicts, where a replicated update targets a row deleted by another origin. Detection also requires track_commit_timestamp on the subscriber, and pg_stat_subscription_stats counts these conflicts.
Sequence synchronization removes an awkward migration edge case, especially during major-version upgrades that use logical replication. Copying table rows without advancing their sequences can leave a promoted subscriber generating values that already exist, causing duplicate-key errors.
PostgreSQL copies the initial sequence values when the subscription is created with copy_data = true, the default. Later sequence changes are not streamed continuously, so refresh them immediately before cutover:
This updates the sequences already known to the subscription to their current values on the publisher. If the publication has gained new sequences, use ALTER SUBSCRIPTION ... REFRESH PUBLICATION to add them and synchronize their values before cutover.
Asynchronous read replicas add read capacity without making primary commits wait for them. The tradeoff is replication lag. An application can commit a write on the primary, immediately read from a replica, and not find the row because the replica has not replayed the relevant WAL yet.
PostgreSQL 19 adds a direct way to coordinate that handoff. An LSN identifies a position in the PostgreSQL WAL, and WAIT FOR LSN blocks the standby until replay reaches at least the requested position:
By default, WAIT FOR LSN waits indefinitely for the standby to replay the requested LSN. Other modes can wait for WAL to be written or flushed, and you can set a timeout.
The client or connection pooler still has to carry the LSN from the primary to the replica. The database now provides the missing synchronization step, so read-your-writes behavior no longer needs polling or arbitrary sleeps.
Foreign data wrappers let PostgreSQL query remote systems as if their data were local. Planning those queries has always been hard when the local optimizer does not know the remote table’s size or data distribution.
PostgreSQL 19 lets postgres_fdw import statistics from the remote server:
ANALYZE imports the existing remote statistics into the local server. If the import fails, postgres_fdw falls back to row sampling. The remote statistics therefore need to be current.
Without remote statistics, PostgreSQL may rely on fallback size estimates and choose a nested loop. After importing them, the planner can estimate row counts more accurately and may choose a hash join instead.
Federated queries still pay network and remote-system costs, but accurate row estimates remove a major source of avoidable bad plans.
INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO SELECT gives you a clean get-or-create pattern that returns both existing and newly inserted rows:
It replaces the DO UPDATE SET col = EXCLUDED.col workaround, which performs an update even though no values change. That creates a new row version and leaves a dead tuple on every conflict.
COPY TO can now emit JSON directly. By default, it writes one object per line, while FORCE_ARRAY wraps the output in a single JSON array:
The new JSON format is export-only, so COPY FROM does not accept it.
Lastly, a few compact changes with a quality-of-life payoff:
btrim(), lower(), replace(), and split_part().random(min, max) family now supports date, timestamp, and timestamptz, which helps with tests and synthetic datasets.IGNORE NULLS and RESPECT NULLS.io_method = worker, I/O workers scale automatically between io_min_workers and io_max_workers. Foreign-key checks are faster, and the planner and executor get another round of targeted optimizations.
Several major features landed during PostgreSQL 19 development but were removed before release. Reviewers found correctness or design problems that were too risky to resolve late in the cycle, so the community held the features for a later release.
Some of the features that finally won't ship in 19:
SQL/PGQ property graph queries. CREATE PROPERTY GRAPH and GRAPH_TABLE brought graph pattern matching over regular tables into SQL. Reverted on September 7, 2026.UPDATE and DELETE ... FOR PORTION OF. Temporal updates and deletes that split rows to preserve history outside the targeted time range.  Reverted on September 15, 2026.ALTER TABLE ... MERGE PARTITIONS and SPLIT PARTITION. Built-in commands for combining or dividing partitions. Reverted on August 27, 2026.GROUP BY ALL. Shorthand for grouping by every non-aggregate expression in the select list.. Reverted on July 17, 2026.pg_dumpall.  Reverted on June 18, 2026.pg_get_role_ddl(), pg_get_tablespace_ddl(), and pg_get_database_ddl() were Reverted on September 13, 2026.
PostgreSQL is made by its contributors: thank you to the hundreds of community contributors credited in the release notes. Special thanks to the engineers on the Lakebase team who worked on this version of PostgreSQL:
As with any new major version of PostgreSQL, we recommend a measured approach to adopting it. Before moving production workloads to 19.0, we suggest you:
PostgreSQL19 also changes several defaults and compatibility behaviors. Check these before you upgrade:
We've covered most of the extensions supported in our PostgreSQL 18 release, but not all of them are available on PostgreSQL 19 yet. We're adding more as they become available from the extension providers. If your workload depends on a specific extension, confirm it's available on PostgreSQL 19 before you plan a migration.
The community typically ships its first minor release a few months after a major version, as real-world usage surfaces fixes and refinements. PostgreSQL 19.1 is anticipated in February, and many teams plan production upgrades around that release. That's a common and sensible pattern for any .0 release, and it's just as true here.
Whatever your timeline, we're here to help. If you'd like to talk through upgrade planning or testing, reach out to your account team or Databricks support.
PostgreSQL 19 is available on Lakebase today. Create a PostgreSQL 19 project in your workspace to try it, or get started with Lakebase if you're new to it.

====================================================================================================

Anthropic rival says companies can (mostly) skip the pricey models
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
00:26:03 UTC
09:26:03 KST

On the same day that Anthropic went public in the largest IPO on record, with investor enthusiasm for the AI lab through the roof, far away at the Slush startup conference in Helsinki, the topic du jour was open source everything.
That’s the enormous opportunity being chased by companies like Fireworks AI, as enterprises seek ways to avoid paying frontier prices for their AI needs.
Four-year-old Fireworks runs AI models for other businesses. The Bay Area-based outfit specializes in open-weight models, which anyone can download, and helps customers tune them on their own data so they run faster and cheaper than the models of Anthropic, OpenAI, and other heavyweights, and are harder for rivals to copy. Customers have included Uber, Shopify, and Notion.
Fireworks co-founder Chenyu Zhao and many others believe companies are gravitating toward platforms like his because frontier models are pricey and, in most cases, he says, unnecessary unless you need the very latest and greatest generalized intelligence. There are also growing concerns that frontier labs absorb too much of their customers’ know-how, even if only through the prompts used to get answers, a worry Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella raised in July.
Fireworks has also gotten its first black eye, having disclosed two security incidents this fall. In one, hackers reached API keys and tokens stored as secrets for a limited number of accounts; in the other, attackers breached its cloud provider, made their way into a development system, and nabbed customer data, including billing records and some datasets.
Notion says AI chat transcripts from fewer than 1% of its workspaces were exposed, but it has disconnected from Fireworks. Uber says hackers stole the names, addresses, birth dates and driver’s license numbers of about 150,000 drivers through a vendor that TechCrunch identified as Fireworks. Fireworks will have to win back the confidence of customers like them.
Fireworks, which is backed by Sequoia and Benchmark, among other firms, was assigned a $17.5 billion valuation in summer. It’s reportedly in early talks for yet another round, though Zhao declined to comment.
There’s money chasing everyone in the category, seems like. Rival companies Together AI and Baseten have announced massive rounds in recent months. On Wednesday, Palo Alto Networks said it will buy Applied Compute, which, like Fireworks, helps companies train and run custom AI models on their own data. The deal for the startup, founded just a year ago by former OpenAI researchers, is $4.5 billion, all of it in stock.
Zhao and I spoke onstage about all of it on Thursday at Slush. Here’s some of what Zhao had to say, edited for length and clarity.
You spent a decade at Google, including managing a 50-person engineering team on Google Cloud Vertex AI. Why leave when you did?
When you meet great co-founders, it’s obvious within five minutes that they’re really smart. I was sold after talking to Lin [Qiao] and Dima [Dzhulgakov]. It’s also important to find people who are genuinely nice, people you’ll enjoy working with.
How do you describe Fireworks to a CEO who has never heard of it?
I describe Fireworks as a specialized intelligence platform. OpenAI and Anthropic are building general intelligence. We use open models, which are often smaller, cheaper, and faster, and let companies add their own data to specialize them. That way you own your AI rather than being beholden to giant AI conglomerates. If you’re just using Opus or Sonnet, that’s not a differentiator.
How far behind are open models right now?
A few years ago I’d have said a year, a year and a half. Then came DeepSeek and a waterfall of fantastic open models. These days it’s at most six months behind, probably closer to three.
If the models are so close behind, why are businesses slow to switch?
Frontier models are a household brand, and before you find product-market fit, using the best model is low risk. But once you pass 10,000 or 100,000 users and you’re spending $10,000 to $100,000 a month on tokens, it’s time to think about open source.
In July, Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella warned that companies using proprietary AI models pay twice, once in money and again in know-how. Do you think that warning had a material effect?
We think we’ll live in a two-model world, with frontier and open models in parallel. You want the best intelligence for high-level planning, but you don’t need a frontier model for basic arithmetic. That’s why there’s so much interest in routing, and why people now think about cost per task, not just cost per token.
Anthropic went public today. What do you think of its valuation? There’s one scenario in which it just continues to cement its place in this sort of oligopoly; there’s another where it starts to lose traction.
On the record: I’ll probably call my broker tonight and buy a few shares. This is a long-term bet. In 10 years AI usage will be at a completely different scale, and that takes an incredible amount of capital, which is why these companies are going public.
Fireworks has had a couple of security breaches recently. What do you tell customers?
Every company is probably seeing an unprecedented frequency of attacks. For our incident, a lot of the motivation was LLM jacking: hunting through providers for API keys and secrets to sell tokens on illicit marketplaces. Our promise is zero data retention for inference traffic, and there’s no indication that has been compromised.
Notion has disconnected from Fireworks. Did you lose other customers?
I don’t think I can publicly name the specific customers that were impacted. Some, such as Notion and Cursor, have gone public already. It was a small subset of our customers that had this data impact.
How did that customer data get exposed, given your zero-retention promise?
Notion was using batch prediction, where you upload a dataset that’s stored at rest, so it’s not a zero-retention product by nature. We’re moving those services to an online flow so we keep data only as long as we need it.
Jensen Huang left Nvidia last week, and Nvidia has backed Fireworks since 2024. Does that affect you?
It’s been a blow to the industry, and we feel for Jensen and his family. But Nvidia has always been a strong supporter of open source, and I don’t see that changing. We expect it to keep investing in Nemotron [Nvidia’s family of open AI models], and we’re excited to keep partnering with them.

ChatGPT for SNES
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-20
00:25:59 UTC
09:25:59 KST

Download the v0.7 ROM (1.5 MB zip: one .sfc file). Requires a flash cart with Super FX support, such as an FXPak Pro or an sd2snes with current firmware. Leave Chip Options › SuperFX speed at Normal. The build with the fact book (17 MB zip: the .sfc and its .msu file) also needs MSU-1 support: put both files in the same folder with their names unchanged.
- ChatGPT for SNES is an unofficial experiment designed by Benj Edwards and coded by AI agents: a ChatGPT-style chat program for the Super Nintendo. Its training conversations were written by gpt-oss-120b, OpenAI's open-weight model. Not made or endorsed by OpenAI.
- This is version 0.7 (2 November 2026).
- A small language model with about 2.2 million parameters runs on the cartridge's Super FX 2 chip and writes its answers one word at a time.
- In the main build every word it writes comes from the language model. A second build adds a fact book (the opening sentences of 150,000 Simple English Wikipedia articles, stored in the cartridge's MSU-1 data file): like ChatGPT searching the web, its model can decide to look something up and print what the book says.
- Here it runs in the Snes9x emulator in your browser; the first visit loads about 4 MB (19 MB with the fact book).
- Press Start (Enter) on the title screen, then type on your keyboard and press Enter to send. Each letter takes a moment to appear, because the page moves the cursor on the game's own on-screen keyboard.
- It is not a useful assistant: the model is millions of times smaller than the models behind ChatGPT, knows about 8,000 words, and often gets things wrong, sometimes confidently. The fact book can carry mistakes too.
On a real Super NES
If you liked this, you might enjoy Séance, which runs a tiny AI language model on a real NES.

Elon Musk says SpaceX has a plan to eliminate cell phone dead zones
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
23:37:56 UTC
08:37:56 KST

CEO Elon Musk said SpaceX has completed the design of a miniature rooftop cell tower that could provide better coverage to mobile phones and eliminate dead zones inside buildings where signals from its Starlink satellites have trouble reaching.
These mini cell towers are expected to go into production in 2027, Musk said, during an interview at the DealBook Summit this week.
“We’ve got a plan that enables ubiquitous access on Earth, including via cell phone,” Musk said, noting the company is launching its Direct-to-Cell V2 satellite constellation next year, which will increase bandwidth 40-fold. “But there is a missing piece of the puzzle here which is that if you’re indoors, especially if you have a metal roof, that blocks the signal from space. So we need something that’s terrestrial that addresses situations where people are, you know, in a building with a thick roof essentially.”
The company’s efforts initially focused on buying wireless spectrum after receiving the Federal Communications Commission’s approval in 2024 to move forward with plans to offer a direct-to-phone version of its Starlink satellite internet service, in partnership with T-Mobile. SpaceX struck a $17 billion deal with EchoStar in 2025 and agreed this year to acquire a portfolio of low-band spectrum valued at $8 billion.
Combining that wireless spectrum with mini cell towers should boost signals and end the dead zone problem, he said. According to Musk, the company has completed the design of what he described as “a micro cell tower thing that you just put on your roof, like a Starlink dish.”
Musk, who has a history of missing his own production deadlines, demurred, saying the devices were “going into production, I think, next year.”
The idea isn’t new, but it wasn’t clear until this week how much progress SpaceX had made toward developing the hardware.
SpaceX President Gwynne Shotwell said during the company’s August earnings call that it wouldn’t use big cell towers and would instead deploy small, low-power cellular base stations.
Even with this latest progress report, SpaceX appears to be still working out the details. Musk said one approach would be to bundle the small cellular base stations together with Starlink terminals.
SpaceX’s plan to build a hybrid cellular-satellite network has fueled speculation, and even predictions, that legacy telecom companies could be in danger. But Musk doesn’t think so, because he expects an entirely new class of customers to drive demand for wireless bandwidth: AI.
“I don’t think this means, like you know, AT&T, Verizon, or others go out of business or anything,” he said, adding that maybe they’ll lose some revenue. “But I actually think that the demand for bandwidth will be tremendous in the future, and a lot, and that bandwidth will be driven primarily by superintelligence and robots, and they’re going to need a lot more bandwidth than humans.”

Mei—or, the Models Engineering Index—calculates language models' practical performance on engineering tasks.
				Mei calculates language models' engineering performance.
			
Each model's score is a composite index across 16 coding benchmarks, adjusted for benchmark difficulty and missing results so models average 50. Cost and token use are adjusted the same way and shown relative to the typical model (1×).

====================================================================================================

chocolate pecan pie bars
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
23:00:45 UTC
08:00:45 KST

“Twenty years of Thanksgivings on Smitten Kitchen!” I told Ed Levine yesterday, as we chatted for an upcoming podcast episode [I’ll link it here as soon as it’s live], a statistic that remains baffling to me. What should one even have to show for two decades of Thanksgiving recipes, ideas, and advice? A degree in dry brining? A medal in single-oven-side-dish-rewarming Jenga? A metamorphosis into my final form: a human-shaped holiday cooking questions hotline (it’s me, or at least the texts on my phone)? Aspirationally, all of the above?
The only pattern I can identify in my Thanksgiving cooking evolution over the years is this: The more I’ve hosted, the more time I’ve spent having Real Talks (with myself, in my head) about what is actually worth my time, energy, and limited stove and oven real estate, the simpler — and I’d argue better — my recipes have become. Turkey? Dry-brined, with a gorgeous burnished finish and the best onions you’ll eat in your lifetime, all from six ingredients. Stuffing? Store-bought challah, celery, leeks, herbs, and a nobody-needs-to-know amount of butter. A carrot side dish? Start with one bag of baby carrots. Yes, really.
My pecan pie has gone through a few iterations over the years: first, a classic pecan pie (still a knockout, but it serves only eight, a travesty); then a chocolate pecan slab pie in my second cookbook, Smitten Kitchen Every Day (party-sized, hooray, but a serious time commitment, with two homemade crusts, a lattice, and a chocolate layer). A few years ago, I streamlined and simplified that recipe into these bars, and I haven’t looked back. The crust is pressed in. The topping is made in one bowl. Yet I’ve packed as much flavor as I possibly can into every bite: The pecans are toasted, there’s honey and dark brown sugar, vanilla bean, dark chocolate chips, sea salt, and there’s brown butter, both in the crust and the pecan layer. The aroma of the tiniest square leaps off the plate, and I think we should be there to catch it.
Pecan Pie Bars
- 1 cup plus 5 tablespoons (10.5 ounces or 300 grams) unsalted butter (to be used for crust and pecan layer)
- 2 tablespoons (30 ml) water
- 2 cups plus 2 tablespoons (275 grams) all-purpose flour
- 1/2 cup (60 to 100 grams) granulated or powdered sugar
- 1 teaspoon kosher salt (Diamond brand; use half of others)
- 2/3 cups (140 grams) packed dark brown sugar
- 1/2 cup (170 grams) honey
- 1/4 cup (60 ml) heavy cream
- 1/2 teaspoon flaky or coarse sea salt, plus an extra pinch or two to finish
- 1 teaspoon apple cider vinegar
- 1 teaspoon vanilla bean paste or vanilla extract
- 2 large eggs
- 3 1/4 cups (12 ounces or 340 grams) pecan halves, lightly toasted
- 1 cup (6 ounces or 170 grams) chocolate chips, semisweet or bittersweet are great here
Shortbread Crust
Pecan layer
[If your pecans aren’t already toasted, while the oven heats, place them on a sheet pan and bake them for 8 to 10 minutes, until fragrant. Set aside until needed.]
Brown the butter: In a medium-large saucepan (we’ll use this as the bowl to mix the pecan layer in a bit), melt butter over medium-high heat. It will melt, then foam, then begin to developed fragrant, toasted brown specks at the edges of the pan. Remove from heat when you see it starting to brown; the residual heat will be sufficient to toast the rest of the butter to a medium brown color. Carefully, so it doesn’t splatter, add 2 tablespoons of water.
Make the shortbread crust: In a large bowl, combine the flour, sugar, and salt. Measure 1 cup of the browned butter (leaving the rest in the saucepan) and add it to the flour mixture, stirring it until it’s mixed into a soft, crumbly dough. Scatter the dough over your prepared pan and press it evenly across the bottom and 1/4-inch further up at the edges. Transfer to the oven and bake for 15 to 17 minutes, until it smells fragrant and begins to get more golden at the edges.
Meanwhile, make the pecan layer: Add brown sugar, honey, heavy cream, flaky salt, cider vinegar, and vanilla to the remaining brown butter in your saucepan and whisk to combine. Add eggs, one at a time, whisking until fully mixed. Add your toasted pecan halves (still a little warm is fine) and stir to coat with honey mixture. Stir in chocolate chips last.
Assemble and bake: Pour the pecan mixture over the crust — it’s fine if it’s still hot from the oven — then sprinkle a few flakes of salt over the top. Bake for 25 to 30 minutes, or until the edges are set but the center is somewhat firm to the touch, but still has some jiggle to it.
Transfer pan to a cooling rack and let the bars cool completely. You can hurry this along in the fridge. Use the edges of the parchment paper to lift bars out and onto a cutting board. Cut into your desired size pieces.
Do ahead: These bars keep in the fridge for up to a week, but only if you hide them from family and, sometimes, yourself.

Nvidia Approaches Arm Boss Rene Haas In Search For New CEO
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
23:00:00 UTC
08:00:00 KST

Nvidia Approaches Arm Boss Rene Haas In Search For New CEO (theguardian.com) 32
			
		 	
				Nvidia has reportedly approached Arm CEO Rene Haas as it searches for a replacement for co-founder Jensen Huang, who resigned last week amid a personal scandal. According to Bloomberg, Haas is emerging as a leading contender for the job. The 64-year-old previously worked at Nvidia as general manager of its computing products business before joining Arm, giving him firsthand experience with the AI chip giant. The Guardian reports:  Heidrick & Struggles, the recruiting firm hired by the company's board, has started contacting potential candidates, according to Bloomberg. It reported that Rene Haas, who joined Arm in 2013 and became chief executive in 2022, is among those approached. [...] Haas, 64, is viewed as the leading contender for the top job at Nvidia, having previously worked at the $3.6tn company for seven years as the general manager of its computing products.
Reports suggest that other potential candidates include: Ian Buck, an Nvidia vice-president; Lisa Su, AMD's chief executive; and Johny Srouji, Apple's chief hardware officer. Arm, which is listed in New York but retains its global headquarters in Cambridge, has about 500 users of its chip designs worldwide, including Nvidia, Apple, Samsung and Qualcomm. The company employs more than 7,000 staff, including more than 3,000 in the UK. It is the biggest tech firm headquartered in Britain, now valued at $196bn. Haas told a tech conference in Austin on Wednesday that Huang was "a legend." "What he built at Nvidia is historic," he said. "You don't have many champions like that."

Court: 4th Amendment Disallows Use Of AI-Generated 'Probable Cause'
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
22:56:59 UTC
07:56:59 KST

Court Tells Cops That The 4th Amendment Doesn’t Cover AI-Generated ‘Probable Cause’
from the remember-that-time-robocop-shot-the-Constitution-in-the-dick dept
I don’t know where this decision will take us, but its implications reach much further than the single case it covers. AI is pretty much everywhere, and if a court says an algorithm can’t make probable cause determinations, it affects/infects everything from facial recognition tech to predictive policing software to Axon adding AI shovelware to its omnipresent body cameras under the (correct) assumption cops don’t like writing reports.
In this case, Alain Osorio was arrested for allegedly passing a bad check at local bank. The only pictures of the suspect came from the bank’s CCTV footage. This was handed over to Detective Ryan Kilmer of the Florence (KY) Police Department.
The detective decided to run a screenshot of the suspect’s face (derived from the CCTV footage) through the PD’s facial recognition program. The decision [PDF] doesn’t specify which facial recognition program was used, but given that the match was made using photos found on Facebook, it suggests Clearview AI might have been involved, given its scraping of hundreds of billions of images from the open web since its inception.
Not that it did anything useful in terms of actual detective work. Nor did it do anything remotely constitutional, not when the operative word in “probable cause” is “probable.”
Relying solely on the AI identification, Detective Kilmer concluded that the match was accurate. [Id. at 4–5]. The Facebook profile belonged to an individual known as “Yony Smith Npoles Charon,” and for an unknown reason, Detective Kilmer concluded that “Yony” was actually Alain Vizcay Osorio—the man in the booking photo.
The court says “for an unknown reason.” I can make an educated guess, however. Passing a bad check is the sort of crime most cops won’t really dig into. Detective Kilmer was handed the case and did the bare minimum: he ran an AI-assisted search. He didn’t actually do anything that resembled actual detective work. But he still felt his 30 seconds of non-existent effort were enough to support a criminal complaint and the issuance of an arrest warrant.
Osorio — who lived in Nebraska — was arrested and extradited to Kentucky to face criminal charges. He was released on bond the day after his arrest (January 24, 2025). He was then indicted by a grand jury in March 2025 and arraigned roughly two months later. Again, Osorio pleaded not guilty. At that point, the state’s attorney’s office dismissed the charges after Osorio provided proof he had been working in Nebraska at the time the bad check was being passed by (the presumably still at-large) suspect captured by the bank’s security camera.
The only thing the AI used by the detective seemed to be capable of doing was reinforcing the investigator’s “all Black people look the same” prejudices. (To be clear, I don’t specifically know if this detective has these prejudices, but the chain of events in this case certainly support an assumption of this person’s assumptions, if you catch my drift.)
The individuals in the security footage and the Facebook profile image do not resemble the booking photo of Osorio. [Id. at 6–7]. Based on the booking photo, the only thing Osorio has in common with the suspect is that he is a black male with facial hair. [Dkt. 3-1 at 2–3]. Based on the photographs, it appears that Osorio has a different head shape, different haircut, different facial features, and different complexion.
Detective Kilmer told the court (via his affidavit) that he had used “investigative tools” to “match” Osario to the suspect. In reality, the court says, Kilmer only used a single tool and rolled with its obviously faulty conclusions despite Florence PD policy specifically stating facial recognition AI alone is not enough to “establish probable cause.”
Kilmer runs directly into this policy and the Fourth Amendment itself, which poses multiple problems for his desire to walk away fiscally intact from this lawsuit. The policy is undeniable, which means Kilmer deliberately ignored his “training” and any “expertise” it was supposed to impart.
But beyond that, there’s the Fourth Amendment itself, which can’t as easily be ignored as cop shop software clickwrap. That much is made perfectly clear in the court’s introductory paragraph:
The Fourth Amendment to the United States Constitution does not allow a detective to delegate to AI the task of assessing probable cause. In other words, the Fourth Amendment does not permit an individual to be arrested based on nothing more than an uncorroborated conclusion by AI that the individual in question has committed a crime. And no reasonable detective in the Defendant’s position could conclude otherwise.
The first part of that suggests implications far beyond this case, given how often “detectives” are letting their fingers do the walking (so to speak) and pretending whatever the computer coughs up is arrest-worthy. That’s not just the facial recognition tech used in this case. This is also the AI that steers patrols and public interactions via so-called “predictive policing.” That’s also the AI that writes reports for police officers, allowing the computer to create the permanent, official record of stops, arrests, and uses of force.
But what’s also important is the last sentence of that paragraph. That’s the thing that’s going to make this stick. The detective isn’t shielded by qualified immunity, which means this is sure to be appealed. But if this officer does this (rather than “allow” the people who he’s supposed to be serving to buy him out of a lawsuit via a settlement), he’s going to make this an appellate case. And there’s definitely no reason to assume the higher court is just going to blow off the obvious concerns raised by this lawsuit and allow QI to cover up this compendium of wrongs.
For now, the decision stands as it is: AI isn’t probable cause. Never has been. Never should be. And if this court has anything to say about it, it never will be. Detectives who prefer to let computers do their thinking for them might want to dial back their reliance on algorithms. Justice should never be subject to available processing power. And if you lack the desire (or intelligence!) to do detective work properly, well…
	Filed Under: 4th amendment, ai, facial recognition, facial recognition tech, false positives, florence pd, kentucky, mass surveillance, probable cause
