====================================================================================================

Want to see your live AI usage on your Mac’s icons? This clever new app lets you.
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
22:33:26 UTC
07:33:26 KST

There’s a new way to keep an eye on your AI usage and when your limits reset: by using your Mac’s app icons. Yes, really!
Designer Michael Flarup has just launched a new Mac utility called IconSwapper, which lets you replace any app’s official icon with one of your own creation. While you can use this tool to change your icons’ appearance, design, shading, and more, one of the more interesting ways to use it is to swap the icons for AI apps like ChatGPT or Claude with versions that can display your live AI usage.
Doing so is fairly simple, and you don’t have to be technically minded to get this to work. After installing the app on your Mac, you simply choose which application you want to create a “live” icon for — meaning one that responds to a particular app.
Besides tracking AI usage in ChatGPT, Claude, and Cursor, you can also create live icons for a number of the built-in Mac apps like Messages, Mail, Safari, Finder, Music, and others like Chrome and Spotify, but these configurations tend to be more fun than they are practical. For instance, you can configure Safari’s compass to follow things like page loads and downloads, or you can make it so that the Finder guy’s eyes follow your cursor around the screen. (That one is hilarious!)
Of course, there are plenty of other ways to track your AI usage — you don’t need an icon to do that. You can track usage from your account or your harness, even from a fun little gadget. But tracking it from the icon adds a certain whimsy to your Mac desktop setup. (If your dock is crowded, note that you can also see your icon customizations from your tab bar as you switch apps.)
Beyond live icons, you can also change what an icon looks like using IconSwapper’s built-in tools.
There are a surprising number of built-in customizations that are as easy to use as clicking a few buttons or toggling a switch. IconSwapper also comes with a large library of alternative icons for every app that are already ready to use, or you can upload your own.
Flarup is based in Copenhagen, where he runs a design studio, and he has been drawn to icon design and customization for years. He’s been making his own Mac icons since the early 2000s and even wrote a book about app icons that celebrates the work of designers.
He had been working on a solution that would allow him to customize Mac icons, which had proven tricky because macOS has no official support for theming and protects its system icons. To work around this, he came up with a different technique that involves drawing directly on the icons without replacing them, allowing him to use shaders, motion, and display real data.
Flarup tells TechCrunch that his renderer runs the icon artwork through custom Metal shaders in real time.
“The breakthrough was figuring out how to send the Dock a bitmap for another running app and have it draw that frame itself,” he explained. “I spent a lot of time getting the artwork to follow the icon through all the little ways macOS moves it, from Dock magnification to the Command-Tab switcher. Once I had that working, I could make the icon react to whatever’s happening,” Flarup said.
That lets users configure icons to do all kinds of fun, new things they never could before, like make light move across a gold icon as you drag your cursor over it, or animate an app’s icon while it’s thinking.
“That opens up a lot of fun ways to make your Mac feel like yours. I’ve always felt the software we use every day could be a little more fun and a lot more personal,” he added.
The app itself is a one-time purchase of $19 as a launch promo but will later sell for $29, its website says.

Apple is hiring engineers to build its own AI agent
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
22:31:41 UTC
07:31:41 KST

Apple is working on its own AI agent that can perform a range of tasks for you, including browsing the web, working across apps, writing code, and calling services. That’s according to a now-removed job listing spotted by Bloomberg, which said Apple is looking for an engineer to lead the creation of an agentic AI assistant.
Apple is hiring engineers to build its own AI agent
Apple’s job listing describes an AI agent that can browse the web, work across apps, and call services.
An AI agent from Apple would put the iPhone maker in the running to compete with the likes of Meta’s Muse, OpenAI’s Dots, Anthropic’s Duet, and Google’s Tasks. Muse, ChatGPT, and Perplexity can already take control of a computer through their Mac apps, while Google just started letting Gemini do the same.
Apple began rolling out its AI-upgraded Siri in September, which you can use to ask questions about what’s on your screen, as well as perform actions across apps. It looks like Apple is planning to give Siri more agentic capabilities, as a report from Bloomberg suggests the company could add the ability to rotate and crop images in Photos, or organize Safari tabs. This lines up with the company’s new job listing, which says the engineer will work closely with teams across its Siri, UI Frameworks, and Apple Foundation Models divisions.
“People already do their most valuable work by moving fluidly between tools: a browser, a document, a terminal, an editor, a service,” the job listing said. “We’re building AI agents that can do the same… These agents plan and carry out multi-step tasks across apps, browsers, command lines, code, and APIs, and they can coordinate specialized sub-agents along the way.”
With its upcoming iOS 27.3 update, Apple is also giving developers the ability to integrate Siri AI into “all apps.” This is rolling out as part of a Developer Preview and will eventually allow Siri to perform tasks across a wider range of apps.
Update, November 20th: Added that the job listing has since been removed.

Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
22:00:00 UTC
07:00:00 KST

JPMorgan, Visa, Mastercard Disclose Cyberattack On Third-Party Vendors (reuters.com) 18
			
		 	
				JPMorgan Chase, Visa and Mastercard have disclosed cyberattacks involving third-party service providers that exposed customer marketing data, including names, email addresses and, in some cases, phone numbers and postal codes. All three companies said their core systems or payment networks were unaffected, while JPMorgan emphasized that the incident was unrelated to the banking disruptions earlier this week. Reuters reports:  A cyber extortion group called "n0n" claimed on November 16 to have obtained cardmember engagement records that included "hundreds of thousands of campaign interactions for tens of thousands of distinct cardmember email addresses," according to a screenshot of the message captured by cybercrime research platform eCrime.ch. The data includes what appears to be 124 bank employee email addresses and more than 16,000 customer email addresses, some with phone numbers and postal codes, according to a Reuters review. Reuters could not independently verify the authenticity of the data.
The same hacking group on November 16 posted similar claims regarding Visa and Mastercard and posted data. [...] The Mastercard data posted by n0n includes marketing data related to customers in North America, the Middle East and Africa, and a small number of Mastercard employee email addresses. The hacking group has claimed roughly 200 incidents since emerging in September 2026, according to eCrime.ch.  "Customers don't distinguish between a bank and a vendor when it comes to a cyberattack, so there is a question of reputational risk here," said Carissa Robb at SolomonEdwards, a consulting firm. "There needs to be a little bit more pressure on how often companies are scrutinizing the amount of data that is shared with vendors and the necessity of the data being shared," Robb said.

A brief history of Nvidia’s recent stock fluctuations
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
21:59:35 UTC
06:59:35 KST

“We’ve got this,” Colette Kress, interim CEO of Nvidia, said Tuesday night as she closed out the embattled chip company’s third quarter earnings call.
A brief history of Nvidia’s recent stock fluctuations
Tracking the stock price amid Jensen Huang’s extramarital affairs and alleged spy scandal.
By Thursday, the company’s stock price was hovering at around $150 per share, a nearly 40 percent drop from its all-time high on October 6th, and wiping out a brief post-earnings pop on Tuesday night. Meanwhile, Wall Street was paying up for one of Nvidia’s biggest customers as Anthropic made its Nasdaq debut at $111.50 per share — 34 percent higher than expected.
Looking at the earnings report numbers alone, Nvidia had a blockbuster quarter. Q3 revenue more than doubled from the same period last year up to total $115.8 billion, driven by data center revenue that was up 110 percent and exceeding Wall Street expectations. Nearly $20 billion in revenue came from the highly anticipated Vera Rubin platform, which shipped during the quarter, becoming the “fastest product ramp in company history,” Kress told investors.
“Our momentum is real,” Kress said. “With the market-leading multiyear roadmap, supply agreements extending through to the end of the decade, and sustainable demand, we are well positioned to accelerate Nvidia’s leadership position.”
But investors are stilltrying to reconcile Nvidia’s stellar recent financial performance and position as a central pillar in the AI economy with serious concerns over its future.
The company’s stock has taken a stunning tumble in recent weeks. Fueled first by a broader market sell-off of chip stocks amid concerns about the AI bubble, the stock took another blow following the resignation of former CEO Jensen Huang amid reports of extramarital affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy, according to US intelligence sources cited by The New York Times.
Harvey Jones, Nvidia’s newly appointed chairman of the board, told investors on the call Tuesday that the company “does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO” and has already tapped an executive search firm to help find a replacement. Huang, who cofounded Nvidia in 1993 and has led the company ever since — famously without a succession plan — has become essentially inseparable from it in the eyes of Wall Street and the media. But while Huang may not be ready to let go, posting on X this week that he’d be “honored” to be reinstated as CEO, Nvidia’s executive suite seems ready to move on.
It has been a tumultuous two and a half weeks for Nvidia.
On October 29th, The New York Times reported that US intelligence agencies believed Jensen Huang to be the target of a Chinese espionage plot. The report centered on his “close relationship” with Alice Huang, a Chinese national and an executive at Megaspeed, a Singapore-based data center company and one of Nvidia’s clients. Nvidia initially contested the report, adding that Jensen Huang’s interactions with Alice Huang “are not unusual or unexpected.”
Nvidia’s stock seemingly remained unaffected by the reports, at least at first. The share price closed at $213.66 on October 29th, down just 0.8 percent from the previous day. (Julian Barnes, one of the reporters on the story, later commented, “I will confess I was kind of surprised that the world’s initial reaction to our October story was a bit muted.”) The stock closed at a 4 percent drop the next day as the entire Nasdaq fell 2.9 percent, the worst single-day decline since June.
The stock continued to fall through Wednesday, November 4th, the day after the blue wave swept the midterms, stoking fears on Wall Street that the new class of Democratic governors in key states like Texas would slow down data center construction. On Thursday, November 5th, Nvidia’s stock fell 9.2 percent, its largest single-day drop in at least a year. But chip stocks began to recover the next day, and by close on Friday, November 6th, Nvidia was back up 2.8 percent to a share price of $186.64.
Then all hell broke loose. On Sunday, November 8th, Jensen Huang sent a company-wide memo to Nvidia staff in which he apologized for engaging in an extramarital affair. The memo coincided with the opening of an independent investigation by Nvidia’s board into Huang’s alleged misconduct and whether company secrets or national security had been compromised. Huang said he would cooperate fully with the investigation. By the opening bell on Monday, November 9th, Nvidia shares had tumbled 7 percent and trading volume surged past its three-month average as investors reacted to the memo. After a brief rebound, the stock continued to oscillate the next day, as more details of Jensen Huang and Alice Huang’s relationship emerged. By the time the market closed on Tuesday, November 10th, Nvidia shares were down to $168.89, nearly 10 percent below the closing price on the last day of 2025.
The stock rebounded slightly on Wednesday, November 11th, as investors prepared for Nvidia’s third quarter earnings call, scheduled for the following Tuesday, November 17th. By Wednesday night, however, shares had plunged yet again as media reports circulated that Nvidia’s board was considering ousting Huang, and other reports emerged detailing a second alleged affair, this time between Huang and an Nvidia employee.
Nvidia announced Huang’s departure on the morning of Thursday, November 12th, sending the company’s shares falling nearly 14 percent at the open. It closed at just a 4.3 percent dip. The intraday low of $148.80 per share was its lowest price in a year.
On December 1st, Jensen Huang will appear before the House Foreign Affairs Committee, the Select Committee on the Chinese Communist Party, and the House Intelligence Committee for transcribed interviews. Nvidia’s general counsel will do the same on December 2nd.
Retail investors have already seized the opportunity to buy the dip. In the first 30 minutes of trading on Thursday, November 12th, retail investors bought $111 million of Nvidia shares in the largest-ever first half-hour of opening for the stock, Bloomberg reported, citing Vanda Research.
They might be onto something.
Nvidia’s average price target is $232.59, with the lowest estimate at $130 and the highest at $350, according to an analysis of analyst projections over the last three months by Google Finance.
Nearly two-thirds of those analysts issued a “Buy” rating for Nvidia in the last three months. Of those, all 30 reiterated that rating post-earnings.
The stock closed at $145.98 on Thursday, a new 52-week low.
Then again, numbers are the core basis for stock market ratings, and numbers may not be Nvidia’s problem right now. It’s everything else — the leadership vacuum, upcoming congressional testimony, regulatory uncertainty, broader questions about neocloud customer financing and sales to China — that will continue to impact the stock in the coming weeks.

Meta plans to charge $300 for Muse Charm, its upcoming Tamagotchi-like AI device that was teased at the company’s Connect event earlier this year, The Information has reported.
When reached for comment by TechCrunch, a Meta spokesperson didn’t deny the claims, merely providing the following statement: “We’re charmed by the interest in our AI devices, and we’ll have more to share soon.”
So far, Meta has made a relatively successful push into consumer AI, launching Muse — its agentic helper — along with a variety of cute avatars that users can use to give the system more of a personality.
Muse Charm, the company’s push into AI hardware, will allow users to carry a physical personification of its software around in their pocket (or potentially wear it around their neck), talking to it, asking it questions, and giving it commands.
It remains to be seen whether customers will bite or not. AI wearables have had a rough go of it so far (here’s looking at you, Humane Ai pin), but companies continue to iterate on the concept in an attempt to break through to a broader audience.
Indeed, $300 is arguably a pretty steep price for a product the size of a keychain, although it’s also a price point that’s in line with what other companies have been charging. The wearable launched by Friend — the AI startup whose device lets you text and talk to an AI persona — is currently retailing for $250. On the other hand, Amazon’s all-new Bee Frontier wearable AI device — which transcribes conversations and tracks tasks — lists for $75 and is currently on a Black Friday, 30% off sale of $52.49. Even with its $99/year subscription to let it tie into a user’s apps (photos, emails, etc.), it’s still far less expensive than Muse Charm.

====================================================================================================

These tech outages are giving Y2K, AI agent style
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
21:42:31 UTC
06:42:31 KST

It’s going down, as the American rapper Yung Joc might say: the airline booking systems, banking platforms, and productivity platforms. In the past two weeks, the U.S.’s top businesses have faced technical outages that have disrupted the systems that run everyday life.
Apple Pay went down. NASA’s internal communication network went down. The FAA had to ground all flights for hours (only the third time in history since 9/11), and Microsoft was hit with an outage that took down Outlook, Teams, and Copilot for nearly two days.
The first thought, naturally, is an AI hacker invasion of some sort, especially after hackers managed to attack fridges in a grocery store and a blood bank in France a few weeks ago. Here in the U.S., the director of National Intelligence said these outages don’t seem related to state-sponsored terrorism, while President Trump called the issues “individual events.” Many of the companies themselves have said the outages were not caused by cyberattacks or AI attacks, but tech issues.
Only, there’s the fact that they all struck within a short timespan of each other, and just a few weeks after Cybersecurity’s so-called “Black October,” when a slew of also-unrelated cybersecurity incidents occurred on October 21, striking PHP, Node.js, LiteLLM, more.
While there’s no reason to view these outages as cyberattacks, there may be more than coincidence going on: It’s giving AI-native Y2K, frankly.
Dave DeWalt, an investor who worked in cybersecurity for more than three decades (including as president and CEO of McAfee and as vice chairman of the CISA Advisory Council for the Department of Homeland Security), also thinks it’s probably AI-related.
The technical outages are most likely the “growing pains of a security system that’s getting stronger, not weaker,” and that “AI is surfacing vulnerabilities at a speed and scale we’ve never had before through AI tools like Mythos and others,” he told TechCrunch.
The thing is, many of these technical flaws have always existed, but because AI is surfacing them so fast, enterprises are rushing to fix them before bad actors can exploit them. “That surge in patching is disrupting things in the short term,” he said.
This was, perhaps, the first moment when legacy meets modern, Anusha Iyer, a software engineer and founder of the security startup Corsha, told TechCrunch. “These industrial systems were never designed to be connected, let alone securely connected in the way we are pushing them to be today,” she said.
Virginia Senator Mark Warner, vice chair of the Senate Intel Committee, came to a similar conclusion (although investigations are still underway as to what could be happening). “These disruptions should serve as a wake-up call. It feels like every day brings another reminder that AI is moving faster than our ability to manage its consequences,” the senator wrote on X.
AI is reshaping how software is built, deployed, and operated, on a scale where everyone feels the sharp learning curve. Iyer cited the FAA outage as an example of legacy tech that is not as sophisticated or advanced as the technologies companies are trying to merge it with.
In less technical terms, she said the outage may have been caused (and preliminary findings point to this) when the FAA was trying to modernize its telecommunications infrastructure and the transition overwhelmed parts of its legacy hardware, where the new tech likely operated at a normal modern capacity while the legacy hardware couldn’t keep up.
When looked at this way, she said, AI is actually getting the bad rep. “It’s not rapid adoption of AI yet causing these outages,” she said. “The fault is the rushed mix of the legacy and modern systems without a clear understanding of operational and cyber risk or the ability to gatekeep and protect the legacy systems.”
Organizations need a more steadfast approach to AI adoption, including more testing and prioritizing what needs to be fixed and when, DeWalt said.
Iyer added, “These industrial systems need to be segmented more tightly and more access controlled, so as modern systems are introduced, it can be phased in and risk mitigated.”
Actually, there might be a silver lining to all of this for companies (and for venture investors). DeWalt said he’d rather see these U.S. enterprises find and patch their flaws, even in this messy way, than have bad actors find and exploit those vulnerabilities in AI warfare that could cause even more national security issues.
And maybe some aspiring founders were inspired by them. DeWalt expects a rise in companies focused on “AI-driven vulnerability management, autonomous remediation, safe patch testing and deployment, and resilience for the systems that keep critical infrastructure running,” he said.
“There’s real pain in this transition as we’ve seen in the past week,” DeWalt said. “But there’s also enormous opportunity for the companies that help organizations move at AI speed without breaking things.”

Bending Spoons to acquire Mailchimp
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
21:36:40 UTC
06:36:40 KST

Bending Spoons has entered into a definitive agreement to acquire Mailchimp
November 19, 2026
Milan, Italy—November 19, 2026 | Bending Spoons S.p.A. has entered into a definitive agreement to acquire Mailchimp, the email marketing automation platform for businesses, from Intuit, the global financial technology platform, at an enterprise value of $3.68 billion. In connection with the all-cash transaction, Intuit will invest $368 million of its proceeds into newly issued Bending Spoons equity. The acquisition, including the investment, is subject to customary closing conditions, including regulatory approvals.
“Mailchimp is a powerful brand, built over two decades of reshaping the email marketing category and redefining how companies grow their audiences,” said Luca Ferrari, Bending Spoons CEO and co-founder. “We’re honored to welcome it to Bending Spoons and excited about what we can accomplish together. After closing, we’ll invest substantially in the business, building on the powerful, easy-to-use tools customers already value and rely on.”
“Mailchimp has helped millions of businesses grow and acquire customers through email marketing automation, and we are grateful to the Mailchimp team for what they have built and the impact they have had on customers and Intuit,” said Jack Tam, Senior Vice President, Intuit. “We are confident Bending Spoons will lead Mailchimp to continued success in this next chapter. We are focused on ensuring a smooth transition for Mailchimp’s customers, partners, and employees.”
Details of the Proposed Transaction
Under the terms of the agreement, which was unanimously approved by the board of directors of Bending Spoons, Bending Spoons will acquire the Mailchimp business by purchasing 100% of The Rocket Science Group, LLC and other assets and liabilities of the Mailchimp business.
The transaction is expected to close in early 2027, subject to receipt of required regulatory approvals and other customary closing conditions.
Legal Counsel and Financial Advisors
Citi and J.P. Morgan are acting as co-lead financial advisors to Bending Spoons, and Goldman Sachs Bank Europe SE, Succursale Italia and UniCredit are acting as financial advisors on the transaction. Freshfields US LLP is serving as legal counsel to Bending Spoons, EY-Parthenon is providing financial due diligence services and EY STP tax due diligence services, and NewTeam is providing financial services.
About Bending Spoons
Bending Spoons is built on the conviction that operational excellence enables efficient growth through acquisitions. It acquires digital businesses, implements deep transformations and ongoing optimizations to sustainably expand earnings, and reinvests in additional acquisitions, thereby continuing the compounding cycle. The company has executed this strategy for more than a decade and, to date, has never sold a material business.
Bending Spoons strives to envision the most successful version of an acquired business, and works to close the gap between its current state and that vision as quickly and completely as possible. The transformation is typically deep and entails reorganizing teams, overhauling technology, redesigning user interfaces, accelerating product development, and enhancing marketing and monetization. AI is often both a central component of the vision and a key tool in implementing the transformation.
Bending Spoons’ performance is driven by its Platform—comprising its people, proprietary technologies, and proprietary data—and reflects an intense focus on achieving exceptional talent density, cultural strength, and technical capabilities.
Bending Spoons’ main businesses include Airtable, AOL, Brightcove, Eventbrite, Evernote, Miro, Tractive, Vimeo, and WeTransfer.

Uber tests rides for kids as young as 10, with extra pay for drivers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
21:33:24 UTC
06:33:24 KST

Uber has opened its ride-hailing service to children as young as 10 in three U.S. cities as the company tests whether there’s a market for shuttling tweens without an adult by their side. The program, called Uber Kids, launched quietly over the past few weeks in Austin, Texas, Nashville, Tennessee, and Madison, Wisconsin.
The program, which builds on Uber’s existing teen ride-hailing service for passengers ages 13 to 17, is the latest bid by Uber to expand its customer base. Uber is describing this as a limited pilot program, which means it may never expand beyond these cities or even exist by next year. But if successful, it could open up a potentially lucrative market for Uber: busy families with active kids.
The Uber Kids program is stacked with mandatory requirements for parents and drivers. Parents or guardians are the only ones who can order a ride for their child, which they can do by adding the child to their family profile and completing a mandatory onboarding that explains the built-in safety features. A parent or guardian must be present at the pickup locations for the trip to begin and also have to include a note to the driver that explains the drop off.
Meanwhile, only highly rated drivers who have been invited are allowed to participate. Those drivers are required to complete an education module that includes step-by-step instructions and must be opted into live video streaming so parents can watch the trip in real time, according to Uber.
Uber said it also requires PIN verification for every Uber Kids trip, and says all recordings will be encrypted and not available to the driver, and will use sensors and GPS data to detect if the ride goes off course, stops unexpectedly, or ends early.
Drivers who are willing to adhere to those rules to shuttle kids will get a pay bump for the effort, according to Uber. Eligible drivers will earn $7 more on every Uber Kids trip compared to a standard one, the company says on the dedicated Uber Kids web page. Drivers will receive an extra $20 for the first Uber Kids ride they complete.
The extra compensation underscores a key challenge Uber faces as it expands into this younger age group — how to convince drivers to take on that added responsibility. If the program does expand it could eventually compete with existing transportation services such as HopSkipDrive and Zum.
Uber opened its ride-hailing service to minors in 2023 when it launched Uber for Teens, although the company had conducted early pilots several years prior. As the program expanded nationwide, Uber introduced a live video streaming feature.

Meta now sells 3D movies on Quest VR headsets
Use your Quest 3 to catch films like Avatar and Jurassic World Rebirth in 3D.
The company suggests watching movies in a Theater view that’s rolling out with a new Meta Quest update. Theater view “provides a big screen and dims your surroundings to create a cozy, personal cinema experience,” Meta says.
Meta promises that the library will “keep growing, with thousands of titles and international availability coming” when it launches its VR Glasses next spring. If you’re a Disney Plus subscriber, you can also watch 3D movies on Meta Quest hardware in the Disney Plus app thanks to an update rolled out earlier this month.

PHP Readies Critical Security Updates to Patch Another RCE Flaw
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
21:00:00 UTC
06:00:00 KST

PHP Readies Critical Security Updates to Patch Another RCE Flaw 17
			
		 	
				PHP has released security updates addressing seven critical remote code execution (RCE) vulnerabilities across its supported versions, just four weeks after patches for the widely exploited PHP2Shell flaws. SecurityWeek reports:  One of the security defects expected to be resolved is CVE-2026-115560, a remote code execution (RCE) defect in the file-upload progress tracking functionality. The vulnerability was publicly disclosed two days after PHP2Shell made headlines. PHP's maintainers warned at the time that the security flaw can be triggered only under an unusual combination of configuration and code.
"An application must set session.serialize_handler to php_binary or php_serialize in its configuration and then switch it to php at runtime with ini_set() before calling session_start(). Session upload progress must also still be enabled," PHP said last month. Noting that patches would be released as part of the regular update cycle, PHP developers recommended disabling session upload, a setting also listed as a mitigation for CVE-2026-109619, one of the two RCE bugs tracked as PHP2Shell.

Rebuilding my local voice assistant pipeline
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
20:57:14 UTC
05:57:14 KST

Rebuilding my voice assistant pipeline
Swapping openwakeword for microwakeword and making voice detection smarter
I wrote an article a couple of months ago describing how I rebuilt my old voice assistant pipeline in Platypush.
And I wrote earlier articles describing my journey through self-hosted voice assistants - in 2024 (with Picovoice), in 2020 (diving into the legacy assistant.google integration, the now defunct assistant.alexa integration, the now defunct Snowboy integration, the now defunct DeepSpeech integration and Picovoice) and the original article in 2019 describing the Google Assistant integration.
For a couple of months I used the voice pipeline described in my latest article as my main driver, but after getting used to the robustness of commercial voice assistants I was unhappy with the high rates of false positives/negatives of my self-hosted pipeline, as well as its lack of robustness in detecting start/end of speech, especially in noisy environments and using cheap microphones.
After a bit of experimentation, I decided to swap and fine tune a few parts, and after a couple of days of trying the results are satisfying enough for me to write a short article about it.
The resulting pipeline is:
Wake word detection: a new assistant.microwakeword plugin
Until recently I used assistant.openwakeword, which wraps openWakeWord.
It works, but the project has been quiet for a while and all of its pre-trained models are v0.1 files from 2023, mostly trained on synthetic speech.
In practice I was getting both false positives (the hotword triggering on background noise) and false negatives (the hotword not detected unless I was close to the microphone).
I added a new plugin, assistant.microwakeword, on top of microWakeWord through the pymicro-wakeword Python bindings.
microWakeWord is the engine used by ESPHome and Home Assistant Voice, its models are actively maintained, and the built-in models (okay_nabu, hey_jarvis, hey_mycroft, alexa) ship with the package — no download required.
The current official models are provided under the ESPHome micro-wake-word-models repository.
Custom models are supported too: drop a microWakeWord JSON manifest (with its .tflite model) in the plugin's models directory and reference it by name.
The plugin exposes the same API as assistant.openwakeword (same models, detection_threshold, frame_duration, patience_frames, input_device, input_volume parameters, same list_models action, same HotwordDetectedEvent), so switching is a matter of just changing the configuration to point to the new plugin:
assistant.microwakeword:
  models:
    - alexa
A few design notes:
- Without an explicit detection_threshold, each model uses the probability_cutoff from its own manifest (0.9 for alexa), which is the value the model was validated against.
- Models already smooth their output over a sliding window, so unlike openwakeword, patience_frames defaults to 0 here.
- The audio thread never touches the TFLite models directly: it queues frames (and a reset marker) to the main thread, because MicroWakeWord.reset() reloads the interpreter and isn't safe to call concurrently.
- Like assistant.openwakeword, the plugin releases the audio device when another assistant plugin starts a conversation, and re-acquires it when the conversation ends.
Speech-to-text: assistant.openai, with a much better ear
assistant.openai records audio when a conversation starts and transcribes it through Whisper.
The tricky part has always been end-of-speech detection: knowing when the user has finished talking. The previous implementation used a per-frame boolean from WebRTC VAD, and any single frame classified as speech would reset the silence timer. With even a bit of background noise, phrases would only end when the maximum conversation duration was hit.
The audio preprocessing layer (AudioPreprocessor, shared by the STT integrations) has been reworked:
- Silero VAD as the primary backend (through onnxruntime, no PyTorch required), with WebRTC VAD and a plain RMS-based detector as fallbacks. To get an idea of Silero VAD's performance compared against other solutions for speech activity detection, take a look at the insightful article One voice detector to rule them all. The Silero model is downloaded on first use into the Platypush workdir and checksum-verified:
assistant.openai:
  # auto = silero -> webrtc -> energy, depending on what's installed
  vad_backend: auto
- Hysteresis + sliding window: a frame becomes speech above the VAD threshold and stays speech until its score drops by a margin, and the per-frame decisions are smoothed by majority vote over a 0.5s window. A single noise burst can no longer keep a conversation alive.
- Adaptive noise floor: the background noise level is tracked over time, and frames that aren't louder than noise_floor_factor (default 2.0, ~6 dB) times the floor are never considered as speech.
On a synthetic benchmark (Piper-generated phrases mixed with babble and burst noise), the old settings kept conversations open for 6–10 seconds after the speech had ended; the new pipeline ends them 1.0–1.6 seconds after the last word, which is the configured conversation_end_timeout plus the smoothing lag.
Since the changes on Platypush have been performed directly in the AudioPreprocessor also used by other native speech-to-text engines, other speech-to-text plugins also benefit from them - e.g. if you use assistant.vosk for full on-device detection.
Responses and context: the openai plugin
The transcribed phrase is processed by the openai plugin, which handles both the API key and the conversation context. By default the assistant renders the response through the configured tts_plugin; if the response ends with a question mark, the conversation continues with a follow-up.
Custom hooks can intercept phrases before the default response kicks in — for example, to handle home automation commands locally without an LLM round-trip:
from platypush import run, when
from platypush.message.event.assistant import SpeechRecognizedEvent
@when(SpeechRecognizedEvent, phrase='play (the)? music')
def play_music():
    run('music.mpd.play')
And the hotword event is wired to the conversation start with a similar hook:
from platypush import run, when
from platypush.message.event.assistant import HotwordDetectedEvent
@when(HotwordDetectedEvent)
def on_hotword(**_):
    run('assistant.openai.start_conversation')
Text-to-speech: tts.openai
Responses are rendered by tts.openai, with volume and voice configurable. In my tests however tts.piper also yielded good results, and it runs entirely locally. In my tests on a RaspberryPi 5 however it added ~3-4s of latency to process the response to audio compared to tts.openai.
Since the TTS output goes to the speakers and the wake word detector listens on the microphone, the assistant plugins pause hotword detection while a conversation is active — otherwise the assistant would hear itself.
The full configuration
openai:
  api_key: YOUR_API_KEY
  # Or, for a custom setup using a model provider compatible with the OpenAI API,
  # e.g. a local Ollama instance:
  # base_url: http://localhost:8000/v1
  # model: gpt-5
  # Optional context that can be passed to initialize the assistant
  # context:
  #   role: system
  #   content: You are a useful assistant.
assistant.microwakeword:
  models:
    - alexa
assistant.openai:
  tts_plugin: tts.openai
  conversation_start_sound: ~/sounds/wake_word_detected.wav
  input_volume: 100
# assistant.vosk:
#   model: /path/to/vosk/model
#   conversation_start_sound: ~/sounds/wake_word_detected.wav
#   input_volume: 100
tts.openai:
  output_volume: 80.0
# Or, for a fully local setup that uses tts.piper:
# tts.piper:
#   output_volume: 80.0
What changed on the code side
- New assistant.microwakeword plugin: microWakeWord hotword detection through pymicro-wakeword, same API as assistant.openwakeword, built-in models bundled with the package, custom models via JSON manifests.
- assistant.openwakeword fixes: frames are now 80ms (1280 samples, the size openWakeWord recommends) instead of 500ms — longer chunks only return the highest score over their sub-frames and can miss hotwords — plus a patience_frames parameter (consecutive frames above threshold) and an explicit detection_threshold parameter (default 0.5).
- Shared AudioPreprocessor rework: Silero/WebRTC/energy VAD backends with vad_backend: auto selection, threshold hysteresis, sliding-window majority vote, and an adaptive noise floor. Used by both assistant.openai and assistant.vosk.
- New Silero VAD wrapper: ONNX inference through onnxruntime, recurrent state carried across frames, checksum-verified model download into the workdir.
Caveats
The microphone on my RPi doesn't support 16 kHz natively, so audio is captured at 44.1 kHz and software-resampled — it works, but if detection feels sluggish software resampling is usually the first suspect.
The VAD and threshold defaults were tuned on synthetic audio plus a couple of my audio recordings.
Different rooms, microphones and acoustics may yield different results. You may want to adjust detection_threshold on the wake word plugin and noise_floor_factor on the STT plugins if you don't feel satisfied with your mileage.
Reactions

A New Jean-Michel Basquiat Documentary Considers the Artist Behind the Work
Unknown
Unknown Publisher

A New Jean-Michel Basquiat Documentary Considers the Artist Behind the Work
A forthcoming documentary spotlights the life and work of luminary artist Jean-Michel Basquiat (1960-1988). Directed by Quinn Whitney Wilson and Viridiana Lieberman for Netflix, the feature film is the first project authorized by the artist’s two younger sisters, Lisane Basquiat and Jeanine Heriveaux, who helm his estate.
In a trailer, the siblings describe their brother’s energetic approach to life and how the story typically told about his meteoric rise omits so much of who he really was. Simply titled Jean-Michel Basquiat, the film features archival footage and audio, along with never-before-seen works and commentary from his family. The documentary seeks to venture beyond the mythology that’s come to surround Basquiat, who died of an accidental overdose at just 27 and whose work has continued to captivate the art world.
As one sister says in the trailer, “he was unapologetically Jean-Michel Basquiat.” The documentary will be available for streaming globally on December 11.

Meta’s Muse Charm might cost $300

Meta is planning to price the Muse Charm, a little gadget with a screen that lets you chat with your Muse AI agent, at $300, according to The Information. CEO Mark Zuckerberg revealed the gadget earlier this year and said the company expected to ship the Charm “in time for the holidays in December,” but he didn’t provide a price at the time.
Meta’s Muse Charm might cost $300
The Information has reported a potential price for the Muse Charm, which is expected to ship in time for the holidays.
When he announced the Muse Charm, Zuckerberg said that the company had “packed the whole Muse experience,” including real-time voice and avatar technology, into the gadget. It has a fingerprint sensor, and you can tap it to be able to talk to a Muse AI agent without unlocking a phone or opening an app. The Information reports that the Muse Charm will come with a free 12-month 5G data plan from Verizon.
Spokesperson Tyler Yee wrote to The Information that “We’re charmed by the interest in our AI devices, and we’ll have more to share soon.” When reached for comment by The Verge, Yee provided the same statement.
The company is also working on a smartwatch, The Information says, but a launch for that has been pushed to 2027 as Meta tries to get Muse Charm out the door.
Meta also open sourced code to let users make their own Muse gadgets, which my colleague Sean Hollister used to make his own DIY Muse Charm.
Update, November 19th: Noted statement given by Meta spokesperson Tyler Yee.

Tubemap Mark Two: A geographically-accurate diagram of the London Underground
United Kingdom
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
20:21:15 GMT
05:21:15 KST

- Tube Map Mark Two A new version of the geographically-accurate diagram of the London Underground system
Tubemap Mark Two
A geographically-accurate diagram of the London Underground system
The New London Tubemap project is alternative look at mapping the London Underground system with the stations positioned so they are more geographically accurate in relation to each other. It is intended to help people, particularly those unfamiliar with the city, to relate the underground system to London at street level.
This second version, building on the original first published in 2011, anticipates the opening of the Elizabeth Line (formally known as Crossrail) and the extension to the Northern Line. An on-going project, this will continue to develop as indeed the network it represents will.
Purchase the Map
A pocket-sized version of the map
A2 folding to A6 – is available to buy from the Lettering Arts Trust shop. The price includes a donation to the charity.
A poster-sized version
of the original 2011 design is also available through the shop.
Origins
I think it’s safe to say that I never set out to redesign the London Underground map. The original design, conceived by Harry Beck in 1932, is a classic and one of the best pieces of graphic design of the 20th Century; I hold it in very high regard.
However, friends of mine from outside of London commented that they found the current map hard to use and in particular found the disconnect between the map and London at street level confusing.
This sparked my curiosity as a designer, would it be possible to design something as clear as the Beck map but manage to be more geographically accurate? That ‘thought experiment’ eventually expanded to become the complete alternative map of the system published in 2011.
With the imminent arrival of Crossrail (or the Elizabeth Line as it will be known), I thought it was time to update my map. Looking back at the design I published nearly ten years beforehand, I found I really didn’t like it very much any more. It’s a bit busy, a bit fussy in some places, slightly chaotic in others. One comment on the original was from writer and actor Mark Gatiss who described it as ‘aesthetically frightful’, and revisiting it, I think he has a point.
One of the hardest things to do as a designer is to put an idea to one side and come up with something different but that is what I did. Instead of just adding the new line to the old design, I rebuilt the whole thing from scratch. This time as well as the geographical parameter, I concentrated on making a simpler, more elegant solution adopting the 45º lines of the classic Beck original.
It is work in progress and will continue to change and develop as indeed the network it represents will continue to do. I have said all along that this is not intended to replace the standard map based on Beck’s original design, it’s simply another way of looking at it.
A note about the place name origins map
The version of the map showing the origins of the names of places on the London Underground system is a collaboration with writer Mark Forsyth www.inkyfool.com
This etymological guide is an updated and expanded version of The Etymological Map of the London Underground first published by Mark in 2011. It has now been expanded to include London Overground, the Croydon Tramlink, and the Elizabeth Line.

Google is putting AI chats right on the homepage
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
20:05:06 UTC
05:05:06 KST

Google is updating its homepage to include AI searches. In a post on X, Google’s vice president of product, Robby Stein, shows how you can now open a collapsible sidebar to view and continue your AI chats.
Google is putting AI chats right on the homepage
You can now view your recent AI searches by opening the sidebar on Google’s homepage.
Based on the GIF shared by Stein, it looks like you can open the sidebar by clicking the gray bar that appears on the left side of your screen. From there, Google will show a list of the searches you’ve made using AI Mode, along with the ability to start a new thread, search your threads, or connect a Gemini Notebook.
It’s yet another big change for Google’s homepage, which has long presented you with a single bar to kick off your web search. The company brought its AI Mode button to its search bar last year, which opens up a chatbot-like search experience within Google. In May, Google updated its search box to accommodate longer queries and added AI-powered autocomplete.
Stein says Google is rolling out the option because “people are coming to Search with more questions that aren’t just one and done,” adding that “in testing, this sidebar made a big difference for making progress & continuing to explore the web.” The new homepage is rolling out globally on desktop.

Google Will Launch Android App Verification Requirement Globally In January 2027
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
20:00:00 UTC
05:00:00 KST

Google Will Launch Android App Verification Requirement Globally In January 2027 (arstechnica.com) 98
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from Ars Technica: Google announced Android developer verification last year, and despite numerous objections from developers and users, the company is forging ahead. It began blocking the installation of unregistered apps in some countries this fall, and the system is expanding globally soon. Google says it will start rolling out the requirement in all regions in January 2027, and it urges devs who still haven't registered their apps to do so soon.
The apps you download from the Play Store all come from verified developers, and Google's new requirement expands that requirement to apps from third-party sources. Once developer verification is live in your region, you won't be able to sideload apps unless the developer has registered with Google, which is framed as a security measure. Verification requires providing government identification and paying a $25 fee, which will allegedly make it harder for nefarious actors to distribute malware and scammy apps.
In September, Google launched verification in Brazil, Indonesia, Singapore, and Thailand. Apparently, nothing exploded, so the company is now ready to expand. It previously said verification would go global in 2027, and it's not wasting any time with the January start. It says that verification will reach all devices within "several months." So far, Google is getting what it wanted from this initiative, with "millions" of non-Play apps already registered. The company says this accounts for more than 99 percent of global Android app installs.

FCC greenlights rule allowing prisons to jam cellphone signals
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
19:40:28 UTC
04:40:28 KST

The Federal Communications Commission voted unanimously to allow state and local prisons to jam cellphones. In an announcement on Thursday, the FCC argues the rule will help combat an “influx of contraband cellphones” used to carry out crimes from prison.
FCC greenlights rule allowing prisons to jam cellphone signals
The FCC passed the rule despite warnings that jammers could block lawful communications, like 911 calls.
Jammers are generally banned under Section 333 of the Communications Act of 1934, though they’re used at some federal facilities. FCC Chair Brendan Carr claims that giving correctional facilities access to this technology will help prevent prisoners from using phones for illegal activity. “These devices [phones] are being used by inmates to order hits, run drug operations, traffic women and children, and unleash mayhem on our communities from behind bars,” Carr says.
In the months leading up to the vote, trade groups and human rights advocates have raised concerns about the risks of blocking cellphone signals in prisons. The US Chamber of Commerce and the CTIA, a wireless association representing the nation’s biggest carriers, have both warned in letters to the FCC that jamming technology could disrupt lawful communications outside prisons, including 911 calls. Meanwhile, the American Civil Liberties Union (ACLU) has long argued that cellphones can keep prisoners safe by allowing them to document safety and human rights issues that the outside world may not see.
FCC Commissioner Anna Gomez, the sole Democrat on the Commission, voted for the measure despite the risk of “potential unintended consequences,” according to the Associated Press. “I want to make sure that we don’t harm public safety in the name of helping public safety,” Gomez added.
The FCC says prisons that choose to jam cellphone signals will need to obtain a license, adhere to “technical standards and testing requirements,” and ensure that the jamming technology doesn’t interfere with communications outside the facility. Prisons must also notify nearby communities of testing and when the technology is being deployed.

The Big Idea: Melissa Poett
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
19:37:50 UTC
04:37:50 KST

The Big Idea: Melissa Poett
Posted on November 19, 2026 Posted by Athena Scalzi 1 Comment
What’s better than a whip-smart, whip-wielding protagonist? One who uses those skills to combat injustice and help those in need. Robin Hood fans rejoice, for author Melissa Poett has brought us a dystopian, femme-fatale retelling, without the merry men. Clutch your coin purses and follow along in the Big Idea for A Thief to Die For.
MELISSA POETT:
When you hear the name Robin Hood, perhaps you think about a revolution. Or maybe it’s more specific—you think of a medieval man who was radicalized to defend the powerless against the tyranny of the corrupt elite. A hero. A leader. Maybe even a criminal, an outlaw who was willing to break the rules to right the scales of justice in a tale as old as time, and one that, unfortunately, remains timely.
I, too, think of all those things. And yet, I was also left with a question: how would that story have unfolded differently if the same revolution was led by a woman?
More specifically, Marian.
A Thief to Die For is my reimagining of Robin Hood set in a dystopian world. It’s about a whip-wielding Marian who has made it her mission to sabotage her tyrant father, while risking the kind of love wars are fought over.
True to the original legend of Robin Hood, injustice is the catalyst. Except, it wasn’t killing a deer in the King’s forest or a fight with the sheriff’s men that started this rebellion. In A Thief to Die For, Marian is radicalized by growing up under her horrific father—a tyrant who runs a slave operation in a coal mine. And her simmering rage is why she steals from his wagons to help the people trapped under his control.
But this is where the story you know and love takes an unconventional turn. Where Robin Hood showcases his mastery of the bow and arrow while recruiting an army of charismatic men to fight with him, Marian’s path as the heroine looks quite different. I imagine she would move through the world sacrificially—like a woman.
This isn’t a spoiler, so don’t worry. But it is the foundation to Marian’s character. In a world where she can’t dethrone her father without bringing the house of cards down, she must think outside the box. Move strategically. Outsmart the powerful, while prioritizing the weakest around her. She’s an everyday woman considering all the consequences of a confrontation, and every way it could go wrong. Is she capable of violence? Yes, occasionally. Especially when she secretly raids. Is she excellent with her weapon of choice—a whip? Most definitely. But as any number of women can tell you, there’s a moral burden we carry that isn’t seen in the original folktale of Robin Hood. It means Marian’s revolt is exercised in the shadows as she prioritizes the protection of the people around her. There is no ever-growing army of people joining her rebellion. Marian sacrifices herself by raiding alone.
Which brings me to the next significant shift in the narrative, an epic love story. Yes, I can hear you reminding me that the original folktale did include romance. You’re right; Robin Hood got the girl in the end. But I’m not talking about some proverbial cherry on top of an already half-eaten sundae. I’m talking about an epic love so foundational to the story that it’s as consequential as taking down the villain. Give me yearning. Break my heart.
Now that’s the kind of reimagining I want to read—a story of Robin Hood, a revolution reimagined. But this time through the lens of Marian’s sacrifice and epic love.
—-
A Thief to Die For: Amazon|Barnes & Noble|Bookshop|Powell’s|Canada Pre-order|UK Pre-order

Sony’s WH-1000XM5 are cheaper than ever for Black Friday

You can now buy a Steam Machine without getting on a waitlist
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
19:33:22 UTC
04:33:22 KST

Valve announced some updates about Steam Machine availability today, including good news if you want to buy one right now and potential bad news about future supply due to RAMaggedon.
You can now buy a Steam Machine without getting on a waitlist
Valve’s 512GB Steam Machine is available to buy now, but the 2TB model is out of stock.
The good news: You can now purchase a 512GB Steam Machine without getting on a waitlist. Initially, interested buyers had to get on Valve’s waitlist if they wanted to purchase one, but the console-like PC is now available in an “add-to-cart” status, according to the company. As I write this, the 512GB versions with and without a bundled controller are showing estimated delivery dates of three to five business days on Valve’s website. If Valve sells through its current inventory, the company says it will indicate that the product is out of stock instead of making another waitlist.
The bad news: If you’re on the waitlist for a 2TB model, you might not get a chance to buy one at all, as Valve says there are more people on the waitlist than there are units available. The company is closing the waitlists for its 2TB models.
Valve says that while it’s still building more Steam machines, “memory and storage costs continue to increase at unprecedented levels” and that it doesn’t know the number it can make or “at what price we’ll be able to offer them.” The 2TB Steam Machine is “especially constrained.” The company anticipates “having more information” to share about additional supply in January.
After delaying the Steam Machine’s expected early 2026 launch due to the memory and storage crisis, Valve launched the product in June with a price starting at $1,049 for the 512GB model without a bundled controller. A 2TB model without a controller costs $1,349.

Some creators are skeptical of Instagram’s new affiliate program
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
19:12:30 UTC
04:12:30 KST

For years, influencers have come up with workarounds on social media to figure out how to share affiliate links with followers. A new integration between Instagram and Amazon was announced earlier this month as a way to make it easier for influencers to make money from affiliate links — but some content creators are suspicious about who really benefits.
Some creators are skeptical of Instagram’s new affiliate program
Influencers are now able to link their Amazon and Instagram accounts to promote products. Some are concerned about what they’re opting in to.
The new integration allows creators to connect their Instagram and Amazon accounts and automatically tag products with their affiliate links, from which they earn a commission when followers make purchases. Influencers will be able to embed up to 30 product links in a Reel or 20 in a feed post, but a permissions setting has led to concerns about what creators are actually agreeing to.
In connecting an Amazon account, creators must agree to allow the retailer and its brand sellers to create ads from “any public content” that includes a related affiliate link. Influencers must also agree to acknowledge that brands can edit their content and use their “testimonial, caption or media” for ads.
The wide-ranging permissions are giving creators pause. Some have urged peers not to connect their accounts without reviewing the permissions and understanding how the feature would affect earnings. People making money on social media spend years tweaking their content and strategy to maximize reach, engagement, and, by extension, their bottom line. For some, it appeared that they would be giving Amazon and its sellers the ability to use their content under unclear terms. On Instagram head Adam Mosseri’s post announcing the feature, influencers complained about the lack of clarity, questioned whether using the feature would decrease their reach (which some creators say happens when they link to outside products), and demanded answers to whether their commission rate would stay the same when Amazon or brands use their content for ads.
Linking products through the new integration won’t reduce reach, Jimmy O’Keefe, Instagram’s director of product marketing, said in an email to The Verge. O’Keefe also noted that creators will continue to earn commission through affiliate links when Amazon boosts their posts, and that they can stop the boosting in settings or disconnect the integration entirely. There’s also a separate, manual way to add products without linking accounts, if a creator wants to opt out of having their content made into ads.
Amazon has an enormous influencer program that reaches across social media platforms. It is so lucrative that some content creators are able to make a living almost exclusively by hawking products for sale on Amazon, building empires on the shopping habits of their followers. When it comes to putting those links in front of followers, though, social platforms have historically been a roadblock, hence the cottage industry of link-in-bio tools meant to circumvent limits. Slowly but surely, though, platforms like Instagram, Facebook, and YouTube — perhaps chasing the success of TikTok Shop — have added more and more shopping features. The result is that shopping and social media begin to feel more and more like the same thing.

Anthropic’s IPO could deliver $156 billion to charity
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
19:10:45 UTC
04:10:45 KST

Anthropic’s blockbuster IPO has made quite a few people generationally wealthy, but it will also be an enormous windfall for the causes backed by the frontier lab and its employees.
As you might expect from a company whose leadership emerged from the world of effective altruism (EA), Anthropic has multiple different avenues for cash raised in its market debut to be directed to philanthropy.
First, the company’s Public Benefit Fund, or PBF (analogous to OpenAI’s foundation), was allotted 5% of the company’s shares, almost 850 million. At the current trading price of about $95, that will leave it with an endowment of $80 billion. And if the company continues its astonishing growth rate, Anthropic could add further 2% increases every time the company’s valuation grows more than 50% over the IPO price of $83, or about $124.50 per share for the next threshold.
The PBF’s money will be focused on research and activities related to increasing the benefit of artificial intelligence to humanity. The first commitment of the PBF was $200 million to study the impact of AI on workers; Anthropic has said it will consider donations to philanthropic organizations or “direct distributions to specific populations” — perhaps a hint of future UBI plans.
Anthropic also donated $50 million to the city of San Francisco last week. A company spokesperson tells TechCrunch the donation did not come from the PBF.
Next, Anthropic’s employees have pledged shares to philanthropic causes of their choice, and the company will match those donations 3-to-1 for longer tenured employees and 1-to-1 for newer ones. In its S-1 filing, the company said that it had reserved more than 550 million shares to fulfill pledges made before September 30.
Working backwards from that figure, and based on the disclosure that 84% of the shares were reserved for early employee matches, we can estimate that employees have pledged $23 billion of donations; the company’s match on those would be roughly $53 billion, depending on the stock price when those matches are delivered.
All told, it adds up to $156 billion — a huge amount of potential philanthropy. For context, Americans donated $617 billion to charity in 2025; if all of the pledged funds resulting from today’s transactions get to do-gooders, that will be equal to about a quarter of all U.S. donations made last year.
Employees will need to make good on their pledges for this to work out, and the value of the match will ultimately depend on when Anthropic makes its donation. The shares could also go to donor-advised funds, which would likely limit their initial impact.
Interviews with employees and institutions suggest that donors are most likely to support EA-inflected charities like Longview Philanthropy, GiveWell, Coefficient Giving, Foresight Institute, and Giving What We Can. And it seems likely that significant funding will be allocated to AI safety research, given the concerns expressed about that subject by many Anthropic employees.

Palo Alto Networks Signs $4.5 Billion Deal For Applied Compute 2
			
		 	
				Palo Alto Networks has agreed to acquire AI startup Applied Compute for $4.5 billion as it expands beyond traditional cybersecurity into securing enterprise AI systems, including the training and deployment of open-weight models. The deal is expected to close in the third quarter of fiscal 2027. Reuters reports:  Palo Alto Networks has been repositioning itself as an integrated cybersecurity provider, with recent deals, including its $3.35 billion acquisition of cloud management and monitoring firm Chronosphere and an agreement with Israeli startup Koi. The company is looking to tap growing cybersecurity spending as the rapid adoption of AI creates new security risks and gives attackers new ways to exploit vulnerabilities.
"AI tools are surfacing vulnerabilities faster than companies can patch them, and that is pushing customers to consolidate onto fewer platforms," said Catharine Trebnick, senior research analyst at Rosenblatt Securities. With the acquisition of Applied Compute, Palo Alto aims to offer a technology platform that helps customers securely train and deploy open-weight models as they expand their use of AI.

How Andrew Badr put a 1940s/1950s Lebanese women’s magazine online
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
18:35:37 UTC
03:35:37 KST

My dad once told me that his father had published the first feminist magazine in Lebanon1, called Sawt al-Mara (صوت المرأة, “The Woman’s Voice”). I’m excited to share that the magazine’s contents are now online at https://www.andrewbadr.com/sawt/, along with English translations.
The rest of this post outlines this archival preservation project and its backstory.
My grandfather, Fuad, had founded a publishing house in Beirut in his late twenties. Among some more academic journals and books, they published Sawt al-Mara. Growing up, I knew very little about the man—we never met—so learning all this was a surprise, and curiosity about him was part of my motivation in undertaking this project. Beyond my dad’s claim above, my first encounter with the magazine itself was when I found a few copies on eBay.
Like a kid, I liked the pictures, but I don’t read Arabic. Understanding what Sawt was really about felt far away. Only when AI started getting much better did a broader digitization and preservation effort become conceivable.
Even seeing these first issues pained me a bit, from an archival preservation perspective. It was clear—not particular to this magazine, but exemplified by it—how much work and care had gone into them. And yet there was very little about the magazine online. It was mentioned in a few scholarly articles, but seemed at risk of being forgotten.
Last December, when I was in Beirut for the holidays, I visited the library at the American University of Beirut. There, I found what turned out to be nearly the entire run of the magazine. They were able to scan all of their volumes for me2—145 issues, or about 7,000 pages worth. Although this lost some of the fabulous color prints, it allowed me to work with the data in bulk. (I had brought a camera to try photographing every page, but discovered that this tedious task would have taken the bulk of my time in the country.)
The first step was to OCR the data, turning the scanned images into digital text. As good as modern OCR software is, it’s not optimized for Arabic, noisy scans, or multi-column layouts, so this wasn’t trivial. I then had to stitch articles back together across pages, sometimes far apart in the magazine, before they could be translated. Finally, in the most novel technical step, AI turned each page of the magazine into an HTML page, both in Arabic and English3. (You can see an example in the screenshot above.) This wouldn’t have been feasible even two years ago—or rather, it would have cost millions of dollars, rather than thousands. Along the way, I also uploaded PDFs of the magazine scans to the Internet Archive for long-term preservation. [Update, Nov. 2026: taken down for now.]
Now, I was able to explore the full range of the magazine. There was poetry, fiction, criticism, fashion, news, society pages, and more. I did learn more about my grandfather—like that he was in a play. And found his marriage announcement, as well as articles by other family members, like this one by my great-aunt Lily, whom I only knew as a formidable opponent at playing cards.
I hope this is a useful resource. I plan to write posts going into more details on much of the above, but wanted to share this initial announcement first.
It turns out that Sawt al-Mara was not the first feminist magazine in Lebanon. There are several better contenders.
AUB doesn’t have the whole run of the magazine. In particular, I haven’t been able to find the first year, when it was published in a newspaper format.
This pipeline—transforming foreign-language scans to translated HTML layouts—seems generally useful. I may try it on a few other magazines and see how it goes.

Sonos is taking up to 30 percent off speakers and soundbars right now

For a more immersive experience, you can even pair two for stereo sound or use them as rear surrounds with a compatible Sonos soundbar.
If you want a speaker you can take on the go, the newer Sonos Play is available from Sonos, Amazon, and Best Buy for $239 ($60 off), which is the first notable discount we’ve seen. The portable speaker delivers clear, detailed sound that can fill both larger rooms and outdoor spaces. Plus, its removable carrying loop makes it easy to bring along, and you can even use it to top off your phone. Like other Sonos speakers, it supports both Wi-Fi and Bluetooth, and you can also group it with other Sonos speakers over Bluetooth when you’re outdoors and away from Wi-Fi.
Last but not least, you can also buy the Sonos Ace for $229 ($120 off) at Amazon, Best Buy, and directly from Sonos, which marks a new low price. The newer Ace Ultra offer better sound and improved noise cancellation, but the Ace is still a solid option, especially if you already own a Sonos soundbar. Unlike the newer Ultra, though, they can’t transfer music from your other Sonos speakers to your headphones. However, their TV Audio Swap feature still lets you transfer audio from a compatible Sonos soundbar to your headphones, so you can watch movies or listen to music playing on your TV without disturbing anyone else.
