Yoshitomo Nara’s Beloved Wide-Eyed Figures Return to New York
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
18:28:00 UTC
03:28:00 KST

Yoshitomo Nara’s Beloved Wide-Eyed Figures Return to New York
The first time I encountered Yoshitomo Nara’s work was when I opened the mailbox as an angsty teenager. A friend a few years older had recently visited the Walker Art Center, where she bought a postcard printed with two of the artist’s portraits and scribbled a note to her friend (me) still stuck in their tiny Wisconsin hometown. As is wont to happen to teenagers on the cusp of adulthood, I took that postcard as a sort of guiding light—a reminder that the world was bigger than the tractors and small-town gossip I was surrounded by—and promptly cut and taped the works to fit within my clear phone case. I carried this pair of strange characters everywhere I went for years after.
Nara also was born and raised in a rural region, albeit on the other side of the globe. The artist has described his childhood in Aomori on the northern tip of Japan’s main island as solitary and removed from the culture of the cities, instead flanked by the endless expanse of the Pacific Ocean and the snowy ridges of the Hakkōda Mountains. These early years indelibly informed his sensibility, and even in his most emotionally charged works, there’s a deep sense of calm that comes from immersion in nature.
In his first solo presentation in New York in almost a decade, Nara takes over two of David Zwirner’s galleries. The self-titled exhibition includes nearly 100 works from the last several decades, spanning paintings, drawings, and monumental bronze sculptures. Central are the artist’s signature childlike figures with large heads and eyes and an underlying sense of innocence, their skeptical, bored, and even rebellious expressions providing a necessary counterbalance—one that also spoke to my teenage sensitivities.
Profoundly enigmatic, Nara’s works are also instantly recognizable, relatable, and broadly appealing. According to the gallery, 4,000 visitors lined up to see the exhibition on opening night, with 26,000 attending in the first week alone.
Ranging in scale from intimate miniatures to larger-than-life, Nara’s two-dimensional portraits emerge from soft, pastel-hued palettes. Several also often contain pithy phrases that hearken to our collective challenges. Both “world peace” and “tomorrow’s far away” drip with longing and speak to the artist’s broader concern for the contentious state of human relations.
Introducing clay into his practice also emerged from unease and grief, when Nara picked up the earthen material after the 2011 Tōhoku earthquake. The disastrous event killed nearly 20,000 people and injured and displaced many more around the artist’s hometown. In the moment, the tactile medium offered a reprieve the canvas couldn’t.
In the exhibition, Nara’s monochromatic bronzes, coated in white urethane paint, capture these forays into three-dimensions and evoke the icy, snow-blanketed landscapes of his youth. Based on smaller ceramic prototypes, the sculptures bear the traces of the handmade, as if he had sculpted each head with an enormous thumb.
As an exhibition statement says, Nara’s overarching impulse is simple: he “creates art in order to express and communicate what he feels unable to convey in words, and his distinctive body of work has evolved through his ongoing search to identify what is uniquely his: his upbringing in rural northern Japan at a particular moment in time and, above all, his profound concern for humanity, including those outside mainstream society.”
The artist recently wrapped up a large-scale project at the Hirshorn Sculpture Garden, and next fall, the San Francisco Museum of Modern Art will host a major retrospective of his work. Until then, see Yoshitomo Nara through December 12 in New York.

====================================================================================================

Chile: UFO Sightings by 18th Century Jesuits
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
18:26:28 UTC
03:26:28 KST

Chile: UFO Sightings by 18th Century Jesuits
Source:IIEE Chile
JESUITS IN THE 16TH CENTURY OBSERVED UFOS
The Jesuits pioneered a revolutionary form of Christian socialism with the Guaraní people of Paraguay, challenging the greed of Spanish colonists and Portuguese *bandeirantes*. This atypical system—a model of social organization that flourished in Paraguay among the indigenous population and the Jesuits—was known as "reductions." This took place in the early 17th century (around 1607).
Upon arriving in the region, the religious order encountered the *encomienda* system, under which the Franciscans—in a sense—continued the regime of the Conquest in their dealings with the indigenous people. The Jesuits vehemently opposed this system; while the concept of "reductions" did not originate with them, they utilized this model to counter the *encomienda* system employed by the Franciscans.
In researching this history, we discovered that the archives of the Society of Jesus contain records of extraordinary incidents. They describe countless events revealing that this region—like so many others in South America—is a source of mysteries and enigmas, and that the UFO phenomenon accompanied the Jesuits and indigenous people as they laid the foundations of this new nation. There is a document in these archives that states the following:
"At the San Ignacio de Ipané mission, on August 10, 1631, something strange and luminous was seen streaking across the sky. Between 6 and 7 in the evening, a luminous orb of unusual size was seen rising in the east; it moved not hurriedly, but rather slowly, over the settlement, resembling a full moon. It cast off a great number of sparks toward the east, and upon reaching the western horizon, it flared up with intense light before going dark; then, after the time it takes to recite the Creed, it produced a tremendous blast like a thunderclap."
A few kilometers from this mission lies an area in the Chaco province known as *Campo del Cielo* ("Field of the Sky"—named for the vast number of meteorites that once fell there). While it is natural to consider the possibility of another meteorite, a word-for-word analysis of the document—such as the one conducted by researcher Antonio Las Heras in one of his excellent works on the subject—inevitably raises doubts as to whether the observed object was, in fact, a meteorite.
The object was traveling slowly; the phrase "was seen rising in the east" gives the immediate impression that it "gained altitude." This suggests it may have been one of the earliest recorded instances of a UFO lifting off from the ground in this region, which spans the province of Misiones near the borders with Brazil and Paraguay. Without delving into the nuances explored by this distinguished Argentine researcher, we believe the document in question is valuable; it provides evidence that we have long been visited by these flying objects—objects whose origins we still cannot define.
18TH CENTURY – ANOTHER ACCOUNT
The Paraguayan War was a dramatic and extremely bloody conflict, as all wars are. Argentina, Uruguay, and Brazil participated, resulting in thousands of deaths among the combatants. It was precisely during this era that another significant UFO-related event was recorded in this small country. On November 26, 1866, a Brazilian brig commanded by Captain Augusto Leverger (later honored with the title of Baron of Melgaço) was sailing up the Paraguay River on a wartime mission when, while on deck at night, he observed a phenomenon that defied logical explanation. The event was recorded as follows:
"At 5:47 a.m.," Captain Leverger explains in his report, "with the sky perfectly clear and calm, I spotted a luminous globe that, with incredible speed, traced a 30-degree arc toward the northwest, leaving behind a streak of light in which three distinct bodies could be seen; their brightness was more intense than that of the streak itself and equaled—if it did not exceed—that of a full moon on a clear night. After 25 minutes of observation—during which part of the ship's crew also looked on—everything vanished abruptly, and there were no further atmospheric disturbances thereafter."
"In the city of Asunción," Leverger adds, "I spoke with the Brazilian ambassador and others who corroborated this account." Based on trigonometric calculations applied to the positions personally observed by Leverger, it is estimated that the phenomenon occurred within the atmosphere at a distance of only 59 leagues from Asunción.
This is the notarized text of Captain Leverger's statement. Subsequent inquiries made by the witness with authorities in Asunción determined that the sighting must have taken place in the atmosphere, just 59 leagues distant from the capital, and all investigations determined that the sighting was entirely consistent—and therefore of incalculable value. Through archival research, Marta Virginia Capalló—an Argentine researcher who tracked this event—determined that this might well be the first newspaper report on UFO sightings, predating even a story that appeared in the London *Times* in 1870.
To briefly summarize Captain Leverger’s sighting, it is nearly impossible to believe that the object he observed could have been manufactured using the technology available at the time. We must remember that aircraft only made their debut in the skies during the first decade of the 20th century, and test flights were sporadic. Explanations involving a meteorite or an atmospheric phenomenon also fail to align with Captain Leverger’s account, given the duration of the sighting: a full 25 minutes.
Little is known about the UFO phenomenon within the borders of Paraguay, a small country. Unfortunately, it has often made headlines due to the coup plotters of the day, though—more positively—it is also known for its immense passion for the continent's national sport: soccer.
It is no secret that the UFO phenomenon has a long history; we have frequently cited documents from the era of the Spanish Conquest and accounts by church chroniclers describing sightings and unusual events. These records reinforce the conviction that this phenomenon has always been with us; while its outward form may have changed over time, its essence remains the same—whether one likes it or not.

Google’s Gemini app for macOS now has computer use, meaning it can help you “get multi-step tasks done across your local files, desktop apps, and the web,” the company says. Google suggests the feature might be useful for things like speeding up workflows, filling in forms, making presentations, and listing items for sale.
Gemini on Mac can now use your computer
With the new computer use feature, Google says Gemini can better help with things like filling in forms and creative workflows.
Users will be able to decide which of your apps Gemini can access, and be able to follow along as Gemini works because it has its own background window that shows a live preview in your chat, Google says. The feature is rolling out now in English for users in the US.
Gemini is introducing the new feature ahead of Apple launching new restrictions for apps wanting “full disk access” on Mac due to potential risks from AI agents with computer use. A preview of the settings rolled out with this week’s macOS 27.3 beta.

New leaked images of Motorola’s next folding smartphone shared by SammyGuru add more credence to the theory that widescreen could be the default design for foldables moving forward. Following a single leaked shot shared to Reddit yesterday, this new crop of images reveal the Motorola Razr Flex from several angles in two finishes with its inner screen completely open and partially folded.
Leaked images reveal Motorola’s wide foldable
The Razr Flex will compete with the iPhone Duo, Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8, and other widescreen folding devices.
The photos show the Razr Flex in two finishes: coriander brown and dark botanical garden, according to SammyGuru. The brown option features a back panel with a bumpy leather-like finish, while the green has a fabric-like woven finish. On the back is a pill-shaped bar with two cameras and a flash.
The Flex is expected to be a smaller and more affordable alternative to the Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8, which has a 5.5-inch cover screen and a 7.6-inch main display when unfolded. But more specific details — including the size of the Razr Flex, its pricing, and when it’s expected to be debut — are still unknown.

Jon McCormack, Apple’s vice president of camera software engineering, left the company last month, Bloomberg reports. Apple’s iPhone camera leader has reportedly left the company
Bloomberg reports Jon McCormack retired last month, continuing Apple’s recent leadership shuffle.
McCormack played a major role in defining how iPhone photos look, and he was always ready to get into the weeds with reporters after iPhone events. In 2024, he told The Verge that Apple sees a photo as “a personal celebration of something that really, actually happened.”
The departure follows John Ternus taking over for Tim Cook as Apple’s CEO in September. Apple has had other major leadership changes recently, including Phil Schiller stepping down as head of the App Store and Apple events and Steve Smith stepping in as the company’s new M&A and corporate development boss.

Oh, Elon Musk is ‘moderate’ now, is he?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-19
18:09:53 UTC
03:09:53 KST

A little less than two years ago, Elon Musk stood onstage at the Conservative Political Action Conference, sunglasses on, black MAGA hat perched atop his head, wielding a chainsaw. Last night, Musk called in to The New York Times DealBook Summit and distanced himself from President Donald Trump’s movement.
Oh, Elon Musk is ‘moderate’ now, is he?
After spending nearly half a billion dollars to thwart Democrats, Musk tries to distance himself from MAGA.
“I think I’m ideologically quite moderate. I don’t think I have any kind of extreme political views,” Musk told DealBook editor Andrew Ross Sorkin. “You’ve known me for a long time. Can you think of an extreme view that I have?”
I can certainly think of a few.
Let’s start with the great replacement. For those who are blissfully unaware, the great replacement is a white nationalist conspiracy theory whose adherents believe that a cabal of powerful people is conspiring to replace white Americans and Europeans by promoting mass migration from the developing world. (The most hardcore great replacement types believe this is a grand Jewish conspiracy; the more politically palatable Fox News types blame Democrats.) As recently as this year, Musk was promoting the notion that white men would be “slaughtered” if they became a minority in the United States.
Musk is X’s most prolific promoter of anti-immigrant conspiracy theories, according to a Bloomberg analysis. At a 2024 campaign rally with Trump, Musk claimed that Democrats want to “take away your right to vote effectively.” His racist fearmongering extends beyond the US. At a rally for Germany’s far-right party last year, Musk encouraged Germans to not lose their national values “in some sort of multiculturalism that dilutes everything.” He encouraged Germans to be proud of their heritage, saying there was “frankly too much of a focus on past guilt and we need to move beyond that.” Musk posted about race nearly every single day in the month of January. “Whites,” he wrote, “are a rapidly dying minority.” It’s a claim he’s made multiple times. “If current trends continue, Whites will go from being a small minority of world population today to virtually extinct!” he said in December 2025. Musk, a birth rate obsessive, is doing his part to keep the white race alive. He has fathered at least 14 children with four different women.
In fact, fears of replacement animated Musk’s support for Republicans in the midterm elections, to the tune of nearly half a billion dollars. (At the DealBook Summit, Musk said he now feels “pretty foolish” for throwing so much money at Republican campaigns “and not seeing much for it.”) Musk himself said that if Democrats return to power, they’ll “open the borders and legalize everyone living in America illegally to cement a permanent majority.” You could ignore the dogwhistles and argue he meant a permanent political majority, but that would require you to ignore basically every other thing Musk has said about race, migration, and politics over the past four or so years. In 2024, for example, Musk amplified the viral far-right lie that Haitian immigrants in Springfield, Ohio, were eating people’s pets. “Vote for Kamala if you want this to happen to your neighborhood,” Musk posted on X.
The only thing that rivals Musk’s obsession with white birth rates is his fixation on trans people. Musk has repeatedly asserted that his daughter Vivian Wilson, from whom he is estranged, was “killed by the woke mind virus.” (Wilson, who is very much alive, was reportedly cut off from Musk’s health insurance after coming out as transgender.) Shortly after taking over X, Musk decided that the platform would consider the words “cis” and “cisgender” slurs. After J.K. Rowling equated trans activists’ rhetoric to Hitler’s musings on propaganda, Musk reposted her on X, adding, “Precisely.”
Sorkin feebly gestured at Musk’s comments about “some of the things happening in Europe and around immigration that people did think were far or extreme,” to say nothing of his MAGA affiliation. Musk countered that he doesn’t think these things are extreme, and also that he’s “not MAGA.”
“I’m not sure what MAGA means,” he continued.
An interesting thing for the architect of a key part of the MAGA agenda to say! The dizzying pace of news may make it hard to remember just how much damage Musk did to the federal government — and to the world. The chainsaw Musk brandished onstage at CPAC was meant to represent his now-defunct Department of Government Efficiency, which he unofficially led as a government employee under Trump. Musk’s views weren’t an anomaly in DOGE. Marko Elez, a former DOGE staffer — who followed Musk there from SpaceX — resigned after reporters uncovered an old social media account where he urged the US to repeal the Civil Rights Act and reinstate a “eugenic immigration policy.” Elez was subsequently rehired by the Social Security Administration. Though it was never an actual government department, DOGE gutted the federal workforce, infiltrated the Treasury Department and the Social Security Administration, and dismantled USAID, resulting in hundreds of thousands of deaths around the world.
Shortly after Trump’s return to office, Musk called USAID — which, until he and his DOGE staffers destroyed it, provided people across the developing world with lifesaving food and medical aid — a “criminal organization.” Earlier this year, amid mass opposition to ICE’s Operation Metro Surge in Minneapolis — during which federal agents killed two US citizens — Musk defended DHS against its critics. “It is pure evil for people to stop the arrest of child predators,” he wrote on X.
Musk is a creature of the internet, and as such he’s left a pretty extensive paper trail of his beliefs. As recently as October, he was endorsing a reposted screenshot of a 4chan post arguing that women shouldn’t participate in democracy because they “can’t defend themselves physically,” saying it “sounds correct.” Charles Cornish-Dale, the pseudonymous X user behind the post, who goes by Raw Egg Nationalist, is a far-right British bodybuilder who edits the magazine Man’s World.
Given the on-again, off-again nature of their relationship, it’s surprising that Musk’s comments about MAGA were so well received by Trump.
“Oh, I don’t know. He’s a friend of mine. What can I tell you? I mean, you have to ask — there are lots of definitions out there,” Trump told reporters. “MAGA, to me, means something very specific, and when people veer from it, they don’t do very well. But Elon is — Elon’s got very good intentions. I mean, he was with me the whole — he traveled with me.”
The public has broadly rejected the chaos wrought by MAGA 2.0, and Musk is trying to distance himself from the fallout. More cynically, he’s probably also trying to prevent Democrats in Congress and in Texas from investigating him come 2027. Whatever his motivation, Musk knows the winds have shifted, though his pivot is even less convincing than Alex Karp’s. Though, to be fair, there is earlier video evidence of Musk saying he isn’t MAGA.
“I’m not just MAGA,” he said at a 2024 Trump rally in Madison Square Garden. “I’m dark, gothic MAGA.”
Update, November 19th: This post has been updated to include Trump’s response to Musk’s comments.
